What we decided

Doing it

Backing

Consolidated from two independently written plans — the engineering delivery pipeline and the quality engineering culture. Neither is complete alone.

Start here

How we build software here

A proposal for a greenfield engineering team where AI writes most of the code and people stay accountable for it. This page explains the whole thing in plain English; the other eight go deep on one part each.

The whole document, in ninety secondsEverything: ~4 hours · this box: 90 seconds

  • The problem. AI makes engineers faster at writing code and worse at everything around it — duplication up 81%, refactoring down 70%, roughly a quarter of generated code carrying a security flaw. One controlled trial found experienced developers 19% slower while believing they were faster.
  • The response. Not "use AI carefully" — a delivery process with the failures designed out. Eleven stages from request to live, of which nine are automated and two need a person.
  • The rule that decides what's automated. If undoing a step is cheap, a machine does it. If undoing it is expensive, a person approves it. Not a judgment about trusting AI — a judgment about what a mistake costs.
  • Where people stay. Deciding what to build, and confirming the finished change fits the system. Both require judgment a machine can't supply, and both are cheap compared with what they prevent.
  • What stops the code rotting. Before anything is written, a step asks "does this already exist?" and blocks if it does. Every rule — types, tests, accessibility, comments — is enforced by a machine on every change, not remembered by a reviewer. Because the codebase is two languages, every one of those rules is wired twice, and a check makes sure both halves actually have it.
  • What it costs. About $410–545 per engineer per month for the entire toolchain — roughly 4–7% of a loaded engineer, depending where in the salary band they sit. Almost everything in the stack is free and open source; the bill is Azure and Claude usage.
  • The honest risk. The best-documented failure in this field is a company that measured a real productivity gain and cancelled anyway, because nobody could defend the bill. Cost tracking is in from week one for that reason.

If you read nothing else, read that. Everything below is the working out.

What changed in v1.1 — read this if you read v1.0

The platform is settled, and it changed the answer to one of the fifteen decisions. Azure, C# on the back end, React on the front. v1.0 recommended one language across browser and server and was written to that; the reasoning is preserved on Decisions, because the gap between the two answers is exactly what the C# choice costs — and those costs are now named as three things we have to build deliberately rather than left implied.

Four contradictions were resolved rather than left standing. Whether Lane 1 can auto-merge while mandatory review is an invariant (it can, via one named bypass, and the cost is stated). Where a Member actually lives, now that FHIR, a database and a domain model all claimed to define it. Why the per-feature cost cap could not be true as specified. And how the review rules work at eight engineers rather than thirty.

One threat class was missing entirely and has been added: prompt injection. The pipeline reads issue bodies and diffs and holds a token that can write code. That is a property of the shape of the design, not a bug a security scanner finds.

Several code snippets did not run. The ESLint config banned the TODO format the document recommends, a placeholder rule would have failed every file, and a regex was wrapped across lines in a way that is a syntax error. Fixed, and flagged, because "buildable rather than a diagram" is the claim this document makes about itself.

Numbers corrected: the toolchain is 4–7% of a loaded engineer, not "under 6%" (v1.0 divided dollars by pounds); AI spend is two to five contractor days a month, not one; and every price is now quoted at list rather than at an introductory rate that expires on 31 August 2026.

Plain English

Why it's built this way

The box above says what we're proposing. This says why, without assuming you write software.

Start with the thing that surprises people. The evidence on AI and software is not the story either camp tells. It doesn't say AI makes engineers faster, and it doesn't say AI is a fad. It says something more awkward: AI reliably increases how much code gets written, and reliably decreases how well that code holds together. Both effects are real, both are measured, and they pull in opposite directions.

You can see it in the same organisation at the same time. Microsoft measured its engineers merging 24% more work. Separately, telemetry from over ten thousand developers found 98% more proposed changes, 91% longer to review them, and no change at all in what actually reached customers. Twice the work going in, the same value coming out — because the extra volume landed on the one part of the process that didn't get faster: a human reading it.

The analogy that makes the rest of this document obvious

Imagine hiring twenty junior engineers overnight. They're fast, they never tire, and they've read everything. They also have no memory of last week, no knowledge of why your system is the way it is, and a tendency to write a new function rather than look for the one that already exists.

You wouldn't respond by telling them to be careful. You'd change the process around them: write requirements down properly, check work before it merges, keep a catalogue of what exists, and make sure nothing they do is expensive to undo.

That's this entire document. Not "use AI carefully" — build the process that makes twenty tireless juniors safe.

Why a rule about undoing, rather than a rule about trust. The obvious way to decide what AI is allowed to do alone is to ask how much you trust it. That question has no answer, so it gets settled by whoever is most senior in the room and re-litigated every time something goes wrong.

So we ask a different question: if this step goes wrong, how expensive is it to take back? Writing a document is free to undo. Releasing to customers is not — unless you've built the ability to reverse a release in seconds, at which point it is. That reframes "how much do we trust AI?" into "how good is our undo?", which is a thing engineers can build and measure rather than argue about.

Where people stay, and why it's these two places. An AI can check code against a written requirement all day. It cannot tell you the requirement was wrong. So a person signs off what we're building. And at the other end, a person reads the finished change — not to catch bugs, the machines are better at that, but so that somebody at this company still understands how the system works in a year's time. That's the real risk in a heavily automated pipeline: not shipping something broken, but shipping something nobody can explain.

Six words that appear constantly, in plain English

  • Repository (or "repo") — the folder where all the code lives, with a complete history of every change ever made to it.
  • Pull request (or "PR") — a proposed batch of changes, parked for review. Nothing reaches customers until someone or something approves it. This is where review happens.
  • CI/CD — the robot that runs automatically on every proposed change: does it compile, do the tests pass, is it safe. Then, if it all passes, it releases it.
  • Agent — an AI given a goal, a set of tools and permission to work in a loop until it's done, rather than answering one question at a time.
  • Spec — the written statement of what should happen, agreed before any code is written. In this design it's written in a strict format so it can be turned into tests automatically.
  • Lane — how risky a change is, from 1 (a wording tweak) to 4 (anything touching member data or clinical logic). The lane decides how much checking a change gets.
