Orientation

How this document is organised

Nine sections, top to bottom in reading order. Nobody needs to read all of it — each opens with a one-minute summary, and every section carries its own reading time.

If you have one meeting and nothing is set up: read The plan. It is twelve minutes and it is the whole thing — what gets decided in week one, what may never be switched off, and what order everything else happens in.

The nine sections, in reading order — each answers one question
SectionAnswersLengthRead it when
1 · Start hereWhat is this, and what do the words mean?40 minYou're new to it, briefing someone non-technical, or hit an unfamiliar term
2 · The planWhat do we actually do, and what's non-negotiable?13 minYou have one meeting and nothing is set up yet
3 · DecisionsWhat are we choosing, and why that over the alternatives?45 minYou need to make a call, or defend one
4 · How work flowsHow does a request become live software?22 minYou're designing the process, or explaining it
5 · Code standardsWhat does good code look like, what test proves it, and what stops it slipping?34 minYou're setting the engineering bar, or asking "what test do I write for this?"
6 · How we knowHow do we know any of it is working?24 minYou're wiring telemetry, defining a metric, or checking review hasn't decayed
7 · What it costsWhat's the bill, and how do we keep it defensible?19 minSomeone asks what this costs, or spend starts drifting
8 · Build planWhat do we do, in what order?44 minYou're actually implementing it
9 · Why we believe itWhy should anyone believe any of this?18 minA claim gets challenged and you need the citation
Every option carries its own evidence

On Decisions, each option has a "Why this rating?" button. It expands the evidence behind that specific option — the findings with numbers, the evidence strength, and a line on the decisive difference versus the alternative — plus a link through to the fuller research.

Losing options get panels too, and they say when that option would be right. Several are close calls rather than bad ideas, and a couple flip on inputs you don't have yet.

On the scores in Recommendations

Percentages there are a weighted rubric I compute from stated criteria, not measurements of real-world outcomes. The criteria and weights are shown for every category so you can disagree with the weighting and recompute.

Separately, each recommendation carries an evidence strength label — Strong means published data directly supports it, Moderate means indirect or partial support, Judgment means it's reasoning from principles with no direct study behind it. Don't conflate a high score with strong evidence; they're different things.

Terms · Claude

The Claude Code extension model

Six mechanisms that do different jobs. Most confusion in this space comes from picking the wrong one for a given problem.

Claude Codethe CLI / desktop agent
The agentic coding tool. Reads files, runs commands, edits code, and works through problems autonomously rather than answering one question at a time. For us: the thing developers actually use daily. Distinct from the API, which is what our product would call.
Claude Agent SDK
The library for building your own agents on top of Claude — the same loop Claude Code uses, exposed for you to embed in products. For us: this is what Layer 2 product AI would be built with. Not part of the developer tooling decision.
CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.mdinstruction files
Markdown loaded into context at the start of every session. Project conventions, commands, gotchas. AGENTS.md is the cross-vendor standard read by 30+ tools; CLAUDE.md is Claude Code's own filename. For us: advisory, not binding — and it degrades as it grows. Keep it short; a bloated file causes rules inside it to be ignored.
Skill
A folder with a SKILL.md containing domain knowledge or a repeatable workflow. Loaded on demand when relevant, so it costs nothing when unused. Invoked as /name when marked non-model-invocable. For us: where FHIR conventions, test rules and the /spec workflow live. The main defence against instruction-file bloat.
Subagent
A separate Claude instance with its own context window, its own system prompt, and a restricted tool list. Returns a summary to the caller. For us: two real uses — keeping wide codebase reads out of the main context, and reviewing a diff without having seen the reasoning that produced it.
Hook
A script that runs deterministically at a defined lifecycle point — before a tool call, after an edit, when a turn tries to end. Can block the action outright. For us: the only mechanism that actually guarantees anything. Everything compliance-relevant belongs here rather than in prose.
MCP serverModel Context Protocol
A standard way to connect an agent to external systems — databases, issue trackers, APIs. For us: note that third-party MCP data flows sit outside Anthropic's BAA. Any MCP we run must not carry member data.
Plugin & marketplace
A plugin bundles skills, hooks, subagents and MCP servers into one installable unit. A marketplace is a git repo cataloguing plugins, which an organisation can distribute centrally. For us: the only practical way to get standards into many repos without copy-paste. Becomes essential the moment we're polyrepo.
Managed settings
Organisation policy deployed by MDM (registry on Windows, plist on macOS) that users cannot override. Sits above every other settings layer. For us: ring three. Needed eventually, not needed at ten people, and reads as distrust if deployed too early.
Plan mode
A permission mode where Claude reads and reasons but cannot modify anything, used to agree an approach before implementation starts. For us: the cheapest quality intervention available. Standard for Lane 2 and above.
Headless modenon-interactive, claude -p
Running the agent without a terminal UI, output streamed for scripts to consume. How agents run inside CI. For us: the basis of the autonomous pipeline and of cross-repo campaigns.
Worktree
A git feature giving multiple working directories from one repository, so several agent sessions can run in parallel without colliding. For us: deliberately deferred. It multiplies input to the review bottleneck, which is the thing already constraining us.
Prompt caching
Reusing the unchanged prefix of a prompt across calls instead of reprocessing it. Cuts input token cost dramatically. For us: the single largest lever on spend. Works only if the stable part of the context stays stable — so instruction files and loaded skills shouldn't churn mid-session.
Progressive disclosure
Loading information in tiers — names and descriptions first, full instructions only when relevant, reference material only during execution. For us: the reason skills beat putting everything in the instruction file.
Terms · Method

Ways of structuring agentic work

These are the approaches people mean when they say "how should we work with agents". They are not equivalent, and the differences are mostly about cost per change.

Ad hoc prompting"vibe coding"
Describe what you want, the agent produces code, you review it. No written spec, no plan artefact. For us: correct for prototypes and cosmetic changes. The independent code-quality decline data is largely measuring this at scale.
TODO-list / task-list driven
The agent maintains a checklist of tasks and works through it, ticking items off. Often built into the tool. Structure without a specification — it organises execution, not intent. For us: useful inside a task, insufficient as the whole method. A task list derived from nothing still encodes whatever the agent assumed you meant.
Spec-driven developmentSDD
A versioned specification is the source of truth. Write the spec, derive a plan, break it into tasks, then generate code. The spec is reviewed by humans; the code largely isn't written by them. For us: the right approach for sensitive lanes, and the spec chain doubles as an audit artefact for health-plan security reviews.
EARSEasy Approach to Requirements Syntax
A constrained grammar for requirements, developed at Rolls-Royce and standard in aerospace and medical devices. Five templates forcing you to name trigger, condition, system and response. For us: the grammar makes vagueness ungrammatical — you cannot write "handles errors gracefully". That precision is what an agent needs to implement your intent rather than its guess.
Gherkin / BDDGiven-When-Then
Scenario syntax describing behaviour in domain language, readable by non-engineers and executable as tests. For us: the contract between product and engineering. Product approves the Gherkin; that's their gate. Engineering owns the EARS requirements.
Risk lanes
Classifying changes by blast radius and applying process proportional to it, routed by file path rather than author claim. For us: lets us spend scarce review capacity where it matters instead of uniformly.
Spec Kit / OpenSpec / BMAD / Kiro
Competing SDD toolkits. Spec Kit is GitHub's, agent-agnostic across 30+ tools. OpenSpec is minimal and delta-based. BMAD simulates a 12-role agile team. Kiro is an AWS IDE with SDD built in. For us: compared and scored in Recommendations. Short version — take the templates, not the runtime dependency.
ADRArchitecture Decision Record
A short document capturing one architectural decision, its context, and its consequences, committed alongside the code. For us: where architecture forum outcomes get recorded so the reasoning survives the people who made it.
Deep dive

EARS vs Gherkin — what each actually is

These get mentioned together constantly and they are not alternatives. They answer different questions, are written by different people, and we use both. Here's the difference from a standing start.

The two formats at a glance
EARSGherkin
Full nameEasy Approach to Requirements SyntaxNot an acronym. It's the syntax of BDD — Behaviour-Driven Development. Named after the Cucumber tool that popularised it.
OriginRolls-Royce, ~2009. Standard in aerospace, automotive and medical devices.Cucumber / the agile testing community, mid-2000s.
Answers"What must the system always do?""How do we know it worked, in a specific case?"
NatureA rule. Universal, applies to every instance.An example. One concrete scenario with concrete data.
Written byEngineering (tech lead), from the agent interviewAlso drafted by the agent, but Product approves it
Read byEngineers and the implementing agentProduct, QA, engineers — deliberately non-technical language
BecomesTraceable requirement IDs (R1, R2…) in commits and test namesThe actual automated tests
GrammarFive fixed templates. Rigid by design.Given / When / Then. Loose — prose inside a light structure.
Testable directly?No — it states a rule, not a caseYes — maps straight to a test
Catches vagueness?Yes — the grammar makes it impossible to be vaguePartly — you can still write a woolly scenario

The same requirement, written both ways

Take one behaviour: what happens when a discharge summary can't be parsed. Here it is as a rule, and as an example.

EARS — the ruleEngineering owns 
R2  If the discharge summary cannot be
    parsed, then the intake service shall
    queue the record for manual review and
    emit an intake.parse_failed event.

One sentence. Covers every unparseable summary, forever. Names the trigger condition, the system, and both required responses. An engineer reading this knows exactly what to build.

Gherkin — the exampleProduct approves 
Scenario: Unparseable discharge summary
  Given a discharge ADT message with a
        malformed summary
  When  the intake service processes it
  Then  the record is queued for manual review
  And   an intake.parse_failed event is emitted
  And   no CarePlan draft is created

One concrete case. A product person can read this and say "yes, that's what should happen" — or "no, we also need to notify the care coordinator", which is how you catch a missing requirement before anything is built.

Notice what the Gherkin caught

The scenario includes a line the EARS requirement doesn't: And no CarePlan draft is created. Writing the concrete example surfaced a negative case nobody had stated. That's the mechanism — rules describe intent, examples expose gaps in it. In practice this loops: the missing case gets added back as R3, and the scenario stays as its test.

The five EARS templates

This is the whole grammar. If a requirement won't fit one of these, it isn't clear enough yet — which is exactly the signal you want, and why /spec refuses to paraphrase them.

EARS patterns, with worked examples
PatternTemplateUse whenExample
UbiquitousThe <system> shall <response>.Always true, no triggerThe intake service shall record an audit entry for every message received.
Event-drivenWhen <trigger>, the <system> shall <response>.Something happensWhen a discharge ADT message is received, the intake service shall create a CarePlan draft within 60 seconds.
State-drivenWhile <state>, the <system> shall <response>.True for a durationWhile a CarePlan draft is unconfirmed, the intake service shall not trigger any downstream fulfilment.
UnwantedIf <condition>, then the <system> shall <response>.Error and failure handlingIf the discharge summary cannot be parsed, then the intake service shall queue the record for manual review.
OptionalWhere <feature>, the <system> shall <response>.Only in some configurationsWhere the member has no active benefit, the intake service shall mark the CarePlan ineligible.

Why the rigidity is the point

You physically cannot write "the system should handle errors gracefully" in EARS. There's no template for it. The grammar forces you to answer: which condition, which system, what response.

That matters more with agents than with people. A human engineer reading "handle errors gracefully" asks you what you meant. An agent implements its best guess, confidently, and you find out in review — or in production. Specification and design issues account for 41.8% of observed multi-agent failures, and vague requirements are the largest single contributor.

Gherkin structure

The keywords and what each is for
KeywordMeansCommon mistake
ScenarioA name for this one caseNaming it after the code path instead of the behaviour
GivenThe starting state. What's already true before anything happens.Putting actions here — Given is setup, not doing
WhenThe single action or event under testMore than one When. If you need two, it's two scenarios.
ThenThe observable outcomeAsserting internal state instead of observable behaviour
And / ButContinues the previous keywordChaining so many that the scenario tests three things at once
BackgroundShared Given steps for every scenario in a fileHiding so much setup that scenarios become unreadable alone
The rule that keeps Gherkin useful

Write it in domain language, never technical language. "Given a discharge ADT message with a malformed summary" is good. "Given a POST to /v1/intake returns 422" is not — Product can't evaluate that, and the moment they can't, the approval gate becomes a rubber stamp and you've lost the whole benefit.

Why not just pick one?

EARS alone
  • You have precise rules but no tests
  • Product can't meaningfully review it — the syntax is engineering-facing
  • Nothing forces you to think through concrete cases, so gaps survive
Gherkin alone
  • You have tests but no statement of the general rule
  • Easy to write five scenarios that don't cover the actual requirement
  • No traceable requirement IDs, so no audit chain from need to code

How we use them together

EARS sets the rule. Gherkin verifies the result. The agent drafts both from the same interview. The tech lead approves the EARS requirements; Product approves the Gherkin scenarios. Every "unwanted behaviour" requirement must have at least one scenario.

The requirement IDs then travel: R2 appears in the EARS statement, the scenario name, the test name (test('[R2] queues unparseable summary for review')), the task list, and the commit message. That chain is traceability for free — and it answers a large chunk of any health-plan customer's security questionnaire without extra work.

Terms · Delivery

Repositories, contracts and deployment

Monorepo
One repository holding many services and applications. Not the same as a monolith — services still deploy independently, triggered by path-filtered CI. For us: the confusion between these two words is the single most common cause of accidentally choosing polyrepo.
Polyrepomulti-repo
One repository per service or application. For us: hard team autonomy, at the cost of agents losing whole-system visibility and cross-cutting changes becoming expensive.
Modular monolith
A single deployable application with strictly enforced internal module boundaries, so extraction into services later is bounded work. For us: the shape most 2026 guidance recommends up to roughly 50 engineers.
CODEOWNERS
A file mapping paths to the teams that must review changes to them. Enforced by the platform. For us: how risk lanes become unfakeable — the lane follows the path you touched, not what you claim.
ContractOpenAPI, event schema, FHIR profile
The machine-readable interface between two services, versioned independently of either. For us: in a monorepo the compiler catches cross-service breakage. In a polyrepo nothing does unless we build this.
Generated client
A typed SDK produced automatically from a contract and installed as a package dependency. For us: the highest-value cross-repo context mechanism. An agent in one service can't read another, but it can read types that are true by construction.
Consumer-driven contract testingPact
Each consumer declares what it depends on; the provider's pipeline fails if a change breaks any declared expectation. For us: this is what replaces the compiler in a polyrepo. Catches breakage the provider's own tests never exercise.
Expand/contractparallel change
Making a breaking change safely: add the new shape alongside the old, migrate consumers one at a time, remove the old shape when nothing references it. For us: removes the need for coordinated deploys, which in turn removes the need for coordinated rollbacks.
Progressive deliverycanary release
Releasing to a small slice of traffic, watching guardrail metrics, and expanding only when the data says it's safe. For us: this is what buys autonomous deployment. It converts an irreversible step into a reversible one.
SLO & auto-rollback
A Service Level Objective is a measurable reliability target. Auto-rollback watches it during a canary and reverts traffic within seconds when it's breached. For us: this is what converts deployment from a human gate into an automated stage. Reversal in seconds versus a person being paged at 3am is a difference in kind — which is the argument, rather than any particular percentage.
Feature flag
A runtime switch decoupling deployment from release — code ships dark, exposure is a separate decision. For us: the second layer of reversibility, and it works even when rollback would be slow.
Terms · Measurement & safety

Metrics, bounds and compliance

DORA metrics
Four measures of delivery performance: deployment frequency, lead time, change failure rate, and time to restore. Throughput and stability, in pairs. For us: the pairing matters. AI adoption reliably raises throughput while degrading stability, so reporting one without the other hides the cost.
Code churntwo-week churn
Lines reverted or rewritten within a fortnight of being merged. For us: the highest-value single signal. Cheap to compute from git, and it captures the specific failure of agentic development — code accepted, merged, then rewritten because it was never right.
Escape rate
Defects reaching a human reviewer (or production) that automated verification should have caught. For us: the promotion criterion for raising a lane's autonomy level.
Delegation gap
The distance between how much work AI touches (~60%) and how much can be fully handed over (0–20%). For us: the honest ceiling on unattended work — though it measures delegation without a harness, and a pipeline is precisely that harness.
The four bounds
Every automated agent loop needs an iteration cap, a budget cap, a success function the agent can evaluate itself, and an escalation path. Missing any one produces a runaway. For us: non-negotiable on every pipeline stage. Bounded loops have been measured using 90% fewer tokens with 525× less run-to-run variance than unbounded ones — and that variance is the point. An unbounded loop isn't just expensive, it's unbudgetable.
Success function
A check the agent can run and read the result of — a test suite, a build exit code, a screenshot diff. For us: without one, an agent stops when work looks done and you become the verification loop. A stage with no success function must be a human gate.
BAABusiness Associate Agreement
The HIPAA contract letting a vendor process protected health information on your behalf. For us: Claude Code is covered only on CLI and Desktop-local, and only with Zero Data Retention on. MCP data flows are excluded.
ZDRZero Data Retention
A configuration where prompts and responses aren't retained after the request completes. For us: required for Claude Code BAA coverage, and mutually exclusive with the Covered Models programme.
PHIProtected Health Information
Health data identifying an individual, regulated under HIPAA. For us: should never enter a development environment. Synthetic data only, enforced by hook and CI scan.
The one sentence that connects all of it

Move standards out of prose and into code. A rule in an instruction file costs tokens every session, is advisory, and degrades as the file grows. The same rule as a hook or CI check costs nothing, is binding, and never degrades. Almost every recommendation in the next section is an application of that.

Terms · Healthcare

Healthcare data standards

These appear throughout the examples. If you've not worked in health tech before, this is the minimum vocabulary.

HL7Health Level Seven
The standards body, and by extension its family of healthcare messaging standards. HL7v2 is the old pipe-delimited format still running most hospital integrations; FHIR is the modern one. For us: you will meet both. v2 for real-time hospital events, FHIR for everything we build ourselves.
FHIRFast Healthcare Interoperability Resources — pronounced "fire"
The modern HL7 standard. Defines healthcare concepts as resources — Patient, CarePlan, Encounter, Coverage — with a REST API and JSON representation. R4 is the version in wide production use. For us: our canonical internal data model, not just an external API veneer. Two payoffs — regulatory alignment, and agents reason far better over FHIR resources than over a proprietary schema, because the model is public and well-documented.
FHIR storewhere the resources actually live
You need a server that speaks FHIR, not just a database with FHIR-shaped JSON. Our answer is the Azure Health Data Services FHIR service — managed, HIPAA and HITRUST covered, and on the cloud we are already on. The alternatives, for completeness: Medplum (open source, TypeScript-native) or Microsoft's own open-source FHIR server if we ever wanted to self-host, and AWS HealthLake on the other cloud. Watch the date: the older Azure API for FHIR retires 30 September 2026 — any tutorial or template referencing it points at a dead end. If Azure, build on Health Data Services from the start.
ADTAdmit, Discharge, Transfer
The HL7v2 message type hospitals emit when a patient's status changes. A01 is admit, A03 is discharge, A08 is an update. For us: the discharge message is the trigger event for the whole product. Getting real-time ADT feeds is a business-development problem wearing an engineering costume — sourced via health information exchanges, not built.
CarePlan
A FHIR resource describing intended care for a patient — activities, goals, who's responsible. For us: the central object. Ours is always a draft until a human confirms it, and nothing downstream fires from a draft.
HIPAAHealth Insurance Portability and Accountability Act
The US law governing protected health information. Its Privacy Rule covers use and disclosure; its Security Rule covers technical safeguards. For us: as a Business Associate we're bound by the Security Rule directly, and by whatever our covered-entity customers flow down contractually.
Covered Entity / Business Associate
Under HIPAA, a covered entity is a health plan, provider or clearinghouse. A business associate is a vendor handling PHI on their behalf. For us: almost certainly a Business Associate — much lighter regulatory load than a health plan, but we inherit our customers' audit requirements.
MBIMedicare Beneficiary Identifier
The 11-character identifier that replaced SSN-based Medicare numbers. Fixed alternating letter/digit pattern. For us: the pattern our data-policy hook scans for. Predictable format makes it detectable, which is why it's a good canary for real member data leaking into a repo.
SSBCISpecial Supplemental Benefits for the Chronically Ill
The Medicare Advantage category that permits non-medical benefits — transport, meals, in-home support — for members with qualifying chronic conditions. For us: likely the regulatory category the product is funded under. Worth confirming, because it determines who the paying customer is.
Terms · Infrastructure

Platform, identity and security acronyms

SSOSingle Sign-On
Authenticating once with a central identity provider instead of per-application credentials. Implemented with SAML or OIDC. For us: wire it before the first seat is issued. Retrofitting identity is miserable.
SCIMSystem for Cross-domain Identity Management
A standard for automatically provisioning and de-provisioning user accounts from your identity provider. SSO handles login; SCIM handles account lifecycle. For us: Enterprise-only. Not needed until manual joiner/leaver becomes painful — but a leaver who keeps access is exactly the finding a customer audit looks for.
MDMMobile Device Management
Centralised management of employee machines — Intune or Group Policy on Windows, Jamf or Kandji on macOS. Pushes configuration users can't override. For us: the delivery mechanism for managed settings, which is why ring-three enforcement is gated on having an MDM estate. We don't yet.
OpenTelemetryOTel; OTLP is its wire protocol
A vendor-neutral standard for emitting metrics, logs and traces. Claude Code exports both metrics and events over it. For us: how we get per-agent cost and rejection rates. Set the endpoint from day one — history can't be generated retroactively.
OpenAPIformerly Swagger
A machine-readable specification format for REST APIs. Generates typed clients, server stubs and documentation. For us: what lives in the contracts repo. The generated client is the thing that lets an agent in one service know the truth about another it can't read.
Pactconsumer-driven contract testing
A tool implementing CDC testing. Each consumer records the interactions it depends on; the provider's pipeline replays them and fails if any break. For us: catches breakage the provider's own tests never exercise — the exact class of failure that becomes a production incident in a polyrepo.
OWASPOpen Worldwide Application Security Project
A non-profit publishing security guidance. Its Top 10 is the standard reference list of web application risk categories. For us: the benchmark the AI-generated-code vulnerability studies test against, and a reasonable checklist for the security-reviewer agent.
SSRFServer-Side Request Forgery
Tricking a server into making a request the attacker chooses — typically to internal services the attacker can't reach directly, like cloud metadata endpoints. For us: the single most common vulnerability class in AI-generated code, which is why the security agent is told to weight it. Any outbound request built from caller-controlled input is suspect.
SLO / SLI / SLA
An SLI is a measured indicator (error rate, latency). An SLO is the internal target for it. An SLA is the contractual promise to a customer, with penalties. For us: SLOs are what the canary watches. You need them defined and trusted before deploy can run unattended.
Canary release
Routing a small percentage of live traffic to a new version, watching guardrail metrics, and expanding only if they hold. For us: combined with SLO auto-rollback, this is what converts deployment from irreversible to reversible — and therefore from gated to automated.
WSLWindows Subsystem for Linux
Runs a Linux environment inside Windows. For us: the reason hooks are written in Node rather than bash. A UK team on Windows shouldn't need WSL for a hook to fire.
RCTRandomised Controlled Trial
A study design where participants are randomly assigned to conditions, isolating cause from correlation. For us: why the METR study carries disproportionate weight despite its small sample — it's the only causal evidence in the field. Everything else is correlational or telemetry.
MASTMulti-Agent System failure Taxonomy
A Berkeley-produced classification of 14 ways multi-agent LLM systems fail, derived from 1,600+ annotated execution traces across 7 frameworks. For us: the evidence base for building agents around boundaries rather than job titles, and for treating verification as the thing worth investing in.
CNCFCloud Native Computing Foundation
The foundation stewarding Kubernetes and much of the cloud-native ecosystem; publishes widely-cited industry surveys. For us: source of the finding that 42% of microservices adopters are consolidating services back together.
Terms · The toolchain

Every product named in this document

The Code standards and How work flows tabs name specific tools rather than categories, because "use a CI system" isn't buildable. Here's what each one is, in a sentence.

Language and framework

TypeScript
JavaScript with a type system bolted on, checked before the code runs. Catches whole classes of mistake at build time rather than in production. For us: the front-end language, and the language of the generated API client. Not the back end — see C# and .NET below. Its type system has to be configured to be strict; C#'s is strict by default, which is most of why the split landed where it did.
Nx
A monorepo build tool for JavaScript and TypeScript. Knows which parts of the codebase depend on which, so it only rebuilds and retests what a change actually affected — and can enforce rules about which module may import which. For us: two jobs, both limited to the TypeScript half of the repository. It keeps CI fast, and its module boundary rules mechanically stop a UI package importing from somewhere it shouldn't. The C# half gets the same guarantees from NetArchTest instead — two tools, one rule, which is the recurring shape of this stack.
C# and .NETASP.NET Core is the web framework
Microsoft's main server-side language and runtime. C# is the language, .NET the runtime and library it compiles against, and ASP.NET Core the framework for building web services on it. Free and open source; cross-platform since 2016 despite the Microsoft association. For us: the back end, and the reason is the compiler rather than the ecosystem. C# rejects a class of ambiguity by default that TypeScript only rejects once it has been configured to — and when agents write most of the code, a language that refuses to guess is doing review work for us. Its rigid conventions also give an agent an obvious place to put things, which reduces the "invent a new pattern" failure mode.
Nullable reference typesand warnings as errors
A C# compiler mode where a variable that might be null must say so in its type, and the compiler complains when you use one without checking. Paired with treating every compiler warning as a build failure. For us: this is the row that earns the language decision. It converts a whole family of runtime crashes into build failures, before a test runs, with no configuration discipline required from anyone. Set it solution-wide in Directory.Build.props so no project can quietly opt out — and if it is ever switched off, we paid for the two-language split and got nothing back.
Entity Framework CoreEF Core
The standard way C# talks to a relational database, with the table structure reflected in the types and schema changes expressed as reviewable migration files. For us: a mistyped column fails the build instead of failing at 2am, and migrations arrive as diffs a human can review and a CI check can scan for destructive operations.
React & Vite
React builds user interfaces from reusable components. Vite is the tool that assembles and serves them during development. For us: React's component model is the mechanism that makes "one button, reused" enforceable rather than aspirational.
shadcn/ui & Radix
Radix supplies unstyled, fully accessible interface primitives — dropdowns, dialogs, tooltips that work correctly with keyboards and screen readers. shadcn/ui is a set of styled components built on Radix that you copy into your own repository rather than install as a dependency. For us: copied-in means we own and can change them, so they become the base of our own component library instead of a vendor's. Accessibility arrives correct by default, which matters for a healthcare product.
Design tokensheld as W3C DTCG JSON
The named values that define a brand — colours, spacing, type scale, radii, shadows — stored as data rather than as code. DTCG is the Design Tokens Community Group format: a W3C-backed JSON standard that design tools and build tools both understand. For us: the tokens are the only hand-edited definition of the brand, and the Tailwind theme is generated from them. Writing a theme alongside a token file gives two definitions of one colour, and they drift the first time somebody nudges a hex because a screen looked slightly off. DTCG is also platform-neutral, so the same source would feed a React Native theme if that ever happens.
Style Dictionaryor any token build tool
Reads token files and emits platform-specific outputs — a Tailwind theme, CSS custom properties, native constants — from one source. For us: free and open source, and the piece that makes "generated, not hand-written" actually true. CI fails if the generated output is stale.
Headless component primitivesRadix, React Aria, Ark UI
Components that supply behaviour with no styling — focus management, keyboard interaction, ARIA wiring, screen-reader semantics — leaving appearance entirely to you. All three are free and React-first. Radix is the most widely used and what shadcn/ui is built on; React Aria (Adobe) is the most exhaustively tested against assistive technology, with HeroUI giving it a shadcn-like developer experience; Ark UI is the newest and works across frameworks. For us: the accessibility behaviour is years of specialist work we take from upstream rather than maintain — and it is the specific thing to insist on if an external firm builds our component library. Note what they don't cover: colour contrast comes from the brand palette, not the primitive, so it is gated separately over the token set.
Zod and FluentValidationthe same job, one per language
Both describe the shape data must have and reject anything that doesn't match, at runtime. Zod does it in TypeScript and hands the type checker the same shape for free; FluentValidation does it in C# with the rules surfaced into the OpenAPI schema. For us: the boundary guard, and a good illustration of what two languages costs. Anything arriving from outside — an API call, a form, a queue message — is validated before it is trusted, because that is where type safety otherwise quietly stops being true. Two tools for one rule is the pattern to expect across this whole stack, and the reason a parity check exists to make sure both are actually wired.
OpenAPI and the generated clientNSwag or Kiota does the generating
A machine-readable description of every endpoint an API exposes, produced automatically from the server code. A generated client is a typed library built from that description, so the browser code calls the API through types that are true by construction rather than by hand-written agreement. For us: the single most important consequence of writing the back end and front end in different languages. With one language, types simply flow. With two, this replaces that — and if the generated client is ever hand-edited or allowed to go stale, the boundary silently stops being type-safe and nothing tells you. CI fails on a stale client for exactly that reason.
Postgres
The database. Open source, and the default choice for relational data unless something specific argues otherwise. For us: Azure Database for PostgreSQL as the managed form. Chosen over Azure SQL for portability and cost rather than any technical shortfall — either would work.
Azure Health Data Servicesthe FHIR service
Microsoft's managed implementation of FHIR — the standard for exchanging health records — so we don't build or run that layer ourselves. HIPAA and HITRUST covered. For us: FHIR is large and easy to implement subtly wrong. On an Azure estate this is the clearest build-versus-buy call on the stack, and the Firely .NET SDK is what our C# talks to it through. Watch the date: the older Azure API for FHIR retires 30 September 2026, so any tutorial naming it points at a dead end.

Testing and code quality

xUnit and Vitestthe fast unit-test runners
Run the small, fast tests that check individual pieces of code in isolation. xUnit on the C# side, Vitest on the TypeScript side. For us: speed is the point — the agent runs these on a loop while it works, so slow tests directly slow the pipeline. Two runners is unavoidable with two languages, and it is a genuinely small cost: they are invoked by the same CI workflow and the agent never has to choose, because the file it is editing decides.
Playwright & playwright-bdd
Playwright drives a real browser to test the product the way a person would use it. playwright-bdd is the adapter that runs Gherkin .feature files as Playwright tests. For us: this is the link that makes the whole spec argument hold. The scenarios product signed off in stage 3 are executed literally, unchanged, as the tests that gate production in stage 10 — so the spec cannot silently drift away from the behaviour.
ESLint and Roslyn analysers
Scan code for rule violations — style, banned patterns, architectural rules — and fail the build on them. ESLint does this for TypeScript, Roslyn analysers for C#. Both support custom rules. For us: the two main enforcement surfaces, and the reason the standards register has two mechanism columns. Every rule that matters is expressed in both, and a CI job compares the two rule sets and fails when they diverge — because a rule wired on one side only is not a standard, it is a standard on half the codebase.
tsc --noEmit and dotnet build
Pure "does this compile?" gates — the TypeScript type checker with no output, and the C# compiler with warnings treated as errors. For us: the cheapest meaningful checks in the pipeline, so they run before anything expensive. Both must pass before a single model token is spent on review — there is no point paying Opus to read a diff that does not compile.
StorybookChromatic is its paid hosted add-on
Renders every component on its own page, in every state, so people can browse the library without running the product. For us: the human-facing half of the component catalogue — catalogue.json is the agent-facing half. Both are generated from the same code, so neither can drift from what actually ships. Free and self-hosted; Chromatic adds hosted visual-regression testing and is not needed yet.
jscpdJavaScript Copy-Paste Detector
Scans the codebase for blocks of duplicated code and fails the build above a set threshold. For us: the mechanical backstop behind the reuse stage. The reuse check catches duplication before code is written; this catches what slips through. Baseline it early so the trend line means something — duplicated blocks rose 81% across 623 million AI-assisted changes, so the number only helps if you have been watching it from the start.
knip
Finds code, files and dependencies nothing is using any more. For us: agents leave dead code behind when they change direction mid-task. This finds it automatically instead of it accumulating.
gitleaks
Scans changes for accidentally committed secrets — passwords, API keys, tokens. For us: paired with a scan for anything resembling real patient data. A secret committed once must be treated as compromised, so catching it before merge is the only cheap moment.
ts-morph and Roslynthe two catalogue walkers
Libraries for programmatically reading and analysing code — ts-morph for TypeScript, Roslyn for C# — effectively letting a script understand the codebase's structure rather than grep it. For us: together they generate catalogue.json, the machine-readable index of everything that already exists. Generated rather than hand-written, because a hand-written index goes stale within a fortnight and then actively misleads the agent. Both walkers must run. A catalogue covering only the TypeScript half would let the reuse gate pass changes that duplicate existing C#, while looking like it had checked.
oasdiff
Compares two versions of an API specification and reports whether the change would break existing callers. For us: makes "did this break another team?" an automated check rather than a conversation someone forgets to have.
Synthea
An open-source generator of realistic but entirely fictional patient records. For us: the starting corpus for the synthetic dataset. Without a convincing fake one, engineers eventually reach for real data — and no automated guard catches every route.

Deployment and operations

Docker & Kubernetes
Docker packages an application with everything it needs to run, so it behaves the same everywhere. Kubernetes runs and supervises large numbers of those packages across machines. For us: assumed background. Worth knowing only because the rollback tooling below is built on Kubernetes.
OpenTofuthe open fork of Terraform
Describes cloud infrastructure — servers, networks, databases — as code in the repository, so environments are reproducible and changes are reviewed like any other change. Terraform is the original; its licence changed to BUSL in 2023, and OpenTofu is the MPL-2.0 community fork, drop-in compatible. For us: puts infrastructure under the same review and audit trail as application code, which is most of what an auditor wants to see. We take OpenTofu — the licence is genuinely open and can't be changed under us, and there is no functional cost to the choice.
Sentryor any error-tracking service
Captures individual exceptions with their stack trace, the release that introduced them and the users affected. For us: a different job from Prometheus, and both are needed. Prometheus tells the canary that the error rate rose, which is enough to roll back; Sentry tells you why, which is what stops it happening again.
WCAG 2.1 AAWeb Content Accessibility Guidelines
The international standard for making web content usable by people with disabilities. AA is the conformance level regulators generally reference. For us: effectively a requirement rather than a nice-to-have — a Medicare aftercare population has materially higher rates of visual, motor and cognitive impairment, and US federal accessibility rules attach to federally funded health programmes. Enforced by eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y and axe-core assertions in the Playwright suite. See Section 1557 / Section 504 below for why 2.1 AA specifically.
Section 1557 & Section 504the US rules that make WCAG 2.1 AA a legal floor
Section 1557 of the Affordable Care Act and Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act prohibit disability discrimination in health programmes receiving federal financial assistance. Recent HHS rulemaking ties both to a named technical standard for web and mobile content: WCAG 2.1 Level AA. Section 508 is the separate rule covering technology federal agencies buy or build — relevant only if you sell to an agency directly. For us: this is why the document names 2.1 AA rather than the newer 2.2 or the stricter AAA — the regulations name 2.1 AA, so that's the conformance target with a defensible basis. Take a legal read on which of these actually binds us once the funding model is settled; the engineering answer is the same either way.
FedRAMPFederal Risk and Authorization Management Program
A standardised US government programme for security-assessing and authorising cloud services. Authorisation levels run Low / Moderate / High. For us: the compliance floor if the "government backing" ambition means becoming a federal vendor handling CMS data, rather than a private company whose product happens to save Medicare money. The two paths are not comparable in cost — worth resolving early.
Argo CD
Watches the repository and makes the running environment match what's described there. The repository is the source of truth; drift is corrected automatically. For us: "what's live" becomes a question with a documented answer, which is a precondition for automating release at all.
Argo Rollouts or Flagger
Releases a new version to a small slice of live traffic, watches the agreed health metrics, and expands or reverses automatically based on what it sees. For us: the single most important piece of infrastructure in the whole design. It's what converts deployment from irreversible to reversible — and therefore, by the governing rule, from a human gate into an automated step.
Prometheus & Grafana
Prometheus collects operational metrics — error rates, response times. Grafana displays them. For us: Prometheus is what the canary queries to decide whether to continue or roll back, so its data has to be trustworthy before deployment is automated. Grafana also hosts the cost dashboards.
OpenFeaturethe standard; flagd and LaunchDarkly are implementations
A vendor-neutral standard for feature flags — switches that turn a capability on or off without releasing new code. flagd is its free reference implementation, which you run yourself. LaunchDarkly is the leading commercial alternative, priced per seat and per usage. For us: separates "the code is live" from "customers can see it". That separation is what makes an unwanted behaviour an instant switch-off rather than an emergency release. We adopt the standard and start on flagd, so moving to LaunchDarkly later is a config change rather than a migration.
EMUGitHub Enterprise Managed Users
A GitHub Enterprise Cloud mode where every account is created and removed by your identity provider. Engineers get a managed account rather than using a personal GitHub login, and the enterprise controls which third-party apps may be installed. For us: this is what we run, so it's a constraint rather than an option. It gives the identity governance this plan wants for free — provisioning, de-provisioning, audit — and imposes three things to work around: no outside collaborators, which affects the design firm; apps needing enterprise approval, which affects the Claude Code integration; and Enterprise Cloud licensing rather than the cheaper Team tier.
CODEOWNERS
A file mapping parts of the codebase to the people who must approve changes to them. GitHub enforces it automatically. For us: how risk lanes are actually implemented. The lane follows the files a change touches, not what the author claims about it — which is the entire point.
MADRMarkdown Architectural Decision Records
A lightweight template for recording a decision, the options considered and why one won, stored in the repository alongside the code. For us: the format for the ADRs the design stage produces. It answers "why is it like this?" for whoever asks in a year, which in an agent-heavy codebase is the question that gets expensive.
Putting it together

One feature, end to end

Every concept above, applied to a single real change. This is what the process actually feels like from ask to production — with the tool running each step named, so it reads as something buildable rather than a diagram.

The ask: "When someone's discharged and their summary is unreadable, we're losing them. A coordinator should pick it up manually instead of it silently failing."

Stage numbers match the eleven stages on How work flows. Stage 5 (contract) doesn't appear because this change stays inside one service, which is the common case — most features touch fewer than eleven stages.

1 · TriageAgent · automated

Product files an issue and labels it ask:new. The pipeline fires. The agent searches existing specs for prior art, finds none, identifies Acme.Intake as the only affected service, and proposes Lane 3 — it touches member records, but not clinical decisioning. The harness then sets the lane from the paths actually touched, not from the agent's suggestion.

GitHub issue formGitHub Actions · claude-code-actionModel Haiku 4.5

→ issue comment + labels lane:3 size:M  ·  cost ~$0.50

2 · Reuse analysisAgent · automated

The agent reads the generated catalogue of everything that already exists and maps the ask onto it. Three of the four capabilities needed are already there — the notification banner, the coordinator queue view, the audit-log writer. Only the parse-failure detector is genuinely new, and it says so with a reason.

Without this stage the feature would have shipped with a fourth banner component. That is how codebases rot, one reasonable-looking decision at a time.

catalogue.jsonModel Sonnet 5Fails if "new" is unjustified

→ reuse.md: 3 reuse, 0 extend, 1 new  ·  cost ~$1

3 · SpecificationGate 1 · human approves

Someone runs /spec discharge-parse-failure. The agent interviews product and the tech lead: what counts as unparseable? how fast must a coordinator see it? what if the member has no active benefit? what should explicitly not happen?

It drafts EARS requirements and Gherkin scenarios. Product reads the Gherkin and catches something — the draft says the record is queued, but doesn't say a CarePlan isn't created. That negative case becomes R3.

Claude Code CLI, localModel Opus 5Gate: CODEOWNERS on specs/**

→ specs/discharge-parse-failure/spec.md, status: approved

4 · DesignAgent · human reviews

Agent produces a plan: files to change by path, the new queue interface, the event schema addition, and a requirement-coverage table mapping R1–R3 to specific changes. No contract change crosses a service boundary, so Gate 2 is skipped.

GitHub ActionsModel Opus 5Project graphADR in docs/adr/

→ plan.md with every R-number covered

6–7 · Decompose and buildAgent · bounded loop

Tasks generated, each tagged to a requirement and sized to one reviewable commit. The agent implements them in order. A Stop hook prevents the turn ending while tests fail, so it iterates against a real success function rather than stopping when work looks done.

The no-prod-data hook fires once when the agent tries to paste a realistic-looking MBI into a fixture. It's blocked, and it generates a synthetic one instead.

Matrix job per taskModel Sonnet 5Stop + PreToolUse hooksFast gate < 90s

→ draft PR, tests derived from the Gherkin  ·  cost ~$14

8 · VerificationAutomated fan-out

Mechanical checks first — lint, types, tests, PR size cap, data-policy scan. Then two reviewer subagents in parallel on the diff. code-reviewer reports no correctness issues. security-reviewer flags that the queue endpoint doesn't verify the caller may act on that member — a real authorisation gap, with an exploitation path.

That returns as a fix cycle. Second pass is clean. No human has looked at this yet, by design.

ESLint · tsc · Vitest · gitleaks · knipReviewer subagents on Opus 53 fix cycles max

→ green pipeline, one finding found and fixed  ·  cost ~$9

9 · Human reviewGate 3 · human approves

Lane 3, so two engineers from the owning team. They see a pre-reviewed diff with the spec attached, and review intent and architectural fit only — everything mechanical is already done. One asks whether the manual queue should be per-market rather than global. It shouldn't, and that's recorded in the PR rather than becoming scope creep.

GitHub PR reviewCODEOWNERS by lane/explain-diff before approvingNo model spend

→ approved, merged

10–11 · Staging, then productionAutomated · reversible

Staging deploy, Playwright runs the Gherkin scenarios end to end. Green. Production canary at 5% behind a feature flag, SLOs watched every 30 seconds. Error rate holds; traffic expands to 100% over 20 minutes.

Argo CD → stagingplaywright-bdd on the .feature filesArgo Rollouts canary 5%OpenFeature flag

→ live  ·  total run cost ~$25

After ship · Verify in productionAutomated

Synthetic checks run the same Gherkin scenarios against production on a schedule. If they'd failed, traffic reverts automatically and the issue reopens with evidence attached.

Playwright synthetics on a schedulePrometheus SLO checksAuto-revert on failure

→ closed

See every stage broken down by trigger, runtime, model and artifact →

What that trace demonstrates

  • Two human touchpoints, both judgment: is this the right behaviour, and does it fit the architecture. Everything else was automated or verified mechanically.
  • Product caught a missing requirement by reading Gherkin — before a line of code existed. That's the cheapest possible place to catch it.
  • The security finding came from a machine, not from a human noticing at 4pm on a Friday.
  • A hook stopped real-looking data entering the repo without anyone having to remember the rule.
  • R1–R3 travel all the way through — spec, task, test name, commit, PR. That chain is the audit trail, produced as a by-product rather than assembled afterwards.
  • ~$25 for the whole feature. Attributable to this change, not smeared across a seat licence.

The plan

Walk in, put this on the table, start

Nothing is set up. There is no repository, no pipeline, no team habits — only a component library being commissioned externally. This is what gets decided in the first week, what gets built in what order, and what may never be switched off.

This page consolidates two plans written independently — one from engineering, one from quality. They were not coordinated and they reached the same conclusions, which is the strongest reason to act on either.

The short versionFull tab: ~13 min · this box: 1 min

  • Seven things exist before the first commit. All are settings, schemas or a page of writing. None is a behaviour, so nobody has to be persuaded of anything — and each becomes materially more expensive the moment code exists without it.
  • Two of them cannot be recovered later at any price: recording AI authorship in the commit, and the telemetry contract that lets a defect be traced back to the change that caused it.
  • Five stages, started by events rather than dates. A company with no pipeline has no basis for a date, and triggers are checkable so nobody litigates whether we're behind. Only the first has a real deadline.
  • Mandate anything that costs an engineer nothing; earn everything else. That single rule resolves what looks like a contradiction between "change defaults, not minds" and "review is non-negotiable from day one".
  • Stages one and two are genuinely free. Stage three is where money appears. Saying that up front is the difference between a credible second ask and a reputation for under-quoting.
Confidence

What we actually know, and what this document is assuming

Read this before anything else on this tab. A plan that doesn't separate the two invites someone to challenge a settled decision because an unsettled one sitting next to it looked equally confident.

Three tiers of confidence
AreaStatusWhat that means for the plan
Platform and language Decided Azure, C# on the back end, React on the front end. Taken as a business decision rather than derived here. It is a defensible stack and this plan is written to it — but it gives up two things a single language would have provided for free, and those costs are named and mitigated below rather than glossed. What it costs, and what we do about it →
Source control and identity Decided GitHub Enterprise Cloud with Enterprise Managed Users. Accounts are provisioned from the identity provider, engineers cannot use personal GitHub accounts, and the enterprise controls which apps may be installed. This settles more than it looks — see the constraints below.
The component library Likely, not certain There may or may not be an external design firm building it. If there is, four conditions belong in the brief and none is unusual to ask for: a standalone versioned library with an example app consuming it as a dependency, built on headless primitives, tokens as DTCG JSON with the Tailwind theme generated from them, and AA contrast proven across the token set. The brief, in full →
Repository topology, standards, review, provenance, telemetry Recommended Decided in this document with reasoning and evidence. Argue with them on the merits — none of them depended on the platform decision, which is why they survived it unchanged.
What of the existing codebase survives Assessment not yet done Narrower than it was, and still the largest open question. With the stack settled, this is no longer "which platform" but "how much of this particular C# is worth keeping" — a code-quality judgment rather than a strategy one. Until someone has looked, this document assumes a rebuild that mines the old codebase for domain knowledge, and flags where that answer would change.
Specific Azure services Named, not final Container Apps, Health Data Services and the rest are named because "use a managed container host" isn't buildable. Each is one way to satisfy a stated requirement, and the requirement is the part that matters. See the capability table below.

Azure decides less of this plan than you would expect

Of the twenty-five standards in the register, twenty-one are indifferent to where it runs. Reuse discovery, type safety, the testing contract, review substantiveness, provenance, comment hygiene, accessibility, agent bounds — none of them care. Worth saying out loud, because it means the platform decision doesn't release a queue of work that was waiting on it. Nothing was waiting.

Four standards touch infrastructure at all: reversible release, an environment per change, result storage and queryable metrics. Those are the rows Azure actually answers, and they are answered below.

One of the four is load-bearing and the other three are chores. If a release cannot be reversed automatically in seconds, stage 11 stays a human gate and a chunk of the automation argument goes with it. Azure offers that capability three different ways, so it is a configuration question rather than a risk — but it is the one to confirm early rather than assume, because none of the three does it without a job you write.

The four infrastructure requirements, and the Azure answer to each

State these as capabilities, not products. The requirement is what has to be true; the service named is one way to make it true, and swapping it later costs nothing provided the requirement was written down first.

Requirement → why it matters → the Azure answer
RequirementWhy it's load-bearingThe Azure answerWhat to watch
Reverse a release in seconds, without a human This is what converts deployment from a human gate into an automated stage. The single most important infrastructure requirement in the document. Container Apps revisions with a weighted traffic split is the simplest — two revisions, a percentage, and something that resets it to zero on an alert. On AKS it is Argo Rollouts against Azure Monitor. App Service deployment slots also satisfy it. None of the three auto-reverts by itself. Something has to watch a metric and move the weight back, and that something is a job you write. Budget for it explicitly — it is the piece most often assumed present and then found missing.
An environment per change, torn down after One permanent shared environment becomes a queue, then a bottleneck, then the reason people batch changes — which defeats every control that assumes small batches. A per-PR resource group the pipeline creates and destroys, or one Container Apps environment with a revision per PR. Both are a few lines of IaC plus a teardown job. Cost, if teardown is unreliable. Put a hard expiry on the resource group and a scheduled sweeper — not just a delete step in a workflow that can fail.
Store every test run against its commit The third telemetry join. Cannot be backfilled — a run that wasn't recorded is gone. A table in the operational database, or blob storage keyed by commit. Genuinely trivial. Nothing technical. This is skipped for lack of a decision rather than lack of capability — which is exactly why it needs a named owner in stage 1.
Metrics the release process can query The rollback decision needs a number it can read. Dashboards for humans are not the same thing. Managed metrics service. Self-hosted time-series database — the common tooling here is open source and runs anywhere.
Two things not to conclude from this

Don't read "Azure is decided" as "the infrastructure work is done". The reversibility requirement is the load-bearing one and Azure does not satisfy it out of the box — the traffic-splitting primitive is there, the thing that watches an SLO and moves the split back is not. Somebody has to build that, and if nobody does, stage 11 stays a human gate and a chunk of the automation argument goes with it. That is a legitimate outcome; it just needs deciding rather than discovering.

And don't let any infrastructure question block stage 1. Every day-one item — review, provenance, the telemetry contract, tiering, the strictness settings, the practice standard — is a setting, a schema or a page of writing. None needs a server to exist. The most expensive mistake available here is deferring those while an infrastructure conversation runs, because they are the items that become unrecoverable the moment code exists without them.

Joint position

Where engineering and quality independently agreed

Two documents, different authors, no coordination. The overlap is the part to trust most.

Same conclusion, different route
ConclusionEngineering routeQuality route
Rules belong in machines, not documentsA rule in an instruction file costs tokens every session and is advisory; the same rule as a CI check is free and binding."If a control depends on someone remembering, it fails as soon as the team grows."
Human review is the highest-return controlComprehension — so the team can still operate the system in a year.Defect classes — AI-assisted PRs carry 1.7× the issues, concentrated in logic and correctness.
Risk decides how much process appliesFour lanes, routed by the paths a change touches.Three tiers, assigned from the change surface, never self-declared.
Measure outcomes, never activityCost per merged feature; throughput and stability always reported together.Escaped defects, not test counts. Coverage as diagnostic, never target.
Decide now or pay laterTwo decisions reach into every file: repository topology and language. Language is now settled, so topology is the one left.Five settings cost nothing today and are unrecoverable once history exists.
Nothing is a purchase firstAlmost the entire stack is open source; the bill is model usage and hosting.Stages one and two are decisions and schemas. First real spend is stage three.

The thesis both plans rest on

AI does not make a team better or worse. It amplifies whatever process it lands in. Good process gets faster; bad process gets faster at producing problems. So the investment is the process, not the tooling — and the window to build that process cheaply is now, before there is anything to retrofit it onto.

Five of DORA's seven capabilities that determine whether AI accelerates outcomes or accelerates chaos are classic engineering hygiene. The quality strategy and the AI strategy are the same strategy.

What each side supplied that the other was missing

Quality filled these engineering gaps: the telemetry contract; provenance at commit; a real definition of what a review is, with an audit; the metric set with written definitions; flake handling and the layering contract; the ownership model.

Engineering filled these quality gaps: the reuse gate that blocks before code is written; bounded agent loops; reversible deployment; accessibility with a legal basis; the stack itself.

The gaps are not symmetrical — quality supplies the evidence layer, engineering supplies the enforcement layer. Run either alone and you get half a system: controls with no proof they work, or proof with nothing behind it.

Reconciled

Where they disagreed, and what we're doing

Three genuine conflicts. Settling them in writing now is cheaper than discovering them in month four.

Conflict → resolution
ConflictEngineering saidQuality saidResolved
Four lanes or three tiers? Four lanes routed by the paths a change touches; Lane 4 needs named approval. Three tiers assigned from the change surface, each with a control set. Keep four, map to three. Same idea at different resolutions. Lanes are the routing label; tiers are the control sets. Lane 1→low, 2→medium, 3→high, 4→high plus a named approval. One label on the issue, no translation layer.
Dates or triggers? Five phases across ~90 days, 51 steps in dependency order. Four stages triggered by events. Attaching durations to a company with no pipeline is invented precision. Triggers win; dependency order survives. Stages start when something becomes true, with the ordered steps sitting inside them unchanged. Triggers are checkable, so nobody litigates progress.

Five stages, not four — quality's four with "one team pilots it" broken out, because a greenfield build has a distinct prove-it-once step that a quality retrofit doesn't.
Confidence score or named approval? The top lane needs an explicit named approval. Member harm isn't undone by a rollback. Release confidence scoring replaces sign-off. Not actually in conflict. Quality's own principle settles it: AI recommends, humans decide, every AI output has a named owner. The score is what the person reads before signing, never the decision itself.
The failure to guard against in the third one

A confidence score nobody may override has become the gate this design exists to remove. Always allow a documented override, and record who used it. A gate wearing a dashboard's clothing is still a gate.

Day one

What exists before the first commit

The only part of this plan with a real deadline.

Seven things, none of which cost money
ItemWhat it isCost of not doing it now
Human review requiredA branch protection rule set when the repository is created, with exactly one bypass actor — the pipeline, on Lane 1 only.The highest-return control available, for one setting. Targets the defect classes behind the 1.7× issue rate. Decide the Lane 1 bypass now rather than discovering the conflict in month two — the two rules as usually stated are incompatible.
Provenance from commit oneA commit trailer recording AI authorship, plus a CI check that it's present.Unrecoverable. You cannot manage what you cannot attribute, and it cannot be backfilled.
The telemetry contractThree joins: deploys carry the commit SHA, incidents carry the deploy, test results are stored per run against the commit.The expensive one. Retrofitting this join is the single biggest blocker in established organisations — an instrumentation programme wearing a quality programme's clothes.
Risk tiering rulesWhich surfaces are high risk here. Applied automatically from the change surface.Risk becomes self-declared, and self-declared risk is always low.
Repository topologyOne repository, with path-filtered CI from the first commit.The one decision left that reaches into every file. Merging repositories back together later is a project, not a decision. Language is settled — C# and React — so this is the half of that pair still open.
Strictness on both languagesNullable and warnings-as-errors solution-wide; the TypeScript strict family; any and unjustified suppressions banned.Unrecoverable in practice. Nobody turns warnings-as-errors on against a year of existing code — the error count makes it a project, so it never happens. This is the setting that earns the C# decision, and it is free today.
The shared config, twice, plus a parity checkDirectory.Build.props and @acme/config — one place per language where every rule is expressed, plus a job that fails when the two diverge. Projects extend; none redefine.Each project grows its own config, they drift, and enforcing anything new becomes eight pull requests and an argument. With two languages the drift is silent — a rule lands on one side and looks adopted, and nobody notices for months. The parity check is the cheapest control in this document.
The one-page practice standardHow we prompt, what context we share, what we check before accepting output. In version control, named owner.Far cheaper to write while the team is small enough to agree. On a wiki it rots and is discovered to be fiction at the worst moment.
If only two things survive contact with reality

Require human review on every AI-authored change, and record provenance in the commit, from the first repository. Both are settings. Both are free today. Neither can be recovered cheaply once there is history — and every other AI-specific control in this document depends on the second one existing.

Six decisions that need a name against them

Distinct from the settings above: these have no pipeline artifact. They are about ownership, authority, and what may not be switched off.

Settled before the team grows past one conversation
DecisionWhat has to be settled
Who owns qualityEvery engineer owns it; the quality function owns the capability to deliver it, never the execution. Stated before the first quality hire, because that job description sets the precedent permanently.
The AI stanceWhat AI may be used for, what review it requires, what data may go near it, who is accountable for accepting its output. Ambiguity is not neutral — engineers hedge, hide usage and over-rely, frequently all three.
What is non-negotiableThe short list nobody may switch off: human review, provenance, no real personal data in any environment. Everything else is a default that can be argued with.
The boundary with legalSecurity, legal and data protection own what must be true. Engineering owns how that is satisfied and enforced. Settle it before the first AI tool is provisioned and it never becomes a dispute.
What "high risk" means hereWritten once, applied automatically from the change surface.
Where the standard livesVersion control, reviewed like code, named owner. Not a wiki page.
Principles

Seven rules that settle arguments this document doesn't anticipate

Each names the decision it resolves and what it rules out. If a principle doesn't rule anything out, it isn't a principle — it's a slogan.

When evidence is ambiguous, the principle decides — not seniority
PrincipleSettlesRules out
1 · Quality is an attribute of the system, not a phase of it
You cannot inspect quality into a product		 Whether to add a proposed control Any control that neither prevents a defect class nor shortens a feedback loop. That's ceremony — remove it, don't refine it.
2 · The best person to prevent a defect is the one writing the code
The function makes them faster and better informed		 Who owns a given quality activity The function taking work back "because it's faster this once". It is faster once, and that is exactly why ownership never transfers.
3 · Feedback speed beats feedback completeness
90% accurate in four minutes beats 100% in four hours		 Trade-offs between a fast partial signal and a slow complete one Blocking on a comprehensive suite when a fast partial one would change behaviour today. Optimise the loop first, the coverage second.
4 · AI multiplies judgement, it doesn't replace it
Every AI output needs a named human owner		 Whether an AI output may act without a human Auto-closing failures, auto-merging changes, or any AI decision in the quality path nobody is accountable for. AI recommends; humans decide.
5 · Trust is the actual product of a quality function
Confidence to ship is what you're manufacturing		 Whether a signal or control is worth keeping Anything people have learned to ignore — a flaky suite, a noisy review bot, a dashboard nobody opens. An ignored signal is worse than none: it costs the same and misleads.
6 · Measure outcomes, never activity
Escaped defects, not test counts		 What goes on a dashboard, and what becomes a target Coverage percentage, test counts and automation percentage as targets. Useful as diagnostics; corrosive the moment anyone is measured on them.
7 · Culture changes through changed defaults, not changed opinions
Ship the paved road; the beliefs follow		 How to roll out any new practice Mandates, awareness campaigns and training as a first move. They work as reinforcement and fail as an opening.
How to use these

When a decision is contested and the evidence is ambiguous, the principle decides — not seniority, not the loudest preference. If you find yourself arguing against a principle repeatedly, change it openly rather than ignoring it quietly.

The rule

Mandate what's free; earn everything else

One distinction resolves what looks like a contradiction at the heart of both plans.

Culture changes through changed defaults, not changed opinions — mandates and awareness campaigns work as reinforcement and fail as an opening move. Yet mandatory human review on AI-authored change is non-negotiable and the very first thing we do. Both are true, because controls are not practices.

Controls — mandate these
  • Things the pipeline does: a required review, a duplication threshold, a scan, a provenance trailer.
  • Cost to comply is close to zero. The control runs whether or not anyone believes in it.
  • Set centrally as a default. Nobody is being asked to change their mind, so mandating costs nothing and buys the control immediately.
Practices — earn these
  • Things people do: how you prompt, what context you share, how you assess risk in refinement, what you check before accepting output.
  • Cost to comply is real. It requires changed habits, and belief is the mechanism by which habits change.
  • Pulled, never pushed. Prove it, package it, let demand carry it. Mandating produces minimum compliance and kills the practice you wanted.

The test to apply to any new rule

Does complying with this cost an engineer anything? If no, mandate it today and never discuss it again. If yes, you have to earn it — and the fastest way to earn it is to make the good version the one already sitting in the path.

Decisions

Every option, scored, with a pick

Sixteen decisions — four of them already settled by the business and documented rather than argued. For each: the criteria and their weights, every option with honest pros and cons, a computed score, and a recommendation carrying an evidence label. Disagree with a weighting and you can recompute — that's the point of showing them.

The short versionFull tab: ~45 min · this box: 1 min

  • Four are already settled and are recorded here, not reopened — Azure with C# and React, GitHub Enterprise Managed Users, GitHub Actions, and GitHub Issues. Three of the four fall out of the first two.
  • One genuinely hard decision is still open: one repository, or many. Under about thirty engineers, several repos cost more than they buy — and an agent that can see the whole system stops guessing. This is the one to spend the argument on.
  • C# on the back end is the stronger verification gate — nullable reference types and warnings-as-errors catch things before a test runs, which matters more when agents write the code. It costs us two reuse graphs and two enforcement layers, and those are named and paid for rather than glossed.
  • Buy Claude directly from Anthropic, not through Azure. This is the one place where keeping everything on one cloud is actively the wrong call — the Azure route silently drops the Batch API's 50% saving and the task budgets the pipeline uses to bound agent loops, and buys back nothing.
  • Put rules in machines, not documents. A rule in an instruction file is advice that costs tokens every session. The same rule as an automated check is binding and free.

Every option below has a "Why this rating?" button with the evidence behind it — including, for the options that lost, when they would have won.

Read the two labels differently

The percentage is a weighted rubric score derived from the criteria shown — my judgment, made transparent. The evidence label says how much published data backs the pick: Strong = direct study support, Moderate = indirect or partial, Judgment = principled reasoning, no direct study. A high score with a Judgment label is a defensible opinion, not a fact.

Category 1

Claude plan and seat model

What we actually buy, and for whom.

Confirm this one with sales before committing

Public sources conflict on whether Claude Code is included on all Team seats or only Premium seats. One reading: Team Standard is ~$20/seat/month and Claude Code requires Premium at ~$100–125/seat/month, minimum 5 seats. Another: Claude Code is on every Team and Enterprise seat, with Premium simply granting roughly 5× the usage. Plan terms move quickly — treat the numbers below as directional and get them confirmed in writing.

Criteria and weights — governance controls (30%) · cost predictability (25%) · admin overhead (20%) · headroom to grow (15%) · time to onboard (10%).
Individual Pro / Max subscriptions
38%

Each developer buys their own Pro (~$20/mo) or Max (~$100–200/mo). No central org.

Pros
  • Instant, no procurement
  • Predictable flat cost per head
  • Max tiers give heavy users generous limits
Cons
  • No central policy, no managed settings
  • No org plugin distribution — every dev configures themselves
  • No consolidated billing or spend visibility
  • No BAA path

Strong Plan capabilities are documented fact

Scores low not on capability — the models are identical — but because every governance mechanism this document depends on is absent: no managed settings, no org plugin marketplace, no consolidated spend visibility, no BAA path.

When it is still right: a solo developer, or a two-week evaluation before procurement. It stops being right the moment a second person needs the same configuration.
