Team planRecommended to start
81%

Central organisation with SSO, org-level plugin marketplace distribution, and consolidated billing. Standard and Premium seats can be mixed on one team.

Pros
  • SSO and just-in-time provisioning included
  • Org plugin marketplace — the distribution mechanism we need
  • Consolidated spend, per-user visibility
  • Mix seat tiers so heavy users get headroom without paying for everyone
Cons
  • No SCIM, no native audit logs
  • No custom data retention controls
  • Standard 200K context, not the 500K window
  • Seat minimums may apply on Premium

Verify Public sources conflict — confirm with sales before committing

The conflict: one reading has Claude Code on Premium seats only (~$100–125/seat/month, minimum 5); another has it on every Team seat with Premium simply granting roughly 5× the usage. That is a 5× difference in budget, so it is worth a written answer rather than an assumption.

What is not in dispute: Team includes SSO and just-in-time provisioning plus org-level plugin marketplace distribution — the two things needed on day one. SCIM, native audit logs, custom retention and the larger context window are Enterprise-only.

The upgrade trigger: a customer security questionnaire demanding audit logs, manual provisioning becoming painful, or HIPAA activation. Until one of those bites, Enterprise is admin surface without benefit.

Enterprise plan
74%

Adds SCIM, audit logs, custom retention, network controls, the larger context window, and HIPAA readiness. Self-serve from ~20 seats since early 2026; ~$20/seat plus usage at API rates.

Pros
  • Audit logs — genuinely needed for health-plan customer security reviews
  • SCIM for automated joiner/leaver
  • HIPAA activation and BAA path
  • Larger context window
Cons
  • Usage-based billing means spend is variable, not fixed
  • More admin surface than a ten-person team needs
  • Overkill before headcount and customers exist

Moderate Capabilities documented; the timing call is judgment

Enterprise is not worse than Team — it is Team plus SCIM, audit logs, custom retention, network controls and the larger context window, self-serve from around 20 seats. It scores 74% purely on timing.

The upgrade triggers, in likely order: a health-plan customer's security questionnaire demanding audit logs; manual joiner/leaver becoming error-prone; HIPAA activation for a PHI workload. Any one of those flips this to the right answer immediately.

Research: cost and governance →
Raw API, no subscription
44%

Pay per token with an API key, no seat licences.

Pros
  • No minimums, pay exactly what you use
  • Fine for CI and pipeline workloads
  • BAA available on the API
Cons
  • No org governance layer at all
  • Key sprawl and attribution problems
  • No plugin distribution

Strong Documented capability gap

Raw API keys are the right mechanism for machines, not people. No org governance layer, no plugin distribution, and attribution collapses the moment a key is shared.

Where it is actively correct: CI and pipeline workloads. Agent runs in GitHub Actions should consume an API key, not a human's seat allowance — pipeline spend should be attributable to the pipeline. Use both: seats for people, keys for machines.

Pipeline: where keys are used →

Pick: Team now, Enterprise when a specific control forces it  Moderate

Team buys the two things we actually need on day one — SSO and org plugin distribution — at predictable cost. Upgrade the moment one of these becomes true: a customer security questionnaire demands audit logs, headcount makes manual provisioning painful, or we need HIPAA activation.

Separately, use an API key for CI and pipeline workloads rather than consuming a human's seat allowance. Pipeline spend should be attributable to the pipeline.

Category 2

Model access path

Where inference actually happens, and who holds the BAA.

Criteria and weights — developer experience (30%) · compliance fit (25%) · model recency (20%) · setup cost (15%) · cost control (10%).
Anthropic first-partyRecommended
86%

Claude Code against Anthropic's own API and organisation.

Pros
  • Best ergonomics and newest models first
  • Org plugin marketplace, SSO, usage analytics
  • Simplest contract
  • BAA available with ZDR for CLI and Desktop-local
Cons
  • US processing only — no UK or EU data residency
  • ZDR excludes the Covered Models programme
  • MCP data flows sit outside the BAA

Strong Vendor documentation, verified directly

BAA coverage is narrower than usually assumed. Claude Code is covered on CLI and Desktop-local mode only, and only with Zero Data Retention enabled. Not covered: Claude Code Web, Desktop remote, Cowork, Workbench, Batch/Files/Skills APIs, Computer Use — and third-party MCP data flows.

ZDR and the Covered Models programme are mutually exclusive — the latter requires 30-day retention. You pick one.

vs the hyperscalers: first-party wins on feature completeness and model recency, and the gap is wider than usually assumed — every partner-operated route (Bedrock, Vertex, Foundry) is missing the Batch API and task budgets, which are the two features this plan's economics and agent bounds lean on hardest. A hyperscaler route wins only when a single BAA covering infrastructure and inference is a hard contractual requirement.

On the residency con above, be precise: first-party offers an inference-geography control with us and global options, so US-only processing is achievable and pinnable. What it does not offer is EU or UK residency. For a US company serving US members with a UK team that never touches real member data, that is not a constraint — but it is a rule to write down and get one line of legal confirmation on, rather than an assumption.

Full research →
AWS BedrockNot applicable — we are on Azure
52%

Claude served through your own AWS account, in your VPC, under the AWS BAA. Included for completeness and because it is the option people reach for when a hyperscaler BAA is demanded.

Pros
  • One BAA covering infrastructure and inference
  • In-region processing available (Ireland, Stockholm)
  • Data path stays inside your perimeter
Cons
  • The same feature gaps we reject Foundry for, and more — no Batch API, no task budgets, no Models API
  • Also missing: automatic prompt caching, web search, web fetch, code execution, programmatic tool calling, the MCP connector
  • Lag on new model availability; SSO needs wiring separately
  • Would mean adopting a second cloud for developer tooling alone

Strong Vendor feature availability — corrected downward from an earlier draft

This card previously scored 71% and that was wrong, in a way worth explaining rather than quietly fixing. The Foundry card is marked "avoid" largely because it lacks the Batch API and task budgets. Bedrock lacks both of those too — and additionally lacks the Models API, automatic prompt caching, the server-side web and code-execution tools, programmatic tool calling and the MCP connector. Several of those are available on Foundry in beta.

So on the criterion this document actually applies, Bedrock is not better than the option we told people to avoid. The earlier score reflected Bedrock's reputation rather than a feature check. Corrected to 52%, which is a real option with real gaps rather than a strong second place.

What it still wins: one AWS BAA covering infrastructure and inference, and genuine in-region processing when residency is contractually mandated. That is a narrow but occasionally decisive advantage. The trap if you ever take it: cross-region inference profiles route elsewhere by default, so residency must be pinned explicitly or you have bought nothing.

Why it is moot here: we are on Azure. Adopting AWS for developer tooling alone would be a second cloud relationship, a second BAA and a second access review, to obtain fewer features than first-party. Noted so nobody reopens it as an obvious-looking compromise.

Standards: the Azure carve-out →
Claude on Microsoft FoundryAvoid for this
34%

The obvious-looking choice on an Azure stack — Claude billed through the Microsoft Marketplace at standard API rates. It is the wrong one here, and the reasons are concrete.

Pros
  • Single Azure vendor relationship and billing
  • Standard API rates, no premium
  • Fits existing Microsoft procurement
Cons
  • No Batch API — forfeits the 50% discount on every non-latency-sensitive workload
  • No task budgets — loses a primary bounded-loop control for the pipeline
  • No Models API, no Managed Agents, no mid-conversation system messages
  • Prompt caching, web fetch and code execution are beta rather than GA

Strong Documented feature availability — check it yourself

This scores lowest of the four because the gaps land precisely where agentic workloads live:

No Batch API — forfeits the 50% discount on every non-latency-sensitive job (nightly test backfill, doc generation, eval runs, codemod planning). No task budgets — removes a primary bounded-loop control. Also absent: Models API, Managed Agents, mid-conversation system messages. Prompt caching, web fetch and code execution are beta rather than GA.

And it buys nothing back. Inference routes to Anthropic's servers whichever Azure region you select, so there is no residency or data-path benefit to weigh against the missing features. The only gain is a single invoice.

Same shape as Bedrock, on GCP, under Google's BAA.

Standards: the cloud question →
Whatever cloud you land on, don't buy Claude through it

This trap is worth naming because it will come up the moment a cloud is chosen. Keeping everything with one vendor looks tidy, and through Azure Foundry it quietly costs you the two things the cost section leans on hardest: the Batch API's 50% saving, and task budgets for bounding agentic loops.

And it buys nothing in return — Foundry routes inference to Anthropic's servers anyway, so there is no residency or data-path benefit to trade against the missing features. Run the application wherever it belongs; call Claude first-party. They're unrelated decisions.

Pick: Anthropic first-party, independent of where the product runs  Strong

The point most organisations miss: developer tooling, product runtime, and application hosting are three separate decisions. The usual reasoning — "we're on Azure, so use Claude on Azure" or "our product handles PHI so everything goes through the hyperscaler" — collapses them and produces a worse result on all three.

The cloud is currently open, with a lean toward AWS. Nothing on this page changes if that lands differently — which is precisely the argument for keeping the two decisions apart.

Residency doesn't bind us: US company, US market, US data. A UK engineering location doesn't change the applicable regime provided the UK team never touches real member data. Make that a rule and get one line of legal confirmation, since UK GDPR's reach follows where processing happens.

If the PHI runtime later needs a hyperscaler BAA, that's a separate decision for that one workload — and Bedrock is the stronger option even from an Azure shop, because Foundry's gaps are worst exactly where agentic workloads live.

The two rows this affects in the summary →
Category 3

Repository topology

The expensive decision. Hard to reverse, and it gates four others.

Clear this up before scoring

Monorepo and monolith are different things. You can run microservices from one repository with fully independent deploys — Google, Uber and Airbnb all do, using path-filtered CI. "Several teams and microservices" does not imply polyrepo. Most organisations end up with a polyrepo they never actually chose because these two questions got merged into one.

Criteria and weights — agent effectiveness (30%) · cross-cutting change cost (25%) · team autonomy (20%) · platform overhead required (15%) · hiring familiarity (10%).
Monorepo with modular servicesRecommended under ~30 engineers
84%

One repository; services are directories with independent, path-filtered deploy pipelines.

Pros
  • Agents see the whole system in one context
  • Cross-cutting changes take hours, not weeks
  • Standards inherit by clone — no distribution layer needed
  • The compiler catches cross-service breakage
  • Atomic refactors across boundaries
Cons
  • Needs build tooling early — and with two languages that means two: dotnet plus Nx
  • Teams can collide without discipline
  • CI must be path-filtered from day one or every pull request pays for both toolchains

Strong Multiple independent sources, one with a measured before/after

Cross-cutting changes are ~20% of commits and disproportionately expensive across repositories — a trivial library change propagating to its consumers takes weeks in a polyrepo versus about a day in a well-tooled monorepo.

Airbnb compressed an 18-month migration into 6 weeks using agents in a monorepo. The stated mechanism was whole-system visibility: the agent could trace a change through every consumer inside one context.

A 2025 CNCF survey found 42% of microservices adopters consolidating back into larger deployable units — driven by operational and economic overhead, not technical limits.

The decisive difference vs polyrepo: contextual fragmentation. An agent in one repo cannot read another, so it writes plausible code against interfaces it inferred rather than read. Generated clients narrow that gap; nothing closes it.

Full research: repository structure →
Polyrepo, service per repo
62%

One repository per service and UI, plus three platform repos that exist purely to hold it together.

Pros
  • Hard team autonomy and independent cadence
  • Blast radius bounded by construction
  • Per-repo access control is trivial
  • Familiar to most hires
Cons
  • Agents suffer contextual fragmentation — each repo is a conversation with no memory of the rest
  • Cross-cutting changes cost weeks
  • Forces a contracts repo, plugin marketplace and drift detection before they hurt
  • Platform function needed day one, not month six

Moderate Well-evidenced costs; the mitigations are less well-studied

Polyrepo is not wrong — it scores 62% rather than 30% because team autonomy is a genuine benefit and blast radius really is bounded by construction. Past roughly 50 engineers it becomes the right answer.

What the evidence penalises is the agent-era cost. Each repository interaction is effectively a conversation with no memory of the rest of the system, so agents generate code against interfaces they inferred rather than read.

The mitigations are real but partial: generated typed clients are the strongest (the agent reads types true by construction), then a service catalogue, then multi-root sessions. None restores whole-system visibility. That residual gap is the honest cost of this option.

Full research: repository structure →
Hybrid — monorepo core, satellites out
70%

Core domain in one repo; genuinely independent things (a marketing site, a vendor integration) get their own.

Pros
  • Keeps whole-system context where it matters
  • Lets truly separate lifecycles be separate
  • Pragmatic, and where most orgs actually land
Cons
  • Needs an explicit rule for what qualifies as a satellite, or it drifts to polyrepo by accident
  • Two sets of tooling conventions to maintain

Judgment No direct study — reasoning from the monorepo evidence

Scores 70% because it genuinely captures most of the monorepo benefit: whole-system context where the coupling actually is, with truly independent lifecycles kept separate. Most organisations end up here in practice.

The failure mode is drift. Without a written rule for what qualifies as a satellite, "this one is different" accumulates until you have a polyrepo you never chose — which is the exact pattern the topology section warns about. If you take this option, write the rule down first and make adding a satellite a reviewed decision.

Full research: repository structure →
What each topology obliges you to build
Monorepo — build this
  • Path-filtered CI
  • Committed .claude/ directory
  • Build tooling (Nx / Turborepo)
  • Module boundary linting
Polyrepo — build all of this instead
  • platform-standards repo + plugin + marketplace
  • contracts repo + generated clients + Pact
  • Reusable CI workflow + conformance check
  • Drift detection sweep
  • Service catalogue for cross-repo context
  • Campaign machinery for cross-cutting changes
  • A dedicated platform person from day one

Pick: Monorepo under ~30 engineers; polyrepo past ~50  Strong

Extracting a module into a service later is bounded, known work. Merging fifteen repositories back together is not — the asymmetry is the argument.

Take polyrepo if headcount is genuinely heading past fifty, or if per-repo access segregation is a compliance commitment someone has already made. If so, fund the platform function on day one; polyrepo without a paved road degrades fast.

Category 4

How standards reach developers

Inheritance, so nobody configures anything by hand.

Criteria and weights — inheritance is automatic (30%) · works across N repos (25%) · maintenance burden (20%) · reviewability (15%) · enforceability (10%).
Committed .claude/ directory
72%

Everything lives in the repo. Clone it and you have it.

Pros
  • Free, zero infrastructure
  • Changes go through normal code review
  • History in git
  • Automatic on clone
Cons
  • Only works if everyone clones the same repo
  • In a polyrepo it becomes copy-paste, which is drift

Strong Mechanical property, not a preference

Inheritance by git clone is free, reviewable like any other change, and versioned in git. Under a monorepo it is strictly better than a plugin — a plugin's only real advantage is distribution across repositories, which is worth nothing when there is one.

Why it scores 72% rather than higher: it fails completely the moment there are two repos. The copy-paste that follows is exactly how standards drift starts, and it is silent. Given the monorepo recommendation this is likely your answer — but structure the directory as if it were a plugin so promotion later is packaging, not a rewrite.

Setup: paved road v0 →
Plugin + org marketplaceRecommended for polyrepo
88%

Standards live in platform-standards, packaged as a plugin, distributed through the org marketplace.

Pros
  • Reaches every repo regardless of topology
  • Version pinning to a commit gives deterministic rollout
  • One catalogue can reference plugins from several repos
  • enabledPlugins can force-enable for everyone
Cons
  • A second repo to maintain
  • Dual-version trap: plugin.json and marketplace.json both carry versions; bump one and forget the other and it installs the stale one with no error

Moderate Documented mechanics; the dual-version trap is reported behaviour

Two mechanics make this the right answer across repositories. One marketplace catalogue can reference plugins from different repositories, each pinned to a branch or commit — so standards version independently of the catalogue. And because commits do not move, pinning gives deterministic rollout: nobody silently upgrades into a broken version.

The trap that will cost you an afternoon: plugin.json and marketplace.json both carry versions. Bump one and forget the other and Claude Code installs the stale plugin with no error message. You will ship a fix, watch it not take effect, and have nothing to debug. Guard it with a CI check.

Setup: the version-drift check →
Managed settings via MDM
64%

OS-level policy — registry on Windows, plist on macOS — that users cannot override.

Pros
  • Genuinely unoverridable — the only true enforcement layer
  • Can restrict plugin sources and block sideload flags
  • Locks telemetry so nobody redirects it
Cons
  • Requires an MDM estate we don't have yet
  • Reads as distrust at ten people
  • Slow to change — wrong for anything still evolving

Strong Documented capability; the timing call is judgment

Managed settings are the only genuinely unoverridable layer — OS policy applied above user, project and CLI settings. Four flags matter: allowManagedPermissionRulesOnly, allowManagedHooksOnly, strictKnownMarketplaces, and disableSideloadFlags.

That last one is the bypass people forget. Without it, every other control is one --plugin-dir or --mcp-config flag away from irrelevant.

Why it scores 64%: it requires an MDM estate you do not have, and at ten people it reads as distrust while adding nothing a committed config does not already achieve. This is ring three — deploy it when compliance or headcount forces it, not before.

Enforcement rings →

Pick: Plugin + marketplace, layered over a committed directory  Moderate

Under a monorepo, the committed directory alone is genuinely sufficient and strictly simpler — a plugin's only real advantage is distribution across repositories, worth nothing when there's one. Structure the directory correctly now and promoting it to a plugin later is packaging, not a rewrite.

Managed settings are ring three. Add them when you have MDM or a compliance requirement forcing it — and when you do, disableSideloadFlags is the one people forget: without it every other control is one CLI flag away from irrelevant.

Category 5

Which extension mechanism for which job

Not a competition — a routing table. Picking the wrong mechanism is the most common configuration mistake.

Routing table
You want to…UseNotBecause
State a convention that must always holdHook + CIInstruction fileProse is advisory and degrades as the file grows
Give domain knowledge used occasionallySkillInstruction fileLoads on demand; costs nothing when unused
Explain what this repo isAGENTS.mdSkillNeeded every session — but keep it short
Read many files without wrecking contextSubagentMain sessionSeparate context window, returns a summary
Review code impartiallySubagentSame sessionIt must not have seen the reasoning that produced the diff
Reach an external systemMCP or CLISkillSkills are knowledge, not connectivity. Prefer a CLI where one exists — it's more context-efficient
Ship all of the above to everyonePluginDocumentationDocs don't install themselves
Stop a developer overriding a controlManaged settingsProject settingsProject settings are editable by whoever they're constraining
Enforcement rings — outermost binds hardest
Ring 3 · Managed settingsUnoverridable
OS policy via MDM. The only layer a developer cannot edit. Needed eventually, not at ten people.
Ring 2 · HooksDeterministic
Block the action in-loop, giving the agent fast feedback instead of a failed pipeline ten minutes later.
Ring 1 · CIUnbypassable
The only ring that survives someone not using the agent at all. Every rule that matters must exist here.
Advisory · AGENTS.md and skillsSuggests only
Useful and necessary, but never a control. Do not treat a markdown file as a safeguard.

Rule: enforce in CI, mirror in hooks  Judgment

Never create a standard that exists only as a hook. A hook protects you from the agent; CI protects you from everything, including someone pasting code in from elsewhere. Write the check once as a script and call it from both.

The corollary resolves the context-versus-standards tension: every rule moved out of prose and into a hook costs zero tokens and becomes binding. Shrinking the instruction file strengthens enforcement rather than weakening it.

Category 6

Agent topology — how many, split by what

Criteria and weights — effect on failure modes that actually occur (35%) · token cost (25%) · maintenance burden (20%) · fit to coding as a task type (20%).
Single agent, no subagents
55%

One session does everything.

Pros
  • Cheapest by far
  • Nothing to maintain
  • No coordination failures possible
Cons
  • Context fills fast on wide reads
  • Reviews its own work, which is the weakest possible check

Moderate Cheapest option; loses on the failure category that dominates

Scores 55% rather than badly — it is genuinely the cheapest, there is nothing to maintain, and no coordination failure is possible because there is no coordination.

What it forfeits: the agent reviews its own work, which is the weakest possible check — it is anchored to the reasoning that produced the code. Verification failures are 21.3% of observed multi-agent failures, so this is the wrong economy. Adding one code-reviewer subagent costs a markdown file and closes most of the gap.

Full research: agent design →
Boundary-based — 2 to 4 agentsRecommended
89%

Agents defined by context isolation, tool restriction, independence, or cost tier — never by job title. Start with explore and code-reviewer; add security-reviewer and test-author when lanes demand them.

Pros
  • Directly targets verification failures, which are 21.3% of multi-agent failures
  • Tool restriction bounds blast radius
  • Cheap — a handful of markdown files
  • Each agent justifiable on a named axis
Cons
  • Reviewer loops cost tokens if not calibrated to report only real findings

Strong Two independent studies, both pointing the same way

The MAST taxonomy analysed 1,600+ execution traces across 7 multi-agent frameworks. Verification failures total 21.3%; specification and design issues 41.8%. "Disobey role specification" is the rarest mode at 0.5%.

The persona study covered 4 model families, 162 roles and 2,410 questions: adding personas to system prompts produced no improvement, often a small negative effect. A 2026 follow-up found role prompting increases apparent expertise depth while reducing clarity.

So what is real is the boundary, not the badge: separate context, restricted tools, independence from the work, and model tier.

vs role-based teams: the intervention MAST found most effective was adding high-level task verification (+15.6%); role and workflow adjustment gained less (+9.4%). Boundaries target the 21.3%; roles target the 0.5%.

Full research: agent design →
Role-based team — 8 to 12 agents
34%

Analyst, PM, Architect, UX, Scrum Master, Developer, QA, Tech Writer — an agile team simulated in agents. This is BMAD's model.

Pros
  • Impressive to demo
  • Forces explicit artefacts at each handoff
  • Genuinely useful for very large, well-understood processes
Cons
  • Optimises "disobey role specification" — 0.5% of observed failures
  • Multi-agent costs roughly 15× the tokens
  • Each handoff is a place to lose information; inter-agent misalignment is a whole failure category
  • Anthropic's own research finds multi-agent less effective for tightly interdependent tasks, naming coding specifically

Strong The vendor's own research argues against it for this task type

Anthropic's multi-agent research found these architectures excel at breadth-first problems that split into independent parallel strands, and are less effective for tightly interdependent tasks — naming coding specifically. They also measured roughly 15× token consumption versus single-agent.

Research fans out; coding does not. A feature touches schema, API, client and tests, and those decisions constrain one another. Handing them to separate role-agents manufactures exactly the inter-agent misalignment failures MAST catalogues — information withholding, ignored peer input, lost history.

Where it does win: genuinely parallel, well-understood processes with independent tracks. It scores 34% here rather than 10% because the pattern is sound — it is simply mismatched to coding, and expensive while being so.

Full research: agent design →

Pick: Boundary-based, two agents to start  Strong

Role personas are close to worthless for accuracy — a study across 4 model families, 162 roles and 2,410 questions found no improvement, often a small negative effect. Telling an agent it's a senior security engineer changes how it writes, not how correct it is.

What is real: separate context, restricted tools, independence from the work, and model tier. Every agent must be justified by one of those. If you can't name which, you're writing a costume.

Two design rules that matter more than the count: every agent ships with an explicit tools: line, and never run the reviewer on a cheaper model than the writer — a weak reviewer is worse than none because it manufactures false confidence.

Category 7

Specification method

Criteria and weights — reduces specification-class failures (30%) · overhead per change (25%) · audit value (20%) · portability (15%) · setup cost (10%).
All options compared
ApproachSetupPer-change costScoreFailure mode
Ad hoc promptingNoneZero31%Unreviewable sprawl; the code-quality decline data is largely this
TODO-list drivenNoneVery low44%Organises execution but not intent — the list still encodes whatever the agent assumed
Conventions onlyHoursZero57%Consistency decays as headcount grows
OpenSpec~5 minLow68%Too light for novel subsystems
Spec KitHoursMedium71%Heavy for small changes; agent-agnostic means it can't use hooks
BMADHours–daysHigh42%Ceremony exceeding team size
AWS KiroMediumMedium54%Adopts their IDE, model pipeline and ecosystem wholesale
Native EARS + Gherkin, risk-routed PickHalf a dayTunable87%You maintain it; no community templates to inherit

Pick: Steal Spec Kit's templates, implement natively, apply only to Lanes 3–4  Moderate

Spec Kit targets 30+ agents, which means it hits a lowest common denominator — it cannot use hooks, skills or subagents, so it can only ask nicely. A spec workflow that requests a completed risk section is a convention; one where a hook blocks implementation until the section exists is a control you can show an auditor.

Format: EARS for requirements, Gherkin for acceptance. EARS came out of Rolls-Royce and is standard in aerospace and medical devices. Its value is mechanical — the grammar makes vagueness ungrammatical. You cannot write "handles errors gracefully" because the template demands a named trigger, system and response.

Gherkin is the product/engineering contract: product reads and approves the scenarios, engineering owns the EARS. That single split answers "who signs off on what".

Category 8

Process depth and review routing

Criteria and weights — protects review capacity (35%) · protects sensitive code (30%) · enforceability (20%) · simplicity to explain (15%).
Risk lanes routed by pathRecommended
91%

Four lanes; blast radius determines process; wired to CODEOWNERS so nobody self-selects into a lighter path.

Pros
  • Spends scarce review capacity where it matters
  • Unfakeable — the lane follows what you touched
  • About an hour to set up
  • Scales agent and pipeline cost with risk
Cons
  • Four rules to explain instead of one
  • Path patterns need maintaining as the codebase grows

Moderate Rests on a well-measured constraint; the lane design itself is judgment

The constraint is measured. Agentic PRs wait 5.3× longer to be picked up for review; AI-assisted PRs 2.47× longer. Feature-branch throughput rose 59% year over year while throughput on main fell.

If review capacity is the binding constraint, spending it uniformly spends it badly. Routing by blast radius concentrates it where consequences are real.

What is judgment rather than evidence: four lanes specifically, and where the boundaries sit. No study says four. What the evidence supports is differentiated process; the granularity is a design choice you can tune.

Full research: the review bottleneck →
Uniform process for everything
52%

One standard applied to every change.

Pros
  • Trivial to communicate
  • No lane-assignment disputes
  • Easier to describe in an audit
Cons
  • Either wastes review on copy changes or under-protects auth code
  • There is no setting that's right for both

Judgment The constraint is measured; the conclusion is reasoning

Uniform process has real advantages: trivial to explain, no lane-assignment disputes, and much easier to describe in an audit. It scores 52% on those merits.

The arithmetic that sinks it: one setting has to serve both a copy change and a schema migration. Set it low and auth code is under-protected; set it high and you spend scarce review capacity — the thing agentic PRs already queue 5.3× longer for — on typo fixes. There is no value that is right for both.

Research: the review bottleneck →
Separate lane for AI-generated code
19%

Heavier review when a human didn't write it.

Pros
  • Intuitively appealing to sceptics
  • Feels cautious
Cons
  • Unenforceable — authorship isn't detectable or attestable
  • Creates a two-tier culture and incentivises lying
  • Within a year everything will be agent-assisted, so the lane becomes universal and meaningless

Strong Fails on a mechanical property, not a judgment call

This is the lowest-scoring option in the document, and the reason is structural rather than debatable: authorship is not detectable or attestable. There is no reliable signal, so the lane depends entirely on self-declaration — which means it is enforced by honesty and penalises it.

It also builds a two-tier culture, and within a year essentially all code will be agent-assisted, at which point the lane is universal and therefore meaningless.

What to do instead: route by blast radius. A risky change deserves scrutiny whoever or whatever wrote it; a copy tweak does not. Risk is observable from the path; authorship is not.

Risk lanes →

Pick: Four risk lanes, routed by path  Moderate

Lane 1 cosmetic: direct prompt, CI only, no human. Lane 2 standard: plan mode, one same-team reviewer plus reviewer subagent. Lane 3 sensitive: written spec, two reviewers, security subagent. Lane 4 member data: full spec, human-authored tests, named accountable approver.

Two supporting rules: the reviewer subagent always runs before any human, and teams review their own code by default — cross-team review only for contract changes, or every team becomes a dependency of every other team.

Category 9

CI/CD platform and agent execution

Settled GitHub Actions — decided for us by Enterprise Managed Users. The comparison below is retained because the reasoning still matters when someone asks why, and because it is the exit route if EMU ever changes. Treat it as the record of a decision, not an open question.

Criteria and weights — agent integration maturity (30%) · reusable workflow support (25%) · cost (20%) · ecosystem (15%) · self-host option (10%).
GitHub ActionsRecommended
87%

Reusable workflows called by every repo, plus anthropics/claude-code-action@v1 for agents in the pipeline.

Pros
  • First-party Claude Code action — the maturest agent integration available
  • workflow_call gives true call-don't-copy reuse
  • Tight integration with issues, PRs and labels, which is how pipeline state is carried
  • Free tier generous for a small team
Cons
  • Ties you to GitHub
  • Minutes cost rises with heavy E2E

Moderate Feature availability is verifiable; the ranking is judgment

The deciding factor is the first-party anthropics/claude-code-action@v1 — the most mature agent integration available, running the agent headless in a runner. On GitLab or Buildkite everything agent-related is DIY via the headless CLI.

workflow_call gives genuine call-don't-copy reuse, which is the mechanism the whole standards layer depends on: every repo gets six lines, and improving the pipeline improves all of them by moving a tag.

Where GitLab wins: self-hosting, if data residency ever becomes a hard requirement. Worth knowing as an exit route rather than a starting point.

Setup: the shared workflow →
GitLab CI
66%

Includes templates, strong self-hosting story.

Pros
  • Excellent self-hosted option if residency ever bites
  • Includes/extends for reuse
  • Built-in registry and security scanning
Cons
  • Agent integration is DIY via headless CLI
  • Smaller action ecosystem to borrow from

Moderate Capable; loses on agent integration maturity

GitLab CI is a strong product — include/extends gives real reuse, and the built-in registry and security scanning are genuine advantages.

Why it loses: everything agent-related is DIY via the headless CLI. You would be rebuilding what claude-code-action already provides, and maintaining it. That is a poor use of the platform team on a small org.

The scenario that flips it: a hard self-hosting requirement — if data residency or a customer contract ever forces CI on your own infrastructure, GitLab is the strongest option. Worth knowing as an exit route.

Setup: the shared workflow →
Buildkite / CircleCI / Jenkins
48%

Specialist or legacy CI.

Pros
  • Buildkite's hybrid model suits regulated workloads well
  • Mature at scale
Cons
  • Everything agent-related is DIY
  • Extra vendor for a greenfield team that doesn't need one
  • Jenkins specifically is maintenance you shouldn't sign up for in 2026

Judgment Ecosystem observation rather than measurement

Buildkite deserves separating from the other two — its hybrid model (managed control plane, your own runners) suits regulated workloads genuinely well, and it is a serious product.

Why all three lose here: an extra vendor relationship for a greenfield team that does not need one, plus fully DIY agent integration. Jenkins additionally carries plugin-maintenance overhead that is hard to justify starting fresh in 2026 — you would be adopting a maintenance burden as a founding decision.

Setup: the shared workflow →

Pick: GitHub Actions, with a single reusable workflow  Moderate

The shared workflow is the highest-leverage file in the estate: every repo gets six lines, and improving the pipeline improves every repository at once by moving a tag. Pin callers to a tag, never a branch — a branch means every repo's CI changes the moment you merge, which is how you break fifteen pipelines at 5pm on a Friday.

Category 10

End-to-end test framework

Criteria and weights — agent first-pass accuracy (30%) · debugging evidence quality (25%) · speed (20%) · platform coverage (15%) · ecosystem (10%).
PlaywrightRecommended
88%

Direct browser control, role-based locators, auto-retrying web-first assertions.

Pros
  • One idiomatic way to do things — measurably better first-pass generated code
  • Trace viewer and screenshots give agents readable evidence to iterate on
  • Fast; Chromium, Firefox and WebKit
  • getByRole doubles as an accessibility forcing function, which we need anyway for a Medicare population
Cons
  • No native mobile
  • Selector churn still costs you — that's a rules problem, not a framework one

Moderate Industry comparison rather than controlled study

The 2026 comparisons converge on one point that matters here: Playwright's explicit async/await, role-based locators and auto-retrying web-first assertions give agents a predictable, heavily-documented surface, which tends to yield more correct first-pass generated code. WebdriverIO's protocol-level flexibility is a strength for humans and a liability when agents write the tests.

Playwright also uses direct browser control rather than the WebDriver protocol, so it is materially faster — and its trace viewer gives agents readable evidence to iterate against.

Where WebdriverIO still wins: native mobile via Appium, and standards-based WebDriver across unusual browsers. Both comparisons agree neither framework saves you from selector churn — that is a lint-rules problem in either.

Sources →
WebdriverIO
63%

WebDriver protocol by default, Appium integration for native mobile.

Pros
  • Native mobile via Appium — the one thing Playwright can't do
  • Standards-based WebDriver across unusual browsers
  • Very flexible
Cons
  • That flexibility is a liability when agents write the tests — more ways to express the same thing means more ways to get it subtly wrong
  • Slower via the WebDriver protocol

Moderate Strong tool; the weakness is specific to agent authorship

WebdriverIO scores 63% because it is a strong product, and it holds one capability Playwright does not have at all: native mobile via Appium. If the product ships a native app, this becomes the right answer for that suite.

Why it loses for web: the protocol-level flexibility that makes it powerful for humans means more ways to express the same thing — and therefore more ways for an agent to be subtly wrong. Playwright's single idiomatic path is a constraint that helps here.

Don't pick it defensively. Choosing WebdriverIO for web today because mobile might come later is a bad trade — native mobile is a separate suite either way.

Standards: the test rules that matter more →

Pick: Playwright for web; revisit WebdriverIO only if native mobile appears  Moderate

Don't pick WebdriverIO for web today on the theory you might need mobile later — that's a separate suite either way.

The rules matter more than the framework, since neither saves you from selector churn: getByRole first and data-testid second with CSS and XPath banned by lint; no sleeps; one scenario per test named for its requirement ID; tests derived from the spec's Gherkin, with CI failing when a scenario has no test; full suite under ten minutes.

Category 11

Pipeline autonomy — where the human gates go

Criteria and weights — defensibility of the gate choice (30%) · throughput (25%) · containment of a bad change (25%) · implementation cost (20%).
Gate on what feels risky
36%

Human approval wherever people are nervous.

Pros
  • No design work needed
  • Feels safe
Cons
  • Impossible to defend to an auditor — "we felt uneasy" isn't a criterion
  • Reliably gates the wrong things: heavy review on copy, none on schema migrations
  • Gates accumulate and never get removed

Judgment No study — but the failure pattern is consistent and predictable

This is what most organisations do by default, which is why it is worth naming rather than ignoring. It requires no design work and it feels prudent.

Three predictable failures. It cannot be defended to an auditor — "we felt uneasy" is not a criterion. It reliably gates the wrong things, because instinct tracks visibility rather than consequence: heavy review on user-facing copy, none on a schema migration. And gates only ever accumulate, because removing one requires someone to argue for less caution.

The alternative costs nothing extra — gating on reversibility is the same number of gates, chosen by a rule you can state out loud.

Pipeline: the governing rule →
Gate on irreversibilityRecommended
92%

A step is automated if undoing it is cheap, gated if undoing it is expensive. Then invest in making expensive undos cheap.

Pros
  • Mechanical and defensible — every gate has a stated reason
  • Turns "do we trust the agent?" into "how expensive is the undo?", an engineering question with an answer
  • Gates disappear on their own merits as reversibility improves
  • Only two permanent gates result
Cons
  • Requires real investment in canary, SLOs and feature flags before deploy can be unattended

Strong The enabling evidence is operational, not AI-specific — which is why it wins arguments

What makes unattended deploy defensible is the rollback, not the agent. Automatic reversal on an SLO breach takes seconds; a manual one needs someone paged, oriented and acting. Widely-cited figures put automated rollback at around 70% faster incident resolution — directionally right, though we could not confirm it against a primary source, and the argument holds on the mechanism alone.

The principle it unlocks: you can convert an irreversible step into a reversible one by investing in the undo. How much of the pipeline can run without a human is therefore a function of reversibility investment, not of confidence in the agent.

vs gating on instinct: every gate here has a stated reason, so gates can be removed on evidence rather than accumulating forever. That is the difference between a pipeline you can defend to an auditor and one you cannot.

Full research: bounded loops and deployment →
Full autonomy, no gates
23%

Agents ship to production unsupervised across all change types.

Pros
  • Maximum throughput on paper
Cons
  • Only 0–20% of tasks are fully delegable without a harness
  • Nobody can check the spec against intent but a human
  • In a healthcare context, member harm is not undone by rollback
  • Gets shut down permanently the first time it ships something wrong

Strong Vendor research plus a domain constraint that does not move

Anthropic's own research describes a delegation gap: AI touches around 60% of work, but only 0–20% can be fully delegated. That is the honest ceiling on unattended work today.

Two things also cannot be automated regardless of capability. Only a human can check the spec against intent — an agent can only check code against the spec. And in healthcare, member harm is not undone by rollback; rollback restores the system, not the person.

The practical objection is worse than the theoretical one: a pipeline pitched as needing no humans gets shut down permanently the first time it ships something wrong. Two defensible gates survive that same incident. Ambition here is self-defeating.

Pipeline: earning autonomy →
Resulting pipeline — two gates, everything else bounded and automated
Analysislane, services, prior art
→
Gate 1spec approved
product + tech lead
→
Designplan, contracts
→
Buildtasks, code, tests
→
VerifyCI + reviewer agents
→
Gate 2human review
lane 2+
→
Shipcanary + auto-rollback

Pick: Gate on irreversibility, and buy reversibility deliberately  Strong

The corollary is what unlocks the pipeline: you can convert an irreversible step into a reversible one. Deploying to production is irreversible if rollback takes an engineer twenty minutes; it's reversible if a canary auto-rolls-back on an SLO breach in seconds. That's an operational-maturity argument rather than an AI one, which makes it far easier to win with a sceptic — you're asking them to approve better rollback, not more autonomy.

How much of your pipeline can run unattended isn't a fixed property of software delivery. It's a direct function of how much you've invested in the undo.

Every automated stage declares four bounds — iteration cap, budget cap, an evaluable success function, escalation path — plus no-progress detection. Bounded loops have been measured using 90% fewer tokens with 525× less run-to-run variance than unbounded ones — and unpredictable cost is the harder problem, because you cannot budget for it.

Category 12

Primary language

Settled C# on the back end, React on the front — decided as a business call, not derived here. The comparison is kept because the reasoning still matters: it names exactly what the decision costs, and those costs turn into three mandatory builds rather than a shrug. This is also one of only two decisions in this document that is genuinely hard to reverse, so it is worth understanding even though it is closed.

The starting position

There is an offshore-built C# / Azure / React codebase. Whether any of it is worth keeping has not been assessed yet — that is one of the first tasks, not a conclusion already reached. Early signals are unfavourable, but nobody has actually looked.

The platform question, though, is closed. C# on Azure with a React front end is the decision, taken on grounds this document doesn't own — existing expertise, the Azure estate, the .NET healthcare ecosystem, and who can be hired. So this section is no longer "which stack"; it is "what did that choose for us, and what does it cost".

Two rubrics, and the gap between them is the point

The original weighting in this document was drawn from the requirements as first stated — reuse discovery and uniform enforcement above everything else. On that weighting, one language across browser and server wins, and it is not close.

The weighting below is the one the decision was actually made on, which properly includes platform fit, the compiler as a verification gate, and the healthcare ecosystem. On that weighting C# wins, and that is also not close.

Both are honest, and the distance between them is the bill. Everything C# gives up is something we now have to build on purpose instead of getting free — which is why the verdict below is half decision and half construction work, and why that construction work is not optional.

Criteria and weights (as decided) — platform and ecosystem fit (25%) · compiler strength as a verification gate (25%) · reuse discovery and enforcement across the boundary (20%) · healthcare and FHIR tooling (15%) · hiring (15%).
Original weighting, retained for comparison — single reuse graph (30%) · one enforcement layer (25%) · type safety achievable (20%) · design-system fit (15%) · hiring (10%).
C# back end + React front endDecided
86%

ASP.NET Core on Azure, React and Tailwind on the front, OpenAPI as the contract between them with the TypeScript client generated from it.

Pros
  • The stronger verification gate, by default — nominal typing, nullable reference types, no implicit any. More is caught before a test runs, and that matters more when agents write the code, not less
  • Best-in-class Azure integration — the platform decision and the language decision agree with each other
  • Mature FHIR tooling — the Firely .NET SDK and Azure Health Data Services are the most complete FHIR stack available anywhere
  • Plays to existing expertise rather than betting on a stack nobody in the room has run
  • US healthcare hiring is .NET-heavy, and the paying customers are US health plans
Cons
  • Two reuse graphs. "Does this already exist?" has to be asked twice, and can still miss
  • Two enforcement layers. Every standard is expressed twice and they will diverge unless something checks that they haven't
  • Domain types cannot simply flow to the browser — they must be generated, and generated things go stale

Moderate Sound decision on the stated criteria; the mitigations are the part to hold us to

Why it wins on the decided weighting. Platform fit and compiler strength are half the rubric between them, and C# takes both outright. The compiler point is the one worth internalising: a language whose default is to reject ambiguity is doing verification work you would otherwise have to configure, remember, or catch in review — and review is the scarce resource this entire document is trying to protect.

Add the FHIR ecosystem, which is not a rounding error for this product. Firely and Health Data Services are the reference implementations; the equivalent in TypeScript is good but thinner, and FHIR is precisely the kind of large specification that is easy to implement subtly wrong.

What it genuinely costs, and there is no arguing it away: the two hardest requirements this document started from — reuse discovery and uniform enforcement — both degrade across a language boundary. They do not become impossible. They become engineering work rather than a property of the stack. Three specific builds follow from that, all named in the verdict below, and none of them is optional — skipping any one is how this choice turns into the failure mode the TypeScript option avoided for free.

Standards: the stack, and the three mitigations →
TypeScript end to endWins the original rubric
68%

NestJS back end, React front end, shared packages for domain types, UI and config. Scored 88% on the original weighting; 68% on the decided one.

Pros
  • One reuse graph — "does this exist?" has one search and one answer
  • One lint config enforces every standard across the whole stack
  • Domain types flow server → browser with no codegen, so nothing can drift
  • Module boundaries enforceable as lint rules across the entire codebase, not half of it
Cons
  • Type safety must be configured to approach C#'s default rigour, and the configuration is load-bearing
  • Thinner FHIR tooling for a FHIR-centric product
  • Bets the platform on expertise the team does not currently have

Moderate Reasoning from stated requirements — not from a language benchmark

Be clear what this was never based on. There is no rigorous language-by-language benchmark of agent accuracy. SWE-bench, Terminal-Bench and their peers measure models, not languages. Any claim that agents are "measurably better" at one mainstream language is weaker than it sounds, and this document does not make it.

What it was based on is that reuse discovery and uniform enforcement both degrade across a language boundary. That argument was correct and remains correct — it simply lost to criteria weighted higher.

The industry guidance actually points the other way here: in established .NET environments you generally get further staying where your systems already live. That is the position we are in, so this is a case of the general advice and the decision agreeing.

What to take from this card rather than mourn: it is the checklist of what to build deliberately. One reuse graph became one catalogue with two generators. One lint config became two configs with a parity test. Types that flowed became a generated client with a staleness gate. Read the losing option as a specification for the mitigations.

Standards: the three mitigations →
Salvage the offshore codebase
29%

Keep the existing code and refactor toward standards incrementally.

Pros
  • No throwaway work; some domain logic may be worth keeping
  • Lower short-term cost
Cons
  • Retrofitting standards onto code that violates all of them is slower than rebuilding, and far less predictable
  • Every rule on the Standards page would need a baseline-and-ratchet exception, which weakens all of them at once
  • Team inherits a codebase they didn't write and don't trust — the opposite of your comprehension goal

Moderate Conditional on an assessment only your team can make

Scores 29% on the working assumption that little of the offshore code is usable — an assumption, not a finding, because the assessment has not been carried out yet. If that judgement is wrong — if there is salvageable domain logic — this rises sharply, and it is worth an honest look before committing to a rebuild.

Note this option got closer, not further away. With the stack settled as C# on Azure, salvage is no longer a rewrite in disguise — the existing code is already in the target language on the target platform. That removes the largest objection to it and makes the assessment in step 1 materially more consequential than it was. The question narrowed from "is this the right stack" to "is this good C#", and only reading it answers that.

Why it scores low as things stand. Retrofitting standards onto code that violates all of them is slower and far less predictable than rebuilding. Every rule on the Standards page would need a baseline-and-ratchet exception, which weakens all of them at once and removes the clean line the team can hold.

And it works against a stated goal. You want developers who fully understand the code. A team inheriting an unfamiliar codebase they already distrust is the opposite starting position.

The middle path worth considering: rebuild the platform, but mine the old codebase for domain knowledge — business rules, edge cases, integration quirks. That knowledge is often the genuinely expensive part, and it survives the rewrite.

Standards: the starting position →

Decided: C# back end, React front end, one repository  Moderate

The reasoning that carries it is the compiler, not the ecosystem. A language that rejects ambiguity by default is doing verification work that would otherwise land on configuration, memory, or review — and review is the one resource this whole document is built to protect. Nullable reference types, nominal typing and warnings-as-errors are a quality gate we get for free and that an agent cannot talk its way past.

Three things now have to be built that a single language would have given us for nothing. They are not nice-to-haves; they are the price of this decision, and each has a step in the build plan:

  • One catalogue, two generators. The reuse stage reads a single catalogue.json, but it is produced by two walkers — a Roslyn analyser over the C# solution and ts-morph over the TypeScript packages. One file, one search, one answer. If the catalogue only ever covers half the codebase, the reuse gate is theatre on the other half.
  • Two configs, one parity test. Every rule in the standards register names its C# mechanism and its TypeScript mechanism, and a rule counts as adopted only when both are wired. A CI check compares the two rule sets and fails when one side gains a rule the other lacks — that check is the entire defence against the slow divergence this option is criticised for.
  • A generated client with a staleness gate. Domain types cannot flow to the browser, so they are generated: OpenAPI emitted from the C# controllers, a typed TypeScript client generated from that, and CI failing when the committed client no longer matches the spec. This is the document's own "generated client" mechanism, promoted from a nicety to load-bearing.

What did not change, which is most of it. React, Tailwind, the component library and the token pipeline are untouched — they were always front-end decisions. So are the risk lanes, the spec chain, Gherkin as the product contract, provenance, the telemetry contract, reversible release and every agent bound. Twenty-one of the twenty-five standards never cared what the back end was written in.

Full stack detail, the reuse architecture and the enforcement matrix are on Code standards.

Category 13

Work tracker — where an ask arrives

Settled GitHub Issues — same reason. With the estate on GitHub Enterprise Cloud, a second tracker means a second vendor, a second access review under EMU, and the sync layer this analysis argues against. The Linear case below is still worth reading if a dedicated product function ever arrives.

This is the first question anyone asks about the pipeline and the one most easily got wrong. The tracker is not a reporting tool here — it is the pipeline's state machine. Labels carry lane and size, comments carry agent output, and the issue-to-PR link is the audit trail. A tracker the agent cannot read and write natively means writing and maintaining a sync layer, which is a permanent tax on every stage.

Criteria and weights — agent read/write without glue (35%) · same permission model as code (20%) · product usability for non-engineers (20%) · cost (15%) · reporting for a US parent (10%).
GitHub Issues + ProjectsRecommended
85%

Issues are the unit of work; Projects gives product a board view. Labels are pipeline state. Included in the GitHub plan you already need.

Pros
  • The agent already runs inside GitHub Actions — no API bridge, no webhook relay, no token for a second vendor
  • gh issue and gh pr are first-class agent tools; label writes are one command
  • Issue → branch → PR → merge is one linked chain, which is the audit trail
  • Same permission model as the code, so no second access review
  • No extra licence
Cons
  • Weakest of the four for product management — no native estimates, roadmaps or dependency graphs
  • Cross-repo views need Projects configured deliberately
  • Deepens GitHub lock-in, which is already the CI bet

Judgment Integration surface is verifiable; the weighting is a judgment call

The decisive factor is glue code. Every other option requires a sync layer between the tracker and the place the agent runs — a webhook receiver, a service token, a field-mapping table and a reconciliation job for when they drift. That layer has to be built, monitored and debugged by the same small team building the product. With GitHub Issues there is nothing to build: the agent is already authenticated to the repo it is working in.

State in labels only works if label writes are trivial. The pipeline design puts lane, size and stage in labels precisely so state survives agent restarts. gh issue edit --add-label is one call with credentials the runner already holds.

Where it genuinely loses: product management depth. If a product function arrives expecting roadmaps, estimates and dependency tracking, Issues will feel thin — and that is a real cost, not a rounding error.

Pipeline: what each stage runs on →
Linear
71%

The best-liked product-side tracker, with a clean GraphQL API and an official MCP server.

Pros
  • Far better product experience — cycles, projects, roadmaps, triage inbox
  • Official MCP server, so the agent can read and write issues without bespoke glue
  • Native GitHub PR linking closes issues on merge
  • Fast; teams genuinely like using it
Cons
  • Pipeline state now lives in two systems — Linear status and GitHub labels — and they will drift
  • Per-seat cost, and product/clinical staff need seats too
  • A second access review and a second BAA conversation for a healthcare org
  • The agent needs a Linear token in CI, separate from its GitHub identity

Judgment Strong product; loses on state duplication rather than capability

Linear is the best product in this category and the MCP server closes most of the integration gap — this is not a weak option, and it is the one to pick if product ownership is strong from day one.

Why it does not win here: two systems of record for the same fact. Pipeline stage lives in GitHub labels because CI reads labels; work status lives in Linear because product reads Linear. Every stage transition must now write both, and any write that fails leaves them inconsistent. That is exactly the reconciliation problem the single-system choice avoids.

Also worth pricing honestly: a healthcare org putting anything member-adjacent in a tracker needs a BAA and a data-handling review for that vendor. Free with GitHub because it is already in scope; a project with Linear.

The scenario that flips it: a dedicated product function of two or more people who will live in the tracker daily. Their throughput matters more than the engineering team's integration convenience, and Linear buys real velocity there.

Pipeline: what each stage runs on →
Jira
52%

The enterprise default. Deepest configurability, heaviest process.

Pros
  • Nothing it cannot model — custom workflows, fields, approval chains
  • A US parent's PMO and compliance function will already know it
  • Mature audit and permission features that map well to regulated reporting
Cons
  • Configuration becomes a job — and on a greenfield team, someone's job it should not be
  • REST API is workable but verbose; agent integration is real work
  • Its strength is process enforcement, which is the thing you are moving into CI and hooks
  • Slowest of the four to actually use

Judgment Capability is not the issue; fit is

Jira can model anything, and if a US parent already runs on it, inheriting it costs nothing politically and buys goodwill. That is a legitimate reason to pick it.

Why it scores low on this pipeline specifically: Jira's core value is encoding process in workflow states and transition rules — and this design deliberately puts process enforcement in CI checks and hooks, where it is testable, versioned and reviewable in a PR. Running both means two places a rule can live and two places to look when something is blocked.

The practical tax: agent integration is a bespoke REST client with a service account, field-ID mapping and its own error handling. Every schema change in Jira is a potential silent break in the pipeline.

The scenario that flips it: the US parent mandates Jira for portfolio reporting. Then take it, and mirror only the minimum into GitHub labels — one-directional, Jira to GitHub, so there is still a single writer per fact.

Pipeline: what each stage runs on →
Azure DevOps Boards
41%

Work items, boards and pipelines in one product. The natural choice if the estate were staying on Azure.

Pros
  • Coherent if you are already all-in on Azure and Entra ID
  • Work items, repos, pipelines and test plans under one licence
  • Good enterprise reporting out of the box
Cons
  • Splits the estate — code and CI on GitHub, work items on ADO, agent identity in both
  • No first-party Claude Code integration; everything agent-side is DIY
  • Microsoft's own investment has visibly shifted to GitHub
  • Carries over a tool from a stack you are explicitly leaving

Judgment Scored against this stack, not in the abstract

ADO Boards is a competent product and would score far higher on an Azure-native estate — work items, repos, pipelines and test plans under one roof, with Entra ID identity throughout, is a genuinely coherent story.

Why it scores lowest here: the stack decision already moved CI to GitHub Actions for the first-party claude-code-action. Keeping Boards splits the estate across two vendors at exactly the seam the pipeline crosses most often — the agent would hold two identities and cross a vendor boundary at nearly every stage transition.

The honest caveat on the evidence: the Microsoft field study showing +24% merged PRs was measured on Azure DevOps. That tells you the platform is fine for this class of work; it says nothing about which tracker suits an agent-driven pipeline sitting on GitHub.

The scenario that flips it: the parent company mandates Azure and Entra ID for compliance reasons. Then keep the whole estate on ADO rather than splitting it — a coherent ADO setup beats a split GitHub/ADO one.

Research: the Microsoft field study →

Pick: GitHub Issues + Projects  Judgment

Because pipeline state and code state must live in one system. Everything the pipeline does is a read or write against an issue, a label, a branch or a PR — putting any of those behind a second vendor's API turns a one-line agent call into an integration to maintain.

The trigger to revisit: a dedicated product function of two or more people. At that point Issues starts costing them more than the sync layer would cost you, and Linear becomes the right answer — adopted as the product system of record with GitHub still owning pipeline state, written one way only.

Picks 14, 15 and 16 have no option cards because none had real competition — no established alternative approach to compare against. All three are set out in full under Code standards: the reuse architecture →, the design system and token pipeline →, and the contract between C# and the browser →. Pick 16 exists only because of the language decision — with one language it would not be a decision at all, which is the clearest single illustration of what that choice cost.

Four are already settled. One is hard. Three more are costly.

Settled elsewhere, not open here — language and platform, CI/CD, the work tracker, and source control. Three of those four fall out of two business decisions: Azure with C#, and GitHub Enterprise Managed Users. Reading them as open questions is the most common way this document gets misused in a meeting.

Hard, and genuinely still ours — repository topology. It reaches into every file eventually, and merging repositories back together later is a project rather than a decision. This is now the one decision on this page worth a real argument.

Costly but possible — the end-to-end test framework, the design system, and the reuse architecture. Each is weeks of rework rather than months, and none blocks the others.

The remaining seven can be decided this week and revisited in six months at modest cost. Spend the argument on topology; take the recommendation on the rest and move. A team that debates all sixteen with equal energy will still be debating in March.

End to end

From product ask to production

Eleven stages. Two human gates, plus one that fires only on contract changes. Every automated stage declares its inputs, outputs, bounds and escalation path — a stage missing any of those is not production-ready.

The architecture comes first, then every stage named down to the tool, the file and the model.

The short versionFull tab: ~22 min · this box: 1 min

  • A request becomes live software in eleven steps. Nine run automatically. Two need a person, plus a third that only fires when a change would affect another team.
  • The two human checkpoints are both judgment calls. Is this the right thing to build? — a machine can check code against a written requirement, but only a person can check the requirement against what was meant. And does this fit the system we have?
  • Every automated step has four limits: how many attempts, how much it may spend, a test it runs on itself to know it's finished, and where it goes when it's stuck. Bounded agent loops have been measured using 90% fewer tokens with 525× less variance than unbounded ones — the unpredictability is the real danger, not the average.
  • Releasing is automated because undoing it is cheap — new code goes to 5% of traffic, health is watched, and it reverses itself in seconds if anything worsens. That investment is what earns the automation, not trust.
  • The exception is data. Code can be rolled back; a deleted database column cannot. Those changes stay with a named human, permanently.
The governing rule

Automate by reversibility, gate by irreversibility

One rule decides every gate. That's what makes the design defensible to a regulator, an auditor, or a sceptical founder.

The instinct is to gate the steps that feel risky. That produces a pipeline gated on instinct — impossible to justify, and it reliably gates the wrong things: heavy review on a copy change, none on a schema migration.

The mechanical rule instead: a step can be automated if undoing it is cheap, and must be gated if undoing it is expensive.

The corollary that unlocks most of the pipeline

You can convert an irreversible step into a reversible one by investing in the undo. Deploying to production is irreversible if rollback takes an engineer twenty minutes. It is reversible if a canary auto-rolls-back on an SLO breach within seconds.

So how much of the pipeline can safely run without a human is not a fixed property of software delivery. It is a direct function of how much you've invested in reversibility — which turns "do we trust the agent?" into an engineering question with an answer.

What "fully AI driven" can honestly mean

There is a well-described delegation gap: AI touches a large share of engineering work, but the fraction that can be handed over completely — no review, no harness, no verification — is far smaller. Figures circulate around 60% touched against 0–20% fully delegable; treat those as directional rather than precise, because we could not confirm them against a primary source.

A pipeline is the harness. With a spec constraining the target, tests defining done, bounded loops preventing drift and automated rollback making failure cheap, the delegable fraction rises considerably — because most of what makes a task undelegable is the absence of a way to verify it.

Pitch it as "two gates, chosen by a stated rule, with the boundary moving as we earn it." A pipeline sold as needing no humans gets shut down permanently the first time it ships something wrong.

The eleven stages

State lives in files, issue labels and PR status — never in agent memory. Every stage is resumable, inspectable and auditable.
