Structure

What's shared, where it lives, how it's consumed

Repository layout — one repo, two toolchains 
src/                          # ── the .NET solution
  Acme.Domain/                # ← domain model + business rules. THE source of truth
                              #   for what a Member, CarePlan or Benefit is.
  Acme.Application/           # ← use cases. References Domain only.
  Acme.Infrastructure/        # ← EF Core, FHIR client, Azure SDKs. Nothing
                              #   above this layer may reference it directly.
  Acme.Api/                   # ← ASP.NET Core. Emits openapi.json at build.
  Acme.Domain.Tests/          # ← xUnit, incl. NetArchTest boundary rules
apps/
  web-member/                 # member-facing React app
  web-admin/                  # internal coordinator console
packages/
  tokens/                     # ← DTCG JSON + generated themes. Brand truth.
  ui/                         # ← components. shadcn primitives + ours
  api-client/                 # ← GENERATED from openapi.json. Never hand-edited.
  utils/                      # ← pure helpers. No React, no I/O, no side effects.
  config/                     # ← eslint, tsconfig, tailwind, prettier presets
  testing/                    # ← fixtures, factories, test utilities
contracts/
  openapi.json                # ← GENERATED from Acme.Api at build time
  events/                     # ← event schemas, hand-authored, own owners
tools/
  catalogue/                  # the two walkers + the merge step
specs/                        # EARS requirements + .feature files
.github/workflows/            # the shared CI definition

Where a Member actually lives — the question the original draft left open

This document's governing rule is that every fact has exactly one hand-edited source. Member data is the fact most worth getting right and the easiest to end up defining three times — once in the domain model, once in the database schema, once as a FHIR resource. So, explicitly:

  • Acme.Domain is the source of truth for shape and rules. Hand-written C#. If you want to know what a CarePlan is, this is the answer.
  • The database schema is generated from it — EF Core migrations, reviewed as diffs. Not a second definition; a projection of the first.
  • openapi.json is generated from the API surface, and packages/api-client is generated from that. Two more projections. Both are build outputs, and CI fails when either is stale.
  • FHIR is the exchange format, not the internal model. This is the correction that matters. Storing and reasoning in FHIR resources internally sounds elegant and produces a domain model shaped by a wire format designed for interoperability rather than by our business rules. Map to and from FHIR at the boundary — the Firely SDK does exactly this — and keep clinical resources that genuinely belong in a FHIR server in Health Data Services, with operational data in Postgres.

The test to apply: if a field's meaning changes, how many files does a human edit? The answer must be one. If it is more than one, one of the copies is about to become a lie that tooling still believes.

Every shared home, and the rule that keeps it clean
WhereContainsThe rule that keeps it clean
Acme.DomainThe domain model and business rules — the single hand-written definition of a Member, CarePlan, BenefitNothing generated lives here, and nothing here references anything else. No EF Core attributes, no FHIR types, no Azure SDK. If it needs an external type, the mapping belongs a layer out.
Acme.InfrastructureEF Core context and migrations, the Firely FHIR client, Azure SDK usageThe only place that talks to anything outside the process. Enforced by a NetArchTest rule, not by convention.
contracts/openapi.jsonThe API surface, emitted from Acme.Api at build timeGenerated. Never hand-edited. Its own CODEOWNERS, because a change here is Gate 2 on the pipeline rather than an incidental edit inside a feature PR. oasdiff fails the build on a breaking change.
packages/api-clientThe typed TypeScript client and query hooks, generated from openapi.jsonGenerated. Never hand-edited, and CI fails if the committed output no longer matches the spec. No app calls the API by hand — this is the one place auth, retry and telemetry are added.
@acme/tokensDTCG JSON source plus the generated Tailwind theme, CSS variables and any future native themeThe JSON is hand-edited; everything else is generated. CI fails on a stale build or an AA contrast regression.
@acme/uiAll components, in three tiers (below)The only package allowed to render raw interactive HTML.
@acme/utilsPure front-end functions only — formatting, parsing, date mathsNo React, no network, no I/O. And before adding one, check the catalogue for a C# counterpart — duplicated formatting logic across the boundary is the most common cross-language duplication and no tool will catch it.
@acme/configESLint, tsconfig, Prettier presets, and the Tailwind preset that consumes the generated themeHow every TypeScript-side standard gets enforced once. Apps extend, never redefine — and never hand-write a colour.
Directory.Build.propsNullable, warnings-as-errors, analyser package references, language versionThe C# twin of @acme/config, and just as load-bearing. Solution-wide so no project can quietly opt out. The parity check compares this against the ESLint preset.
@acme/testing and Acme.TestKitFixtures, object factories, custom matchers — one per languageStops every test file inventing its own member object. Two of them is unavoidable; the synthetic member they build must come from the same generator, or the two halves of the suite test different fictional people.

The component library, in three tiers

What goes where
  • Primitives — from shadcn: Button, Input, Dialog, Select. Copied in, owned by you, accessible by default. Don't write these.
  • Composed — your combinations: FormField, DataTable, PageHeader, EmptyState
  • Domain — healthcare-specific: MemberCard, CarePlanTimeline, BenefitBadge, DischargeStatus
How it's consumed
  • Workspace import: import { Button } from '@acme/ui'
  • Never a relative path into the package — lint-enforced
  • Tailwind theme is generated from the design tokens, never hand-written (below)
  • Storybook as the human-facing catalogue
  • catalogue.json as the agent-facing one

Design tokens — one source, everything generated from it

Tokens are the colours, spacing, type scale, radii and shadows that define the brand. The only question that matters is where the authoritative copy lives, because the failure mode is always the same: two definitions of the same colour, drifting.

Hold them as W3C DTCG JSON — the Design Tokens Community Group format — and generate everything else from it on every build.

packages/tokens/ — the generation chain 
tokens/
  color.json        # DTCG format — the ONLY hand-edited file
  space.json
  type.json
      │
      ▼  build step (Style Dictionary or equivalent)
generated/
  tailwind.theme.ts   # consumed by @acme/config
  tokens.css          # CSS custom properties
  tokens.native.ts    # if React Native ever happens
      │
      ▼  CI
  fails if generated/ is stale, or if any AA contrast pair regresses

Why generated rather than hand-written alongside

A hand-written tailwind.config.ts sitting next to a token file is two sources of truth for one fact. They agree on the day they're written and diverge quietly from then on — someone tweaks a hex in Tailwind because a screen looked slightly off, and the token file is now a lie that a design tool still believes.

It's the same rule this whole document runs on, applied to a different layer: the reuse catalogue is generated because a hand-written index goes stale; contracts have one owner because two definitions of a Member drift. Tokens are no different, and they're the one people skip.

The bonus: DTCG is platform-neutral. If a React Native app ever happens, tokens generate a native theme from the same source. Components would need rebuilding; the design language wouldn't.

If a design firm is producing the library

Commissioning components externally is a good trade — brand quality is a craft in its own right, and it's not what an engineering team should be discovering. But the delivery has to arrive as something we own and can change, not a codebase we inherit and are afraid of. Four conditions, all worth writing into the brief:

  • A standalone, versioned library — plus an example app that consumes it as a published dependency. Not a demo importing the source. This one test is what separates a real library from a website with nice buttons in it, and if they can't build it, extracting a library later lands on us.
  • Built on headless primitives — Radix, React Aria or Ark UI. They supply keyboard handling, focus management and screen-reader behaviour; the firm supplies our look on top. The fiddly accessibility work then stays maintained upstream rather than becoming ours.
  • Tokens delivered as DTCG JSON, icons as SVG sources, guidelines as Markdown — all in a repository we control. Source formats, not exports. A Figma link is not a deliverable.
  • WCAG 2.1 AA contrast proven across the token set before sign-off — see the note below. This is the condition most likely to be missed and the most expensive to fix.

Once it lands, it becomes @acme/ui and lives under the same rules as everything else here: catalogued, lint-enforced, reviewed.

Headless primitives are the hard half of accessibility — not all of it

Radix and React Aria give you behaviour: focus order, keyboard interaction, ARIA wiring, screen-reader semantics. That is genuinely the expensive half, and taking it from upstream is the right call.

What they cannot give you is colour contrast — and contrast comes from the brand palette, which is exactly what a design firm is producing. A palette that fails AA is one of the most common audit findings and one of the worst to receive late, because fixing it means the brand changes, not the code.

So: run automated contrast checks over the token set as a delivery condition, not after integration. Every foreground/background pair the design system permits must clear 4.5:1 for body text and 3:1 for large text and interface components. It's a script over a JSON file — cheap to ask for now, and the alternative is telling a founder his brand colours don't pass six months after the logo went on the door.

Wire the same check into CI so a later token change can't silently regress it.

On "an AI-managed Tailwind framework" — I'd push back

Don't build your own primitive layer. The accessibility work inside Radix and React Aria is years of focused effort — keyboard interaction, focus management, ARIA, screen-reader behaviour across browser and AT combinations. A hand-rolled Button that looks right and traps focus wrong is worse than no component library, and for a Medicare population it's a genuine harm.

The "AI-managed" part that is valuable is the surrounding machinery, not the primitives: a machine-readable catalogue, a docs block per component the agent reads, a skill listing what exists, and a hook that blocks raw elements. That's the layer to build. shadcn is the right base precisely because the components live in your repo — you own them and can enforce your standards on them.

Testing

What you build decides what proves it

The most common question a new engineer or an agent asks, and the one most codebases never answer — so everything drifts to end-to-end.

The layering contract

Every kind of thing has exactly one default test type, and a time budget with a real number on it. Without this contract, end-to-end becomes the layer people pick when they don't know where their test belongs — feedback time goes, batch size follows it, and every control that assumes small batches quietly stops working.

It costs nothing: it is a convention written before the first test exists. It is also one of only two things on this page that cannot be fixed later without a rewrite.

Build this → prove it this way
You're buildingDefault proofRuns inAlso required
Pure function, helper, formatterUnit, in-memory — xUnit or Vitest, whichever language it's in< 10msNothing. If it needs more than this, it isn't pure — move it. And check the catalogue before writing it: a formatter that exists in the other language is the most common cross-boundary duplicate.
Domain type or business rule
C# — the authoritative one		Unit — xUnit, valid and invalid cases, both< 10msEvery rule ships with at least one rejection test. This is where the business logic is tested, once — not repeated on the front end against a generated type.
Validator at a boundary
FluentValidation or Zod		Unit — valid and invalid cases, both< 10msA validator nobody tested against bad input is decoration. Both sides need this, and it is one of the few genuinely duplicated tests — accept it, because the two guard different doors.
React componentIn-memory render — Testing Library, queried by role< 100msaxe-core assertion on every component with interactive elements.
Hook / state logicIn-memory — no DOM, no network< 50msNothing.
API endpoint or application serviceIntegration — WebApplicationFactory against a real Postgres in a container, no mocked repository< 2sAn authorisation test for every endpoint touching member data. No exceptions — this is the row that catches the most common serious finding in AI-written code.
The API contract itselfGenerated-client staleness check plus oasdiff< 5sNew because of the language split. The client must regenerate to a byte-identical result, and a breaking change must fail the build. This is the test that keeps the boundary type-safe.
Cross-service callContract test — consumer expectations recorded, provider verifies< 1sBreaking-change detection on the published schema.
Database migrationUp-and-down against a restored snapshot< 30sProof the previous application version still starts against the new schema. This is the expand/contract check, and it is the only one on this list that cannot be replaced by reading the diff carefully.
A user-facing journeyEnd-to-end — Playwright, driving the Gherkin scenario from the spec< 30s eachOne per acceptance scenario. Not one per component, not one per route.
Anything on a Lane 3+ pathAll of the above, plus mutation testingNightlyCoverage means nothing without it, and AI-generated tests inflate coverage while asserting little.
Why Playwright and not WebdriverIO — and where that flips

Playwright for everything web. One idiomatic way to do things means measurably better first-pass generated code; the trace viewer and screenshots give an agent readable evidence to iterate against; and getByRole only works on accessible markup, so the suite doubles as an accessibility forcing function we need anyway.

WebdriverIO is the right answer for exactly one thing: native mobile via Appium. If a member-facing native app happens, that suite is WebdriverIO and the two coexist. Don't adopt it pre-emptively for a web product — a second framework is a second set of conventions for an agent to get wrong.

The rule that keeps the pyramid upright

A test may only exist at a higher layer if it cannot be written at a lower one. The reviewer's question is never "is this tested?" but "is this tested here, and why not lower?"

The suite has a hard budget: ten minutes end to end. When it exceeds that, the fix is moving tests down the pyramid or running affected-only — never deleting coverage, and never accepting a slower loop. Feedback speed is the thing every other control depends on.

The one place that budget collides with another rule — plan for it now

Every acceptance scenario must have an end-to-end test, and no acceptance test can move down the pyramid, because being end-to-end is what makes it the product's contract. At roughly 30 seconds each, ten minutes of serial execution is about twenty scenarios — which a team shipping two or three scenarios a feature reaches inside a few months. The two rules are not in tension by accident; they are in tension by construction.

The resolution is parallelism and scope, decided up front rather than during the panic. Shard the suite across CI workers so wall-clock stays under ten minutes while total execution grows; run only the scenarios whose service was touched on a pull request; run the complete suite on merge to main and nightly. The ten-minute budget is a wall-clock promise about the pull-request loop, not a cap on how many scenarios may exist — say it that way, because said the other way it eventually becomes an argument for having fewer acceptance tests, which is the one outcome nobody wants.

What makes a generated test acceptable

AI writes most of these. A test that asserts nothing is worse than no test, because it manufactures false confidence and inflates the number people look at.

  • It fails when the behaviour is wrong. The only check that matters — and the reviewer's job is to satisfy themselves of it, not to count tests.
  • It asserts on behaviour, not implementation. A test coupled to internals fails on every refactor and stops anyone refactoring.
  • Its name states the requirement, tagged to the R-number from the spec, so the chain from requirement to proof is traceable.
  • It creates and tears down its own state. The first test that depends on data another test created is the start of a suite nobody trusts, and the pattern is close to unrecoverable once it spreads.
  • Assertion density is sampled in CI — a test file with more setup lines than assertions is flagged for review, not failed automatically.
The platform

The paved road, specified

Changing defaults is the mechanism by which culture changes — but nothing says what has to be on the road. This is that list, in capability terms.

Seven things the platform has to provide
CapabilityWhat it has to provideHow it fails
1 · Test data Every test gets state it controls, created and torn down by the test itself. Synthetic by default; no real personal data in any environment, ever. Shared mutable fixtures. The first test that depends on data another test created is the start of a suite nobody trusts — and the pattern is close to unrecoverable once it spreads.
2 · Environments A named way to get one, how long it lives, and how far it sits from production. At this size the honest answer is usually ephemeral per change, or none at all. One permanent shared environment. It becomes a queue, then a bottleneck, then the reason people batch their changes — which defeats every control here that assumes small batches.
3 · The layering contract
a convention, not a build		 What runs at which layer, what each layer may touch, and a time budget per layer with an actual number on it. The table is above → No contract, so everything drifts to end-to-end — the layer an author picks when they don't know where their test belongs. Feedback time goes, and batch size follows it.
4 · Result storage
a schema, not a build		 Every run's results, per test, keyed to the commit, kept long enough to show a trend. This is the third join in the telemetry contract. Results live only in CI console output on a 30-day retention. Flaky rate, mutation score and feedback time all become unobtainable — and unlike everything else here, this one cannot be backfilled.
5 · Flake handling Detection across runs, automatic quarantine, and a hard expiry on the quarantine. Quarantine with no expiry becomes a graveyard, and the suite quietly stops covering what it still claims to.
6 · Local / CI parity An engineer can run, locally, the exact thing that failed in CI. They can't, so they debug by pushing commits. The feedback loop becomes one pipeline long whatever the pipeline duration says — and feedback speed beats completeness stops being true.
7 · Non-functional hooks A defined place where performance, accessibility, security and resilience checks attach — defined while it is still empty. No hook, so each of them needs a project of its own and gets deferred indefinitely. The one a new build is most likely never to route to at all.

Two of the seven decide the rest

The layering contract, because without it everything becomes an end-to-end test and feedback speed is gone before anyone notices it going. And result storage, because it is the only item here that cannot be added retrospectively — a test run that was never recorded is not recoverable, in the same way and for the same reason as provenance.

Both are free: one is a convention written before the first test exists, the other a schema decided as part of the telemetry contract. The remaining five are engineering time, and they are most of what the platform budget on the cost tab actually pays for.

Where the hands-on version lives, and why it isn't here

This is the capability list. The implementation — runner config, fixture patterns, directory layout, the test-data service interface, CI stage definitions — is stack-specific and has a far shorter half-life than anything else in this document. Put it here and the founding decisions rot at its pace.

It belongs in the repository it serves, versioned with the code that depends on it. Note what that does and doesn't contradict: it rules out a test strategy document — the kind written instead of building controls. It does not rule out a build spec for the platform, which the cost tab already budgets for. Write that one when there is a stack to write it about.

Enforcement

Every standard, and what enforces it in each language

A rule with no mechanism is a preference. This is the mechanism column — and because the codebase is two languages, it is two mechanism columns, which is the whole reason mitigation 2 exists.

Mitigation 2 lives here — read this table as a checklist, not a description

A rule counts as adopted only when both cells are filled. A rule wired on the TypeScript side and not the C# side is not a standard; it is a standard on the front end and a preference on the back end, and nobody will notice which until an incident makes it obvious.

So a CI job compares the two rule sets and fails when they diverge — when the ESLint preset gains a rule with no counterpart in Directory.Build.props or the analyser set, or the reverse. That parity check is a genuinely small piece of work and it is the single thing that keeps a two-language codebase from becoming two codebases. It is step 14 in the build plan; do not defer it, because the divergence it prevents is invisible until it is expensive.

Where a row honestly has no counterpart — accessibility has no meaning in a C# API — the cell says n/a rather than being left blank, so an empty cell always means "not done yet".

Requirement → the C# mechanism → the TypeScript mechanism
RequirementC# sideTypeScript sideEnforced at

packages/config/eslint/standards.jsthe load-bearing rules — TypeScript side 
module.exports = {
  rules: {
    // ---- Type safety ----
    '@typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any': 'error',
    '@typescript-eslint/no-unsafe-assignment': 'error',
    '@typescript-eslint/no-non-null-assertion': 'error',

    // ---- No fifteenth button ----
    'no-restricted-syntax': ['error',
      { selector: 'JSXOpeningElement[name.name=/^(button|input|select|textarea|dialog)$/]',
        message: 'Use the component from @acme/ui. Raw interactive elements ' +
                 'are only allowed inside the ui package.' },
    ],

    // ---- Readability ----
    'complexity': ['error', { max: 12 }],
    'max-lines-per-function': ['error', { max: 80, skipBlankLines: true }],
    'max-lines': ['error', { max: 400, skipBlankLines: true }],

    // ---- Comments ----
    // Comment hygiene is two custom rules, not core ESLint. 'no-warning-comments'
    // cannot express "a TODO with an issue link is fine" — it only bans terms —
    // so using it here would reject the documented-good TODO format below.
    '@acme/no-historical-comments': 'error',   // see the rule source further down
    '@acme/todo-needs-link': 'error',          // TODO(ACME-412, 2026-09): ...
  },

  overrides: [
    // Generated output is exempt from readability caps and comment rules.
    // Without this block CI fails on the committed API client and Tailwind
    // theme, which the design REQUIRES to be generated and checked in.
    { files: ['**/generated/**', 'packages/api-client/src/**'],
      rules: {
        'max-lines': 'off',
        'max-lines-per-function': 'off',
        'complexity': 'off',
        '@acme/no-historical-comments': 'off',
      } },
  ],
};
Two mistakes worth not repeating, because both fail silently in the right direction

Core no-warning-comments cannot express the TODO policy. It bans terms outright, so setting it to error on todo rejects every TODO — including the linked-and-dated format this document holds up as correct. The policy needs a custom rule that requires the link, not a core rule that forbids the word. Ship the core rule and you get a team that deletes TODOs rather than filing issues, which is strictly worse than what you had.

Generated files need an explicit exemption. The design requires the OpenAPI client and the Tailwind theme to be generated and committed. A generated client is routinely thousands of lines, so max-lines fails the build on an artifact nobody wrote and nobody should edit. The overrides block above is not tidying — without it, CI is red on a clean checkout.

The as escape hatch

Banning type assertions outright is too blunt — there are legitimate uses at boundaries. The workable rule is an assertion must carry a comment saying why the compiler can't know. That keeps the escape hatch available and makes every use visible in review. Same pattern as the synthetic-data override: don't remove the hatch, make it loud and logged.

The four places a standard can live — and which to use

Every rule in this document is applied by one of four mechanisms. Choosing the wrong one is the difference between a standard that holds and a standard that gets described in an onboarding session and then decays.

Ordered by how binding each is
MechanismWhat it isUse it forWeakness
CI checkA job that fails the build.Anything objectively decidable. The default — if a rule can live here, it lives here.Feedback arrives minutes after the mistake rather than at the moment of it.
HookA script the agent runs at a defined point, which can block the action outright.Things that must never happen at all: real data entering the repo, tests being edited to make them pass, pushing straight to main.Local only. Every hook must mirror a CI check, never replace one — a hook alone is bypassable.
SkillInstructions the agent loads on demand when the task matches.Things requiring judgment the agent should apply consistently: which test type to write, how to structure a component, the known-bad list.Advisory. It shapes what gets produced; it cannot guarantee it.
Instruction fileAlways-loaded prose.Almost nothing. Only what's true of every task in the repository, in a few lines.Costs tokens every session, and adherence falls as it grows — a long file causes rules inside it to be ignored.

Move rules down this list, not up

The instinct when a standard is missed is to write it down more prominently. That is moving up the list — toward prose, toward advisory, toward decay. The fix is always the opposite: can this be a skill so the agent applies it by default? Can it be a CI check so it's decided rather than debated?

This is also the answer to the context-versus-standards tension. Every rule moved out of prose and into a check costs zero tokens and becomes binding. Shrinking the instruction file strengthens enforcement rather than weakening it.

Every standard, and the mechanism that carries it

What an agent loads, what blocks it, and what proves it
StandardSkill the agent loadsHook that blocksCI check that proves it
Does this already exist?reuse-analysis — reads the generated catalogue, maps every capability to reuse / extend / new-becauserequire-reuse — blocks Lane 2+ edits with no reuse.mdDuplication threshold; unjustified "new" fails the stage
Which test do I write?testing-contract — the build-this/prove-it table, applied to the file being changed—Every scenario maps to a test; suite budget; assertion-density sampling
Component conventionsui-conventions — tiers, tokens, which primitive to build on—Raw interactive elements banned outside the ui package; token contrast gate
Type safetyboundary-validation — where a schema is required and why—Strict compiler settings; any as error; assertions need a written reason
Comment hygieneIncluded in the base standard — describe what the code does now—Historical-narration lint rule; no commented-out code; TODOs need a link
Accessibilityui-conventions carries the role-first rule—jsx-a11y as errors; axe assertions; contrast across the token set
No real data anywheresynthetic-data — how to generate a member, a discharge summary, an ADT messageno-prod-data, no-real-records — block at the moment of writingSecret scanning; PHI-shaped-data scan on every diff
Tests prove behaviourIncluded in testing-contractno-test-edit — blocks editing a test during implementation unless the task is a test taskFails any PR touching both an assertion and its code under test without a spec change
Migrations are reversiblemigration-safety — expand/contract, and what a compatible migration looks like—Destructive operations blocked without a contract-phase label; routed to Lane 4
