Agents can't run away—Iteration cap, budget cap, no-progress detection in the stage harnessCost telemetry per stage; escalation rate tracked
Reviews are substantiveexplain-diff — walks the reviewer through the change and asks them to summarise it—Approval requires a written sentence; bare approve rejected; twice-yearly blind audit
What AI gets wrong hereknown-bad — the list, loaded before any generation in an affected area—Not mechanically checkable. Reviewed and updated at each retro.
Why the skills column matters more than it looks

A CI check catches a mistake after it's made. A skill means the mistake is less likely to be made at all — the agent already knows to write an in-memory test for a pure function, already knows the known-bad list, already knows to check the catalogue first.

The skills and the CI checks are the same standard expressed twice, deliberately. The skill makes the right thing the default output; the check makes the wrong thing impossible to merge. Neither alone is sufficient: skills without checks decay silently, and checks without skills produce a loop where the agent generates, fails, and retries.

One rule about where these live

Skills, hooks and the shared CI workflow live in one repository, versioned, reviewed through the normal pull request process, with named owners. They are production configuration with access to real systems — treat them as scratch work and they will behave like scratch work.

Improving a skill improves every future change in the estate. That makes this the highest-leverage directory in the company, and the one where a careless edit does the most damage — which is exactly why it goes through review like anything else, and why prompt changes run against the golden set before merge.

Comments

Comment hygiene — the rule most teams never enforce

You called this out specifically, and you're right to. Stale comments are worse than no comments, because they're actively believed.

The five rules
RuleBadGood
Explain why, never what// loop over the members// Sequential: the FHIR store rate-limits concurrent writes.
No historical narration// removed the retry, no longer neededNothing. Git holds the history.
No commented-out code// const old = doThing()Delete it. It's in git.
TODOs carry a link and a date// TODO: fix this properly// TODO(ACME-412, 2026-09): remove once v2 contract lands
Match surrounding densityA heavily commented file in a codebase that doesn't commentConsistent with neighbours
packages/config/eslint/rules/no-historical-comments.js 
// Flags comments that narrate the past rather than explain the present.
// Agents produce these constantly — they describe the edit they just made
// rather than the code that now exists.

// One line, deliberately: a JS regex literal cannot span newlines. Wrapping
// this for readability turns it into a syntax error, which is exactly the kind
// of "looks fine in a document" bug this whole page exists to prevent.
const HISTORICAL = /\b(removed?|deleted?|used to|no longer|previously|formerly|changed from|was\s+(using|doing)|instead of the old|legacy approach)\b/i;

module.exports = {
  meta: { type: 'problem', docs: { description: 'No historical narration in comments' } },
  create(context) {
    return {
      Program() {
        for (const c of context.getSourceCode().getAllComments()) {
          if (HISTORICAL.test(c.value)) {
            context.report({
              node: c,
              message:
                'Comment narrates a past change. Comments describe the code ' +
                'that exists now — git holds the history. Rewrite to explain ' +
                'why this code is the way it is, or delete it.',
            });
          }
        }
      },
    };
  },
};

Why this needs an agent as well as a lint rule

The regex catches the blatant cases — and agents produce those constantly, because they narrate the edit they just made rather than describing the code that now exists. But it can't catch a comment that's simply wrong: accurate-sounding, well-formed, and describing behaviour the code no longer has.

So pair it with a comment-hygiene lens on the reviewer subagent, checking every comment in the diff against the code beside it. Mechanical rules for the obvious, a model for the subtle. Same division of labour as everywhere else in this design.

Comprehension

Making sure developers actually understand the code

You named this explicitly, and it's a different goal from quality. It's about whether the team can still operate the system in a year.

The risk in a heavily agentic pipeline isn't bad code — the verification layer handles that. It's a team that ships software it cannot explain. Approval becomes a reflex, knowledge never forms, and the first serious production incident finds nobody who knows how anything works.

The sharpest single mechanism: approve by summarising

A reviewer cannot approve with a click. They approve by writing one sentence saying what changed and why, in the approval comment.

It costs about thirty seconds and it is impossible to do without having read the diff. Everything else on this page is enforced by machines; this one is enforced by the fact that you can't fake it. It also leaves a plain-English trail on every PR, which is separately useful when someone reads the history in eighteen months.

Supporting mechanisms
MechanismWhat it protects against
PR size cap in CINobody understands a 2,000-line diff, and pretending otherwise is how rubber-stamping starts
Spec attached to every PRThe reviewer sees intent, not just diff — reviewing against a stated requirement is far easier than inferring one
Reviewer from the owning teamKnowledge lands where it's needed, not in whoever was free
Reviewer rotation within the teamStops one person becoming the only human who understands a subsystem
ADRs for architectural decisionsThe why outlives the people who decided it
Reviewer subagent runs firstThe human spends their attention on intent and fit, not on hunting for a null check
The metric that tells you it's failing

Watch time-to-approve alongside PR size. If approvals consistently land within a couple of minutes of a PR opening, nobody is reading — the process has become theatre and you'll find out during an incident. That's a leading indicator; escaped defects are a lagging one.

Sequencing

Build order for the standards layer

Ordered by what blocks what
#BuildWhy here
1@acme/config — the shared presetsEvery other standard is enforced through this. Nothing else works until it exists.
2Nx workspace + tags + boundary rulesArchitecture is cheapest to enforce before there's architecture to fix
3Acme.Domain + the generated clientThe moment two places hand-define a Member, drift has started. One is hand-written, the other generated from it — and CI fails when the generated one is stale.
4@acme/tokens — DTCG source + generator + contrast gateBefore @acme/ui, because components consume the generated theme. A component built against a hand-written colour is a component to redo.
5@acme/ui with shadcn primitives + raw-element banBefore the first feature ships, or you get the fifteen buttons
6Duplication detection in CIBaseline it early so the trend line means something
7Catalogue generator + reuse skillNeeds packages to catalogue — but before the codebase is large enough to search by hand
8Reuse stage + require-reuse hookOnce the catalogue is real enough to be worth consulting
9Comment-hygiene rule + reviewer lensAdd when you've seen the actual failure patterns in your own diffs

The one that must come first

@acme/config, on day one, before the first feature. It's the single place every rule on this page is expressed, and the reason a standard can be added once and apply everywhere.

The failure mode if you skip it: each app grows its own ESLint config, they drift, and enforcing anything new means eight pull requests and an argument. That's the same call-don't-copy principle as the shared CI workflow — and it fails the same way, quietly and irreversibly, the first time someone copies instead of extending.

This is the same sequence as Build plan, viewed from the standards side rather than the calendar side. Rows 1–2 are step 14, row 4 is step 23, rows 3 and 5 are step 24, rows 6–8 are step 31, and row 9 is step 32. The build plan, in dependency order →

Evidence layer

How we know any of this is working

Controls tell you what you tried to prevent. This tells you whether it worked — and it is the half most engineering plans skip entirely, because nothing fails a build when it's missing.

The short versionFull tab: ~24 min · this box: 1 min

  • Three joins decided once, before the first commit. Deploys carry the commit, incidents carry the deploy, test results are stored per run against the commit. This is the single decision that keeps everything downstream cheap.
  • Choosing the metric and building the thing that emits it are the same decision here. Established organisations pick metrics from a framework, find the data doesn't exist, then quietly substitute whatever is measurable. We have the opposite problem and the easier one.
  • Seven metrics is the whole set under fifty engineers. Each with a written definition before it appears anywhere. Resist adding to it.
  • Requiring review is one setting; defining what a review is decides whether the control does anything. Four criteria in the PR template, and a blind audit twice a year to check it hasn't quietly become an approval.
  • The quality function is four capabilities, not a team — and three of the four aren't test-related at all. One person holds all four part-time for a long time.
The contract

Three joins, decided once

The highest-value decision in this document that costs nothing, and the one that cannot be retrofitted cheaply.

Why this is the advantage of starting from nothing

In an established organisation the first and most expensive phase of any quality programme is archaeology: working out what data exists, whether it can be joined, and whether the controls already in place do anything. It routinely turns out to be an instrumentation programme wearing a quality programme's clothes.

We skip that entirely. The telemetry is designed into the pipeline as the pipeline is built, so questions everyone else spends a quarter answering retrospectively are answerable by construction. That advantage has a shelf life of about eighteen months. Spend it.

The three joins — one schema decision
JoinWhat it meansWhat it unlocksIf skipped
Deploy → commitEvery deploy record carries the commit SHA it shipped.Lead time, deployment frequency, and the ability to say which change is live.Backfillable with effort, from tags. Undercounts, but survivable.
Incident → deployEvery incident record carries the deploy it relates to.Change failure rate and recovery time, computed rather than estimated.Backfillable only by hand, and only while people still remember.
Test result → commitEvery run's results, per test, keyed to the commit, retained long enough to show a trend.Flaky rate, pipeline feedback time, mutation score.Not backfillable at all. A test run that was never recorded is gone, in the same way and for the same reason as provenance.
The specific thing that kills the third join

Results living only in CI console output on a 30-day retention. It looks fine for a quarter, and then flaky rate, feedback time and mutation score are all permanently unobtainable. Storing results is a schema decision at stage one, not a reporting feature to add later.

Provenance — the fourth thing that cannot be recovered

A commit trailer recording whether a change was AI-authored, plus a CI check that it's present. It is one line of convention and it is what makes every AI-specific control measurable: without it, "are AI-authored changes actually being reviewed?" has no answer except a manual sample.

.gitmessage / commit trailer 
Assisted-by: claude-opus-5
Assisted-scope: implementation      # or: tests | docs | none
Reviewed-by: <human>

# CI check: every commit on a feature branch carries Assisted-by.
#
# MERGE STRATEGY MATTERS. Squash-merge discards branch commit messages and
# replaces them with the squash message, so trailers written on the branch do
# NOT reach main. Provenance is called unrecoverable everywhere in this plan,
# so pick one and configure it on day one:
#   (a) merge commits, preserving branch history and its trailers, or
#   (b) squash-merge with the PR body seeded from the branch trailers, and a
#       CI check that the SQUASH message carries Assisted-by.
# (b) is fine and is the common choice. What is not fine is enabling squash
# and checking trailers on the branch, which passes CI and loses the data.
# Value may be "none" — absence of the trailer fails, not the value.
Why the value may be "none"

Requiring the trailer rather than requiring AI use keeps it a diagnostic. The moment anyone is measured on the share of AI-authored code, the number stops being honest and the diagnostic is destroyed. Provenance exists to answer "was this reviewed appropriately for how it was written" — nothing else.

Gates

Controls in the pipeline, not in a meeting

Every control automated, evidence-producing and risk-weighted. Uniform gates are the reason gates get bypassed.

Tier assigned automatically from the change surface — never self-declared
TierExamplesControls
Lane 1 · LowCopy change, internal tooling behind a flag, documentationAutomated checks only. Ship on green, no human in the path.
Lane 2 · MediumStandard feature workAutomated checks + AI review + one human review. Progressive rollout.
Lane 3 · HighAuth, payments, personal data, anything with a migrationAll the above, plus a second reviewer, a specialist review, an explicit rollback plan, and staged release with monitoring gates. Below about fifteen engineers the second reviewer comes from anywhere in engineering, not from the owning team — see the note below, because the arithmetic does not work otherwise.
Lane 4 · High + named approvalMember data, clinical logicEverything in Lane 3, plus a named human approval before production. Rollback restores the system, not the person.
The review rules need a team-size clause, or they are unenforceable on day one

Four rules in this document are each individually sensible and collectively impossible at eight engineers. One owning team per module. Teams review their own code. Lane 3 needs two reviewers from the owning team. Reviewers rotate within the team. Split eight engineers across the API, the member app and the admin console and an owning team is two or three people — so "two from the owning team" means everyone on it reviews everything, and rotation has nothing to rotate between.

So the rules carry a size threshold, stated now rather than discovered. Below roughly fifteen engineers: one reviewer from the owning area, and Lane 3's second reviewer comes from anywhere in engineering. Rotation is an aspiration, not a rule, and the thing it protects against — one person being the only human who understands a subsystem — is instead handled by the ADRs and the approve-by-summarising trail. Above fifteen, the rules above apply as written.

This matters more than it looks because a control that cannot be satisfied gets quietly ignored, and the habit of ignoring it survives the growth that made it satisfiable. Better to state the small-team version and graduate deliberately.

Why four lanes rather than the three tiers the quality plan proposed

They are the same idea at different resolutions and they nest cleanly, so we kept both: lanes are the routing label on the issue; tiers are the control sets. Lane 4 exists because a Medicare product has a category where rollback genuinely doesn't undo the harm — and that deserves its own line rather than being folded into "high".

The quality knowledge layer

What separates a real capability from a pile of tools, and the reason two teams with identical tooling get different results.

Make the quality context machine-accessible

Architecture decisions, test strategy, domain rules, past incidents, known failure modes, service ownership. DORA names AI-accessible internal data a top capability for exactly this reason: an agent with your context is worth an order of magnitude more than one with generic knowledge.

In practice, three things: MCP servers or equivalent over test management, incident and observability systems · a maintained corpus of architecture and domain docs · and a shared agent and prompt library treated as product code, with review and versioning.

This is the same argument as the shared library below, seen from the systems side rather than the people side. The library is what humans contribute; this is what the agent can reach on its own.

The menu

What AI buys you, and how each one fails

A menu, not an order of work — and deliberately not a lifecycle map, which is the pipeline tab. This is what each capability is worth, what it costs in data, and the specific way it goes wrong.

Ordered by return, with the failure mode named
CapabilityWhat it buysHow it failsWhen
AI code reviewCatches the logic and correctness classes behind the 1.7× issue rate, on every PR, before a human looks. Highest return on this page.Untuned, it becomes noise and people learn to ignore it. Track comment-acceptance rate — falling acceptance means fix the rules, not tolerate the noise.Stage 3
Test generation at authorshipRemoves the "I'll add tests later" gap by making the test the cheap part.Volume theatre — thousands of tests asserting nothing. Only mutation score and escaped defects tell you if it worked.Stage 2
Risk profiling of a changeTier assignment from the change surface rather than the author's judgement.Hardest data prerequisite on this page. Needs the telemetry contract. Skip that and this moves from quick win to build-the-data-first.Stage 3
Risk-based test selectionRuns the tests a change actually endangers, keeping feedback fast as the suite grows.Also needs incident history joined to changes. And don't reach for it early — only once the suite is slow enough to change behaviour.Stage 3
Bug triage and clusteringDuplicate detection and routing without a person reading every report.Becomes a QA activity by accident. It stays team-owned; the AI clusters, the team decides.Stage 3
Incident → risk feedbackIncidents automatically update risk tiering and the known-bad list. This is what makes it a system rather than a pile of controls.Needs roughly six months of history to say anything. Attempt it early and you tune against noise.Stage 4
Release confidence scoringSynthesises change risk, coverage of the changed surface, mutation score and incident correlation into one signal with its reasoning shown.Genuinely last, because it synthesises the others. Attempt it early and you get a score nobody trusts — then the gate comes back, wearing a dashboard.Stage 4
Three things the stage column makes visible

Most of this menu is stage three, and starting there rather than with the stage-one decisions is the failure mode — it's the tool-first adoption trap.

The two hardest data prerequisites are risk profiling and risk-based selection. Both need the telemetry contract. If that was skipped, they stop being quick wins.

Release confidence scoring is last for a reason. It depends on everything above it being real, which is exactly why it is the one most often attempted first.

Most urgent

Governing AI-generated code specifically

The controls that exist because of how the code was written, not what it does. These are the most urgent set in the document, because the risk profile they address is already measured.

Six controls, each targeting a measured failure
ControlWhat it doesWhy this one
Mandatory human reviewBranch protection on AI-authored change. Non-negotiable.Targets the defect classes the 1.7× issue rate is made of — logic, correctness, insecure data handling. A control, not a practice, which is why it can be mandated on day one while everything else is earned.
Provenance labellingAI authorship recorded at commit level.You cannot manage what you cannot attribute. Every other control in this table becomes measurable only because this one exists.
Duplication thresholds in CIFail the build above a set ratio.Up 81% since 2023 and completely invisible without instrumentation. Set the threshold while the number is small and the fix is cheap.
Weighted security scanningBias the ruleset toward AI-prevalent classes: injection, request forgery, broken access control, credential handling, unsafe deserialization.Generic scanning spreads attention evenly. The failure distribution is not even — up to 2.74× the security issues, concentrated in specific classes.
Generated-test auditingSample assertion quality; flag test files with more setup than assertions.A test that asserts nothing is worse than no test — it manufactures false confidence and inflates the number people look at.
Dependency provenanceLicence and supply-chain checks on AI-suggested libraries.Models suggest packages confidently, including ones that don't exist or carry incompatible licences. A confident suggestion is not a vetted one.

Data governance — non-negotiable, and easy to get wrong in the rush

Four rules, one of which isn't ours to write
RuleWhat it means
ClassificationClear rules on what data may reach which AI systems. This is the part security and legal own — consume it, don't write it yourself.
Synthetic data as the defaultFor every environment. Removes an entire class of privacy risk while unblocking testing, and industry use of it in testing nearly doubled in a year.
Retention and training-useStated explicitly, per tool. Does the vendor train on our code? Most teams have never checked.
Audit trailFor AI decisions in the quality path, especially anything that influenced a release. Automated and timestamped beats a signature in a spreadsheet.
Accountability, written down before it's needed

Every AI output in the quality path has a named human owner. AI recommends; humans decide. When something goes wrong — and it will — "who was accountable for accepting this?" needs an answer that isn't "the model".

The AI standard is two documents, not one

An AI standard usually stalls by being written as one monolithic document that then sits in legal review for weeks — when most of it never needed legal input.

What must be true — security, legal, data protection own it
  • Approved tools
  • What data may reach an external model
  • Retention and training-use terms
  • IP and licence position on generated code
  • Controls required for regulated paths

Our role: consume it. Not ours to waive.

How that's satisfied and enforced — engineering owns it
  • How each requirement is implemented
  • Hook versus CI stage
  • How risk tiers map to review depth
  • Prompt and context sharing
  • What gets measured

Our role: own it outright.

Three moves that stop this stalling

1 · Check what exists. The "must be true" rules often already exist, written for another purpose — acceptable-use policy, data classification, procurement checklist. Reference them; don't reopen them.

2 · Surface gaps, don't fill them. If they don't exist, raise it as a dependency rather than owning it. Data rules written without authority bind nobody and discharge nothing.

3 · Write our half immediately. The engineering practice standard doesn't need to wait. Sequencing it behind a legal review it doesn't require is the most common way this stalls before it starts.

Don't over-claim in the other direction either: pipeline controls frequently are security controls — secret scanning, dependency provenance, static analysis. The requirement is theirs; the implementation is ours.

Metrics

Measuring confidence, not activity

Activity metrics get gamed within one quarter, every time. These are the outcomes, plus a small number of leading indicators that predict them.

For every metric, settle three things before it appears anywhere: what emits it — name the system that writes the record; what joins it — most quality metrics link two systems; and who defines it in writing. An undefined metric on a shared dashboard becomes a target within a quarter, and is gone as a diagnostic.

The whole set under fifty engineers — resist adding to it
MetricDefinition — use these wordsSource and join
Deployment frequencyCount of successful deployments to production, per calendar week, per service.CI/CD job history where environment is production and status is success.
Lead time for changeMedian hours from first commit on a branch to that commit running in production.Version control + deploy records, joined on commit SHA.
Change failure rate
define this one first		Percentage of production deployments that required remediation within 48 hours — a rollback, a revert, or a hotfix deploy.Deploy records + revert detection, joined on service and time window.
Recovery timeMedian time from the deploy that triggered remediation to the remediating deploy reaching production.Deploy records only. No incident system required.
Escaped defect rateDefects reported within 30 days of a production deploy, per 100 merged pull requests. A defect is an issue requiring a code change to resolve — excluding feature requests, config changes and known-accepted limitations.Issue tracker label + merged-PR count.
Review coverage of AI-authored changePercentage of Lane 2+ merged PRs marked AI-assisted carrying at least one approval from someone other than the author. Lane 1 is excluded by design and the denominator says so — reporting this against all merges would show a permanent shortfall that looks like a failing control and is actually a stated exception.PR metadata + the provenance trailer + the lane label. Live from the first merged PR.
Cost per merged featureModel spend attributable to a change, divided by features merged.Pipeline telemetry tagged by stage.
The one that quietly breaks

Change failure rate means different things to different people unless it is pinned in words. The definition above is deliberately narrow and mechanical — a remediation event inside 48 hours, not a judgement about severity. Narrow and consistently applied beats broad and argued about. Write it down, put it where the dashboard is, and change it only with an announcement.

Read as a set, or not at all

The critical read is throughput up while change failure rate stays flat or falls. If failure rate rises with throughput we are trading quality for speed and the strategy has failed.

That is the documented default trajectory rather than a hypothetical — delivery instability rose across the industry alongside AI adoption while throughput improved. Assume we are exposed to it unless we are measuring it.

Quality outcomes — the second tier

What the delivery metrics can't tell you on their own
MetricDefinitionNote
Escaped defect rateProduction defects per unit of changeThe most honest quality metric there is.
Defect detection stageWhere in the lifecycle defects are foundThe actual evidence for shifting left — the distribution moving earlier is the whole point, not the total count.
Average bug ageMedian age of open defectsCheap, brutal, hard to game.
Cost of quality splitPrevention vs appraisal vs failureShould shift toward prevention over time. If it doesn't, the controls aren't preventing anything.
Customer-found vs internally-foundWho finds our bugs firstThe most direct measure of whether our signals work. Every customer-found defect is one our own pipeline had a chance at and missed.

Leading indicators — three of these need the third join

Watch these to predict the outcomes above
IndicatorWhy it matters
Pipeline feedback timeDirectly drives batch size and developer behaviour. Everything else in this document assumes small batches.
Flaky test rateThe trust metric. Above roughly 2% people stop believing the suite, and a suite nobody believes is worse than none.
Mutation score on critical pathsThe only real answer to "is our coverage meaningful?" — and AI made raw coverage meaningless overnight.
Code duplication trendEarly warning on maintainability decay, the slow failure nobody sees coming.
Comment-acceptance rate on AI reviewWhether the tuning works. Falling acceptance means people are learning to ignore the bot — fix the rules rather than tolerate the noise.

Metrics to actively resist

Useful as diagnostics; corrosive the moment anyone is measured on them
MetricWhy it's harmful
Code coverage as a targetGoodhart's law in its purest form.
Number of test casesRewards volume over value — and AI made it meaningless overnight.
Automation percentageMeasures activity, not risk reduction.
Defects found by QARewards late detection, the exact opposite of the strategy.
Individual defect countsDestroys psychological safety and guarantees under-reporting.
Share of code AI-generatedProvenance exists to make this a diagnostic. Measuring anyone on it destroys the diagnostic.
Tokens consumed per engineerActivity, trivially gamed. Fluency is whether output improved, not whether usage rose.

Record every metric as available (report the value and trend), proxy only (report under a different name with the limitation stated — never under the real metric's label) or not available (say so, and name what has to emit it). Here "not available" almost always means "not emitted yet", which makes it a ticket in the pipeline build rather than a business case. One named owner for the whole set; refresh weekly. No metric goes on a shared dashboard until its definition is written down.

Review

What a real review actually looks like

The control the evidence most directly supports, and the one most easily reduced to a rubber stamp.

Requiring review is one setting. Defining what it means is the part that decides whether the control does anything — and it is far harder to introduce once habits have formed. Both plans reached this independently: engineering because review is where comprehension either happens or quietly stops, quality because AI-assisted pull requests carry 1.7× the issues, concentrated exactly where a competent reviewer catches what a static analyser misses.

What a reviewer is accountable for
CriterionNot a reviewA review
Engagement with behaviour
the load-bearing one		No comment, or style and formatting only.At least one comment touching logic, edge cases or failure behaviour — the classes AI-assisted code most often gets wrong.
Effect on the codeMerged exactly as opened, every time.Review demonstrably changes behaviour, structure or test coverage often enough to be worth doing.
Attention proportionate to riskA large or high-tier diff approved in under a minute.Evidence the whole change was read — comments spanning files, or a named risk raised.
Reviewer fitReviewer has no context for the area and the change was non-trivial.Reviewer works in this code, or was chosen for the risk in it.
Put it in the template, not in a policy

Four lines a reviewer sees every time they open a change will shape behaviour. A standards document will not. This is change the default, not the opinion applied to the one control that most needs it.

Engineering adds one mechanism on top: approval requires a written sentence on what changed and why, and a bare approve is rejected by CI. Thirty seconds, impossible to fake without reading the diff, and it leaves a plain-English trail on every change.

Instrumenting it — "review substantiveness"

Once provenance and review metadata are live, the same question can be asked from data: are changes reviewed, or approved? No single number answers it, so use a composite — and treat each part as a signal, never as a target.

Report as a set, describe as indicative, target none individually
ComponentWhat it indicatesWhy it fails alone
Change-request rateReviewer engaging with contentInflated by nit-picking.
Comment density vs change sizeDepth of engagementRewards volume — one load-bearing comment beats twenty trivial ones.
Review-to-merge intervalWhether reviews are considered rather than reflexiveLong intervals may mean neglect, not care. Never target this — you'll reward slowness.
Rubber-stamp rateClearest single signal — approvals with no comment on non-trivial changeSome changes genuinely need no comment.
Post-merge defect correlationWhether review actually catches thingsLagging, and needs escaped-defect attribution to work at all.
Only one of the five measures effectiveness

Post-merge defect correlation is the only component that measures whether review worked rather than how much effort went in — and it's the hardest to instrument. The first four are proxies for attention. Until that data exists, use the manual audit below; it answers the same question in half a day.

Checking it stayed real — half a day, twice a year

The standard decays silently. Nobody announces that reviews have become approvals.

  1. Sample twenty merged PRs from the last sixty days, weighted toward AI-assisted and non-trivial. Exclude anything under about ten changed lines.
  2. Calibrate first — score five together, aloud, to agree what the criteria mean in this codebase.
  3. Judge blind. Two engineers score each PR 0–2 against the four criteria. No discussion; nobody scores their own work.
  4. Report the spread, not just the mean. The gap between judges is itself a finding.
Mean of eight
ScoreVerdictWhat to do
5.5 – 8.0SubstantiveThe control is real. Don't add more review process — spend the effort on feedback speed instead, and say publicly that you checked.
3.5 – 5.4MixedReal on some paths. Find the split — usually whether the reviewer knows the code. Risk-weight depth rather than tightening uniformly.
0 – 3.4Approval-shapedThe control exists on paper only, and the 1.7× issue rate is landing unmitigated. Fix this before adding anything else.
The calibration check nobody runs

If the two judges differ by more than two points on over a quarter of the sample, stop — the rubric isn't being applied consistently and the mean means nothing. Disagreement is a finding, not a nuisance: it usually means there is no shared idea of what a good review is, which is more actionable than any score. Report it as a sample and never as a target — twenty PRs is enough to decide something and nowhere near enough to rank people.

Fluency

The shared library, and why it isn't a training course

The component most likely to be skipped — it has no control to point at and nothing to fail a build on. It also decides which way AI amplifies.

Two engineers with identical tools produce work of very different quality, and the difference is mostly not talent. But an engineer who privately worked out how to get good results has raised their own output and the organisation gained nothing durable — it leaves when they do. The unit of fluency is the shared asset, not the skilled person. That is why this is a stage-one decision rather than a training plan.

Five artifacts, all in version control, all reviewed like code
ArtifactWhat it isWhy it must be versioned
Task promptsThe repeatable ones worth getting right — generate tests for this module, review this diff for concurrency, draft the risk assessment.Their behaviour changes when the model changes. An unversioned prompt silently degrades and nobody can say when or why.
Context packsArchitecture summaries, domain glossaries, data models, test strategy, service ownership — assembled and supplied as a unit rather than re-explained every time.Context drifts from reality faster than code does, and stale context is worse than none: it produces confident, wrong output.
Agent and tool definitionsSystem prompts, tool access, server configuration — the wiring that gives an agent reach into our systems.Production configuration with access to real systems. Treat it as scratch work and it will behave like scratch work.
Worked examplesReal before-and-after pairs from our own codebase: the mediocre first output, the prompt that fixed it, the accepted result.Examples are how a standard becomes legible. Imported ones don't transfer — the value is that it is recognisably our code.
The known-bad listWhat AI is reliably poor at in this codebase. The unusual patterns, the module whose conventions it always violates, the framework version it consistently gets wrong.The fastest-moving of the five and the most expensive to rediscover individually.
If you do one thing on this page

Write the known-bad list. Every engineer builds a private version within weeks; none of them share it. Writing it down turns learning that everyone is re-deriving in parallel into something a new joiner reads on day one — and it is the artifact almost nobody writes.

It also has a direct mechanical home here: it becomes a skill the agent loads, so the known-bad list stops being documentation and starts being something that changes what gets generated.

How fluency actually gets built

  • Write it as you learn it. Nobody here has worked out yet what good looks like in this codebase, so capture it as it emerges rather than importing it. The first month of real use is the most valuable and the most easily lost.
  • Pair, don't train. It transfers by demonstration. Watching someone competent work a real task surfaces the tacit moves they'd never think to write down.
  • Put the good prompt in the path. Wire the context pack into the repository, the review prompt into the PR template, the agent config into the dev environment — so the good version is already there and nobody has to go looking. A library nobody opens changes nothing.
  • Review prompts like code. Same repository, same pull request process, named owners. That is the difference between an asset that compounds and a wiki page that rots.

Signals worth watching, as diagnostics and never as targets: distinct contributors to the shared library — one author is a hobby, a third of the team is a capability · share of AI work drawing on shared context rather than ad-hoc chat · time for a new joiner to produce reviewable AI-assisted work · and the best qualitative test available, which takes one question in a retro: can the team name what AI is bad at here? A team that can't is at square one regardless of how much tooling it holds.

Ownership

Who holds what, and what nobody ever picks up

An established organisation has to work out what its quality function should stop doing. We get the far easier version: never start.

Four capabilities, not a team — one person holds all four part-time for a long time
CapabilityWhat it isScales with
Quality engineering
the technical discipline		Building the platforms quality relies on — pipelines, test frameworks, test data services, instrumentation, the paved road.Estate size and number of teams consuming it
EnablementCoaching, pairing, running risk analysis in refinement, building capability inside teams so they don't need you next time.Number of teams, and how far each is from the standard
Standards and governanceThe practice standard, risk tiering, provenance, what controls exist and where they live.Barely — largely fixed cost regardless of size
MeasurementThe metrics layer, the telemetry contract, visibility, release confidence. The capability that makes the other three assessable.Data complexity, not headcount

Three of the four are not test-related at all. Using "quality engineering" loosely for the whole group is how a quality function ends up sounding like QA with a newer name. When stretched, protect standards and measurement — near-fixed cost, and everything else depends on them.

What the function never starts doing
  • Writing the functional tests. Developers write them, with AI assistance at authorship. The moment someone else does, it is their job forever.
  • Executing regression manually. Risk-based selection and automation instead. Manual execution is a role that grows to fill the available headcount.
  • Acting as the release gate. Evidence and confidence scoring replace sign-off.
  • Sole ownership of the test framework. Shared platform, contributed to by everyone who uses it.
  • Bug triage as a QA activity. Team-owned, with AI-assisted clustering.

The first-hire test — the most consequential decision here. Hire a coach, or don't hire. The first quality job description sets the precedent permanently: write it as capability-building, or you have manufactured a gatekeeper on day one.

The rule that holds at every size

Central standards and central platform, yes. Central execution, never. The moment a central team runs tests on behalf of product teams, ownership evaporates and the bottleneck has been built deliberately.

Start the guild immediately — a recurring forum with real substance: shared patterns, open incident reviews, the known-bad list. It costs nothing, works at any size, and is where culture actually propagates.

Starting position

Where a new build actually starts

Not at zero on everything — that's the retrofit's starting position, not ours. A deliberate build starts unevenly, and knowing which is which prevents wasted effort.

Honest self-assessment before anything is built
CapabilityRealistic startWhy
Provenance and trustAheadLabelling and mandatory review are day-one settings. The capability where a new build is furthest ahead, and it costs almost nothing to claim.
Quality and test ownershipAheadThere is no separate function to own it, so developers own testing by construction. Protect this — it is the capability most easily lost by one hiring decision.
Development approachNaturally goodSmall batches are natural at small scale. They stop being natural around the point a second team appears.
Technical debtClean slateNo backlog exists yet. A bug-ceiling policy is trivial to adopt now and painful to introduce later.
AI fluencyGenuinely zeroThere is no shared context because there is no shared history. The only capability here that cannot be shortcut by a decision — it has to be accumulated.
Customer data analysisGenuinely zeroNo customers, no data. Unavoidable — and the telemetry contract is what makes it cheap to fix later rather than expensive.

The two that cap everything else

If quality and test ownership ever slips — one function owning quality rather than every engineer — it caps every other capability here, and no amount of tooling compensates. And provenance at zero while AI use is widespread is a live risk, not a maturity gap.

Everything else can be improved gradually. These two are load-bearing, and both are protected by decisions rather than effort.

How to use this later — a conversation, not an audit

Once there are teams, they self-assess and you facilitate. You don't grade. The value is in discussing why a team sits where it does and which single capability they move next.

Report team-level scores upward and the instrument dies within one cycle, because people assess to the score they are expected to have.

Decay

How the evidence layer rots

Specific to this half of the system. The delivery failure modes are on the Build plan tab.

Signal → what it means → counter
FailureSignalDesigned-in counter
Instrumenting nothingShipping fast, telemetry deferred as premature. Six months in, no baseline exists.The telemetry contract is stage one, not stage three. Emitting is nearly free; excavating is the most expensive phase other companies have.
Telemetry emitted, nobody looksDashboards exist; no decision has changed because of one.Instrumentation without a decision attached is cost without return. Cut the metric set to the ones that changed a decision last quarter.
Review becomes approvalChange volume rises with AI; review depth doesn't scale with it.Four criteria in the PR template, risk-weighted depth, and the blind audit above.
Trust collapseFlaky suite; people re-run until green.Treat flakiness as a severity-two class problem from the first flake. Above 2%, stop feature work.
Volume theatreThousands of generated tests, no change in escaped defects.Mutation score and escaped defects, never test count.
The gate rebuilt in AI clothingThe confidence score becomes an approval nobody can override.Scores inform; humans decide. Always allow a documented override, and record who used it.
Quality concentrates on one personThe model says shared ownership; the practice says otherwise.Ownership never actually transferred — it became a role by accident, which is the exact failure this design exists to prevent.
The standard becomes fictionNobody has changed the one-page standard in a year, and nobody reads it.Version control, named owner, re-read against actual practice at stage four. Whichever is wrong, change it.
The signal that matters more than the others

Escaped defects climb as headcount grows, while every control reports green. The controls are measuring the wrong thing. Say so publicly rather than quietly adding another one — and note this is precisely the case the telemetry contract exists to make visible early rather than late.

Cost, context and throughput

Making it cheap, fast and right first time

Founder-funded means the constraint is stewardship, not survival — spend that's defensible per unit of output, with nothing obviously wasteful. This section covers what actually moves the number, how to stay context-efficient without weakening standards, and which decisions genuinely have to be right first time.

It also prices the whole toolchain, not just the AI — every product named anywhere in this document, with its licence, and the five places a paid option competes with a free one. Skip to the full bill →

The short versionFull tab: ~19 min · this box: 1 min

  • About $400–500 per engineer per month for everything — AI, cloud, tooling, the lot. Against a loaded engineer at £6,000–8,000 a month, that's under 6%. It scales almost perfectly linearly.
  • Most of the stack is free. Nearly every tool named in this document is open source. The bill is Claude usage and cloud hosting; everything else is rounding.
  • Roughly $30–45 of AI per feature, with a $150 cap enforced at the run level rather than per stage. Cheap models do the mechanical work, expensive ones do the thinking, and nothing is allowed to run unbounded.
  • The failure to avoid isn't overspending — it's being unable to explain the spend. Microsoft cancelled Claude for ~5,000 engineers despite their own study measuring a 24% gain, because the bill arrived against a budget set before anyone knew the number.
  • So one named person owns the total, reviewed monthly. Cost per merged feature is the metric that keeps it defensible, because it's attributable and it trends.
Getting the framing right

The money is there, but the allocation isn't yours and you don't know its size. That's a different problem from a shoestring, and it points in a specific direction: optimise for defensible cost per outcome, not for minimum spend.

The failure mode to avoid isn't overspending — it's being unable to explain the spend. Microsoft cancelled Claude Code for ~5,000 engineers despite having the budget and their own study measuring a 24% throughput gain, because they piloted on flat-rate seats and couldn't defend $500–2,000 per engineer per month when the real number arrived all at once. They had the money. They lost the argument.

The mistake in the other direction

Don't under-invest to look frugal. A fully-loaded engineer costs roughly £300–400 a day. A model choice that saves $50 a month but costs someone an hour a week is a bad trade by a factor of ten, and it's the kind of false economy a founder who has run a company will spot immediately.

Everything below targets waste — uncached prefixes, runaway loops, the wrong model for a mechanical task — not usage. Cutting waste is always right. Cutting usage to hit a number is usually wrong.

Lever 1

Model tiering — route by task, not by habit

The single biggest structural saving. Most teams run everything on their best model because it's one config line.

API pricing, per million tokens — verified, and dated
ModelInputOutputRelative cost
Claude Opus 5$5.00$25.00Baseline
Claude Sonnet 5$3.00
$2.00 intro, ends 2026-08-31		$15.00
$10.00 intro, ends 2026-08-31		~40% cheaper
Claude Haiku 4.5$1.00$5.005× cheaper
Two things to know before any of these numbers go in a budget

The Sonnet introductory rate ends 31 August 2026. Output goes $10 → $15, a 50% rise on the half of the bill that dominates agentic spend. Every figure on this tab is quoted at list price for exactly that reason — if you have been forecasting against the intro rate, re-run it. Any pricing table in a document like this needs a date stamp and a re-check before it is quoted at anyone, and this one is current as of publication rather than permanently true.

Why Claude Fable 5 does not appear. It is Anthropic's most capable model and it is deliberately excluded. Two reasons, and either alone would settle it. It is priced above the Opus tier, and the marginal quality on well-specified implementation tasks does not repay that on this workload — the spec chain is doing the work a bigger model would otherwise be paid to guess at. And it requires 30-day data retention, so it cannot run under Zero Data Retention — which this plan requires for BAA coverage. That makes it not merely expensive but unavailable on our configuration, which is worth writing down so nobody re-proposes it in six months.

Routing table — which model for which job
TaskModelWhy
Triage, labelling, classificationHaiku 4.5Bounded, structured, low-judgment. 5× cheaper for identical output.
Codemods, mechanical campaignsHaiku / Sonnet 5Repetitive and independently verifiable — the tests are the safety net
Spec drafting, planningOpus 5Specification errors are 41.8% of failures; this is the wrong place to economise
Task decompositionSonnet 5Structured transformation of a plan that's already been thought through
ImplementationSonnet 5, escalating to Opus 5Sonnet handles most well-specified tasks. Escalate on the second failed iteration rather than starting expensive — the failure itself is the signal that the task is harder than it looked.
Verification and reviewOpus 5 — never cheaperA reviewer weaker than the writer manufactures false confidence. Worse than no reviewer. Note that when implementation has escalated to Opus 5, the reviewer is equal rather than stronger — accept that, and let the second reviewer lens and the human gate carry the difference.
Effort, per stageSet it explicitlylow for triage and classification, medium for decomposition and mechanical work, high for spec, design and review. It is a required input on every stage, not a default to inherit — an unset effort is the largest untuned lever in the pipeline. Sweep it on the golden set rather than guessing.
Why implementation escalates rather than starting on Opus

Because by the time an agent reaches implementation, the hard thinking is already done and written down — the spec is approved, the plan names the files, the tasks are sized. Most of what's left is well-specified execution, which Sonnet does capably at roughly 40% less.

The escalation trigger matters more than the starting model. A task that fails twice is telling you it's harder than the decomposition believed, and that's precisely when the stronger model earns its price. Starting everything on Opus pays the premium on the 80% of tasks that never needed it.

Note this is the one place the pipeline spends more to be safe: verification never routes down, whatever wrote the code.

Pipeline: the model chosen for every stage →
The trap inside model tiering

Prompt-cache minimums are not monotonic across models, and routing a task to a cheaper model can silently cost you more. The minimum cacheable prefix is 512 tokens on Opus 5, 1,024 on Sonnet 5, and 4,096 on Haiku 4.5.

So a 3,000-token prompt caches on Opus 5 at ~0.1× input price on every repeat, and silently doesn't cache at all on Haiku — no error, just cache_creation_input_tokens: 0. A high-frequency task moved to Haiku "to save money" can end up more expensive. Check the cache-read figure after any routing change.

Lever 2

Prompt caching — the structural one

Largest single lever on spend, and it's architecture rather than discipline. Get the layout right once and it works forever.

Cache economics
OperationPrice vs. base inputBreak-even
Cache read~0.1×—
Cache write, 5-min TTL1.25×2 requests (1.25 + 0.1 vs 2.0)
Cache write, 1-hour TTL2.0×3 requests (2.0 + 0.2 vs 3.0)

The governing rule: caching is a prefix match — any byte change anywhere in the prefix invalidates everything after it. Render order is tools → system → messages, so anything volatile near the front destroys the cache for everything behind it.

Silent invalidators to grep for

These produce zero cache hits with no error
PatternEffect
Date.now() / timestamp in system promptPrefix differs every request — nothing ever caches
UUID or request ID early in contentSame
JSON.stringify without sorted keysNon-deterministic bytes
User or session ID in the system promptPer-user prefix; no sharing across users
Tool set varying per requestTools render at position 0 — invalidates everything
Switching model mid-sessionCaches are model-scoped

Three non-obvious caching rules worth knowing before you build

  • Parallel fan-out defeats caching. N concurrent requests with identical prefixes all pay full price — none can read what the others are still writing. Send one, wait for the first streamed token, then fire the rest.
  • Agentic loops blow past the lookback window. Each breakpoint searches back at most 20 content blocks. A turn with many tool-call pairs exceeds that, and the next request silently misses. Place an intermediate breakpoint every ~15 blocks in long turns.
  • Verify, don't assume. cache_read_input_tokens is the only proof. If it's zero across repeated identical-prefix requests, something is invalidating and you're paying full price without knowing.
Lever 3

Effort, batch, and bounded loops

Effort is the per-request cost dial

output_config.effort takes low / medium / high / xhigh / max and controls thinking depth and overall token spend. On Opus 5, low and medium are unusually strong — often matching what previous models produced at their ceiling. Start at high, sweep downward on your own evals, and only stay high where quality measurably holds.

One counterintuitive limit

Effort does not reliably shorten visible output. Lowering it may reduce thinking without reducing the response text. If verbose responses are the cost problem, that's a prompting fix, not an effort one — an explicit conciseness instruction cut user-facing length by ~20% in testing.

Batch API — 50% off everything not latency-sensitive

Up to 100,000 requests per batch, most complete within an hour, maximum 24. Half price on all token usage, and fully compatible with prompt caching. For a cost-constrained team this is close to free money on:

  • Nightly test backfill across a legacy codebase
  • Documentation generation
  • Eval suite runs against the golden set
  • Codemod planning and campaign dry-runs
  • Bulk classification or extraction jobs

Task budgets stop runaway loops

A task budget gives the agent a token ceiling it can see for a whole agentic loop, so it paces itself and finishes gracefully rather than being cut off. Minimum 20,000 tokens. Distinct from max_tokens, which is an enforced per-response cap the model isn't aware of.

Use all three: task_budget so the agent self-moderates, max_tokens as the hard per-response ceiling, and a per-run dollar cap in the pipeline above both. They fail in different directions, which is why one is not enough — a budget the agent can see prevents waste, a ceiling it cannot see prevents runaway, and a pipeline cap catches the case where the stage itself is misconfigured.

The measured payoff: formal resource bounds on agent loops cut token consumption by 90% and reduced run-to-run variance by 525×. Variance is the number that matters — it's the difference between a cost you can forecast and one you can only discover.

Lever 4

Context economy — the part that isn't a trade-off

You asked for cost and context aware. They're mostly the same problem, and the intuition about the trade-off runs backwards.

Two facts to hold together. Token usage alone explains about 80% of performance variance, so starving the agent degrades output. But the real cost drivers are bloated retrieval, unmanaged message history, and unbounded refinement loops — not the length of your instructions. You cut waste, not context.

Practices by payoff
PracticeEffectEffort
Cache-stable prefix orderingUp to 90% off input tokensStructural
Rules moved from prose into hooksPermanently free and bindingLow
Skills instead of instruction-file bulkZero cost when unusedLow
/clear between unrelated tasksKills accumulated irrelevanceFree
Subagents for wide readsExploration happens in a disposable contextLow
Small batches / PR size capsBounded diff means bounded contextCultural

Why a shorter instruction file raises standards

A bloated AGENTS.md doesn't only cost tokens — it causes the agent to ignore rules that are in it, because the important ones get lost. Adherence falls as length rises.

So the diagnostic is: if the agent keeps breaking a written rule, never add emphasis. The file is too long. Delete three other things — or better, convert the rule to a hook, where it costs nothing and cannot be ignored.

Where multi-agent stops being cheap

Multi-agent architectures consume roughly 15× the tokens of a single agent, and Anthropic's own research finds them less effective for tightly interdependent tasks — naming coding specifically. Use subagents for context isolation and independent review, which is cheap and effective. Do not use them as a fan-out strategy for coding work; that's where the 15× lands with no return.

Throughput

Extremely high output, ticket to production

You asked for maximum throughput end to end. The uncomfortable part: the fastest way to raise it is usually not to generate more.

The measurement that reframes this

Feature-branch throughput rose 59% year over year while throughput on main actually fell. Agentic PRs wait 5.3× longer to be picked up. More generation into an unchanged review process produces a longer queue, not more shipped software.

What actually shortens ticket-to-production

Ranked by effect on cycle time
InterventionWhy it works
Automate the entire baseline reviewFormatting, security patterns, coverage, duplication, dependency policy — all machine-checkable. Humans review intent and architectural fit only.
Cap PR size in CIAgents happily produce 2,000-line diffs nobody can meaningfully review. Small batches is also one of DORA's seven amplifiers.
Reviewer subagent before any humanThe human never sees an un-reviewed diff; findings are already fixed
Risk lanesLane 1 skips human review entirely. Most changes aren't sensitive.
Zero handoffs between stagesFile-based contracts mean no waiting for a person to pass something on
Make deploy reversibleCanary plus auto-rollback removes the release-approval queue entirely

The counterintuitive one

Do not add worktree parallelism to go faster. Running 4–8 agent sessions per developer multiplies input to the bottleneck you already have. Teams report review — not the agent — as the limiting factor at that scale. Fix review throughput first, then scale parallelism into the headroom you created.

The number

What the AI actually costs

Token spend only. Everything else in the stack is priced in the section after this one.

Read that cap correctly — it is not the sum of the rows above

Implementation is capped per task, and a Lane 2 feature is normally three or more tasks because of the diff cap. So the stage caps summed give a ceiling near $180, not $150. The $150 is a separate, run-level ceiling the harness enforces by accumulating spend across stages — it stops the run and escalates rather than letting the stage caps add up to whatever they add up to. Why the budget bound is two numbers →

Monthly, small self-funded team
ScenarioVolumeMonthly
Early build, 3 engineers~20 features + exploration~$900–1,400
Steady state, 6 engineers~50 features~$2,200–3,500
With caching and tiering tunedSame volume40–60% lower
The comparison that makes the case

Fully tuned, the entire engineering AI spend for a small team lands at roughly two to five contractor days a month — around $900–2,100 against a contractor day of £300–400. That is the honest framing and it is still a very easy argument to win; an earlier draft of this document said "one contractor day", which was wrong by a factor of two to five and is exactly the kind of understatement that loses credibility the first time someone divides it themselves.

Cost per merged feature is the metric that keeps it defensible, because it is attributable and it trends. Define it before it appears on a dashboard: model spend tagged to a pipeline run, divided by pull requests merged from that run's branch, per calendar month. "Feature" and "PR" are used interchangeably elsewhere in this document and they should not be — pick the PR denominator, because it is the one you can compute without arguing.

The rest of the bill

What every tool on this page costs

Every product named anywhere in this document, with its licence and price. Anything that isn't free, or isn't already inside GitHub, is a decision — so it's listed as one rather than assumed.

Treat these as directional, not as quotes

Vendor pricing moves, tiers get renamed, and healthcare terms are frequently negotiated rather than list-price. Every figure below needs confirming in writing before it goes in a budget. They're accurate enough to decide shape — free versus paid, tens versus thousands — which is what they're for.

Free, and staying free

The great majority of the stack costs nothing, and that's deliberate rather than lucky — a self-funded team should not be paying for anything whose open-source equivalent is the industry default.

Design system (external firm)One-off project costStage 1 briefNot a subscription and not a monthly line, but the largest single discretionary spend here if taken. Everything it produces — tokens, icons, guidelines, components — is free software we then own and maintain. The cost is the brand work, not the technology.
CI minutes are the line item that gets missed

Playwright suites and agent stages both run on GitHub-hosted runners, and both are minute-hungry. A rough model: 40 PRs a week × ~12 minutes of CI × 4.3 weeks ≈ 2,100 minutes, plus agent stages and nightly golden-set runs. That lands close to the 3,000 included on Team and goes over it as soon as the E2E suite grows.

Overage is ~$0.008/minute, so 5,000 minutes over is about $40 — irritating rather than dangerous. It's worth knowing because the instinct on seeing the bill is to cut test coverage, which is exactly the wrong response. Fix it with path filtering, Nx affected-only runs on the TS side and solution-filtered dotnet test on the C# side, not by testing less.

Budget a little more here than a single-language repo would need. Two toolchains means two restore-and-build steps, and a NuGet restore plus an npm install on a cold runner is a few minutes before anything useful happens. Cache both aggressively — it is the single highest-return CI optimisation available and it is a few lines of workflow.

Five decisions where paid competes with free

These are the ones that need an actual answer rather than a default, because in each case a paid product does something the free option doesn't — and in four of the five, our answer is still the free one.

Paid-versus-free, decided
DecisionPaid optionFree optionOur answer
Infrastructure as codeTerraform (BUSL licence) + HCP TerraformOpenTofu — MPL-2.0, drop-in forkOpenTofu Terraform's licence changed to BUSL in 2023. It's still free for our use, but the licence can change again and OpenTofu is a genuine drop-in. Take the fork.
Feature flagsLaunchDarkly — roughly $10–12/seat/mo, scales with usageflagd — the OpenFeature reference implementationflagd Because we adopt the OpenFeature standard, swapping to LaunchDarkly later is a config change, not a migration. Start free, buy only if flag governance becomes a real job.

The one paid decision that's genuinely close

Azure Health Data Services, the FHIR service. It is a managed Azure service with a real per-hour and per-transaction cost, and it needs pricing against expected volume rather than assumed. The alternative is running a FHIR server ourselves — Medplum or the open-source Microsoft FHIR server — which is free in licence and not free in attention.

Buy it. FHIR is large, easy to implement subtly wrong, and the compliance surface around it is precisely what you do not want a small team maintaining. An engineer costs £300–400 a day, so a managed tier saving one day a month has paid for itself at £4,000 a year — and a HIPAA and HITRUST-covered managed FHIR store is worth considerably more than that when the first customer security review arrives.

The date that matters: the older Azure API for FHIR retires 30 September 2026. Build on Health Data Services from the first line. Any tutorial, template or Stack Overflow answer referencing the old service points at a dead end, and that is a very easy mistake for an agent to make from training data — worth a line in the instruction file.

Get a quote before assuming self-hosting is cheaper. An engineer costs £300–400 a day, so a hosted tier that saves one day a month has already paid for itself at £4,000/year — and hosted FHIR infrastructure with a BAA is worth considerably more than that when the first customer security review arrives.

The whole bill, monthly

Indicative run-rate, everything included
