The number to say out loud

Roughly $410–545 per engineer per month for the entire toolchain, of which more than half is Claude and Azure. The GitHub line is fixed by the Enterprise Managed Users requirement rather than chosen, so it isn't a lever.

Against a loaded engineer, that is 4–7% — and quote the range, not a single figure. A loaded engineer at £6,000–8,000 a month is roughly $7,500–10,000, so the ratio runs from about 4.1% at the top of the salary band to about 7.3% at the bottom. An earlier draft said "under 6%", which divided dollars by pounds and happened to land inside the range only at the favourable end. The mistake is worth naming because this is the number most likely to be checked in the room, and being caught mixing currencies costs more than the extra percentage point ever would.

It also scales almost perfectly linearly, which is the useful property when someone asks what happens at thirty people. Nothing here has a cliff except Claude seats — and that cliff is a contract term, not a technical one.

Caveat on Azure — the least certain number on this page

The Azure line is a shape, not a quote. It is load-dependent, it is the only figure here that can move by a factor of two in either direction, and nobody has modelled the actual workload yet. Two non-production environments, a managed Postgres, a managed FHIR service and somewhere to run containers is the shape; the FHIR service in particular is priced on transactions and could be materially more or less than assumed.

Three things to do before this number goes anywhere near a board pack. Price the FHIR service against an estimated message volume rather than a guess. Confirm whether any Azure commitment or enterprise agreement already covers part of it — inherited estates usually carry discounts nobody remembers. And put a budget alert on the subscription from day one, because the failure mode here is drift rather than a spike.

Roughly two-thirds of the per-engineer figure is indifferent to Azure entirely — model usage, source control, error tracking above the free tier. That is the part you can quote with confidence.

So quote it in two parts — "about $150–250 per engineer for tooling and AI, plus Azure, which is load-dependent and the least certain line here". The single blended number invites a challenge on the part you can least defend, and losing that exchange costs more than the precision buys.

Three gaps this pricing exercise exposed

Error tracking was never specified. The document named a metrics backend for SLOs — which is what the canary queries — but nothing that captures an individual exception with its stack trace. Those are different jobs, and stage 11's auto-rollback only tells you that something broke. Application Insights or Sentry, in stage 3.

Nothing owns the bill. Cost caps are set per-user and per-stage, but no step made one named person responsible for reviewing total spend monthly. The Microsoft failure wasn't overspending — it was nobody watching until the number was indefensible. It's now step 44, and the total it covers is Azure and CI minutes as well as Claude.

Nobody was watching for reversals of a saving. The Sonnet introductory rate ends 31 August 2026 and takes roughly a third off the effective discount on the busiest model in the pipeline. Nothing in the plan noticed that a vendor price change is as much a budget event as our own usage change. The monthly spend review now includes one line: has any rate we depend on changed? It takes ten seconds and it is the difference between explaining a rise and being surprised by one.

Watch this

The government-backing ambition has a compliance floor

Worth resolving early, because getting it wrong is expensive in both directions.

You mentioned government backing following because the product saves Medicare money. That could mean two very different things, and the compliance consequences are not comparable:

Selling to MA plans (likely)
  • Your customer is a private health plan that CMS pays
  • You're a Business Associate — HIPAA flows down contractually
  • No FedRAMP
  • Bar is enterprise security review: SOC 2, maybe HITRUST
  • Achievable self-funded
Selling to CMS directly
  • Your customer is a federal agency
  • FedRAMP High is typically required for SaaS handling CMS Medicare/Medicaid data
  • Non-authorised SaaS goes through CMS's Rapid Cloud Review
  • Multi-year programme, six-to-seven-figure cost
  • Fundable here, but a deliberate strategic commitment — not something you drift into

What to do about it now

Find out which one, before you architect. Most post-discharge and supplemental-benefit companies sell to MA plans, not to CMS — so the left column is the likely answer. But if the business thesis genuinely depends on a federal contract, that's a funding conversation, not an engineering one.

Cheap now, expensive later — do these regardless: US-only data residency, audit logging from day one, infrastructure as code, SSO and MFA, encryption in transit and at rest, and no PHI in development. All are good practice anyway and all are painful to retrofit.

Do not pre-build FedRAMP controls speculatively. Months spent on compliance for a customer you may never sell to is the most expensive kind of waste — it consumes the scarcest resource (early engineering time) on an outcome nobody has committed to. Build the hedges, skip the programme until someone signs.

Right first time

What genuinely has to be right, and what doesn't

You said there's no room to get the setup wrong first time. That's true of a small number of things and false of most — and knowing the difference is what stops you over-engineering a runway you can't afford to burn.

Decisions by reversibility
DecisionReversibilityGet it right because…
Repository topologyVery hardExtracting a module later is bounded work; merging 15 repos back is not
One owning team per repoVery hardFree on day one, near-impossible to retrofit socially
Contracts repo + generated clientsHardRetrofitting means changing both sides of every integration you already have
Call-don't-copy CIHardAfter the first copy-paste it's archaeology across N repos
Cost controls before first seatHardHabits form in the first month; retrofitting cost discipline is the documented failure
Primary language and platformSettledC# on Azure, React on the front. Decided outside this document. What is not settled is the three mitigations it requires — the two-language catalogue, the config parity check and the generated client. Those are hard to retrofit and easy to skip, so treat them as if they were the decision.
Shared config packageHardEvery standard is expressed here. Once apps grow their own configs, adding a rule means N pull requests and an argument.
Component library exists before feature oneHardRetrofitting a design system onto fifteen bespoke buttons is a project, not a task
Buying Claude through AzureTrivialConfig, but get it right anyway — Foundry silently costs you Batch API and task budgets
Model routing / effort levelsTrivialConfig change. Sweep and tune continuously.
Which subagents existTrivialMarkdown files. Start with two.
Hooks and lane definitionsEasyAdd with evidence, remove when wrong
Spec framework choiceEasyConventions, not infrastructure

The low-regret core

Five things are right regardless of how anything else lands, and they cost almost nothing:

  • One owning team per repo or module.
  • Contracts as generated, typed clients from the first service boundary.
  • A shared CI workflow that's called, never copied.
  • Per-user cost caps and usage-based billing before a single seat is issued.
  • Telemetry on from day one, even pointed at a free tier — history can't be generated retroactively.

Everything else in this document can be wrong initially and corrected cheaply. Spend the "must be right" budget on these five and the topology decision, then move. Six weeks of deliberation costs more than a reversible wrong turn — not in money, but in the thing you can't buy back: being six weeks later to a working product.

Evidence

What the research actually shows

The studies behind every recommendation, including the ones that contradict each other and the ones that contradict the vendor narrative. If a claim gets challenged, the citation is here.

The short versionFull tab: ~18 min · this box: 1 min

  • The evidence genuinely conflicts, and that's the finding. A controlled trial measured experienced developers 19% slower with AI while they believed they were 20% faster. A large field study measured 24% more merged work. Both are sound; they measured different things.
  • Speed goes up, durability goes down. Across 623 million code changes: duplication +81%, copy-paste +41%, reuse across files −35%, refactoring −70%.
  • Security is a real cost, not a talking point. Around a quarter of AI-generated samples carry a vulnerability, and 56% of organisations report an incident partly attributable to AI-written code.
  • Agent "teams" with job titles don't work for coding. The failure they fix accounts for 0.5% of observed failures and costs about 15× the tokens. Verification failures are 21.3%; specification and design, 41.8%.
  • The consensus: AI amplifies whatever process it lands in. Good process gets faster; bad process gets faster at producing problems. That is the entire reason this document exists.
The gap

Adoption is universal. Scaled value is rare.

Two things are simultaneously true, and the distance between them is the opportunity.

Where the industry actually is
MeasureFigureSource
Software professionals using AI at work90% (+14pts YoY)DORA 2025
Organisations pursuing GenAI in quality engineering~90%Capgemini World Quality Report 2025–26
GenAI capabilities actually running in production~37%Capgemini WQR 2025–26
Achieved enterprise-scale deployment15%Capgemini WQR 2025–26
Adopted AI tools faster than they built policy80%CTO survey 2026

Nine in ten are trying. Fewer than one in six have scaled it.

That gap is not a capability problem — the models are the same ones everybody has. It is a process and evidence problem, which is the thesis of this entire document and the reason the investment goes into the pipeline rather than the tooling.

The 80% figure is the sharpest of the five: four in five organisations adopted the tools before they wrote the policy. We are in the rare position of being able to do it the other way round, and that advantage expires roughly eighteen months from the first commit.

DORA's seven capabilities — five are ordinary hygiene

DORA identified seven capabilities that determine whether AI accelerates outcomes or accelerates chaos:

  • A clear AI stance — what AI may be used for, what review it needs, who is accountable
  • Healthy data ecosystems
  • AI-accessible internal data — the quality knowledge layer
  • Strong version control
  • Working in small batches
  • User-centric focus
  • Quality internal platforms — the paved road
The observation worth repeating in the room

Five of the seven are classic engineering hygiene. Nothing about model choice, prompt technique or tool selection appears on the list. That is what "AI is an amplifier, not a lever" means in practice — and it is why the quality strategy and the AI strategy are the same strategy.

One figure to handle carefully

A widely-cited secondary analysis puts unreviewed AI code at roughly +22% above human defect rates and reviewed AI code at about −8% below it. It cites a 200-organisation study but names no primary source — treat the exact numbers as indicative and don't quote them alone.

The direction — that review discipline is what separates AI code being better or worse than human-written code — is independently corroborated by the CodeRabbit research, and that direction is what the recommendation actually rests on.

Summary

Four findings that shape everything

The load-bearing evidence
FindingStrengthWhat follows
Productivity gains are real but unevenly distributed, and self-reports are worthlessContestedNever justify the programme on developer sentiment — it was measured as inverted
Cost, not capability, kills these programmesStrongUnit-cost governance is the first thing built, not the last
Throughput rises, stability falls, unless something holds the lineStrongAI is an amplifier — greenfield is when you set which direction
The bottleneck moves to verification immediatelyModerateEvery design choice judged on whether it makes review cheaper
Primary studies

The five that matter

Method, headline, and honest limitations
StudyMethodHeadlineWhat it can't tell you
METR RCT
Jul 2025		 Randomised controlled trial. 16 experienced open-source developers, 246 real tasks, their own repositories. Tools available Feb–Jun 2025. −19% speed
+20% perceived		 Expert developers on mature code they knew intimately. METR itself now labels the result historical.
Microsoft field study
Jul 2026		 Direct telemetry, not surveys. Tens of thousands of engineers, four-month window, synthetic control with placebo testing. ~24% merged PRs
sustained over 4 months		 Counts merged PRs, not what's inside them. Azure DevOps only. Self-selection only partly addressed.
DORA 2025 5,000+ respondents, 100 hours of interviews. Throughput up
instability up too		 Self-reported and correlational, not causal. Note the reversal: DORA's 2024 report associated AI adoption with reduced throughput. 2025 flipped it. The instability finding held both years.
GitClear 2026 623 million code changes analysed, 2023–2026, across Cursor, Copilot and Claude Code cohorts. Duplication +81%
Refactoring −70%		 Proxy metrics for maintainability. Vendor-published.
AppSec Santa 2026 534 generated samples across 6 frontier models, tested against OWASP Top 10. ~25% contained a confirmed vulnerability Synthetic prompts without project guardrails — a floor, not a prediction of your rate.

Reconciling METR and Microsoft

These are the two most rigorous studies available and they point in opposite directions. The reconciliation matters because it tells you where your gains will actually come from.

METR studied experts working on code they already knew intimately. In that setting the developer's mental model is already better than anything an agent can reconstruct, so prompting and reviewing is pure overhead. Microsoft studied a broad population across unfamiliar surfaces, with a year-newer generation of tooling, measuring merged PR throughput.

The Microsoft data supports that reading from inside: the effect was dose-responsive, from +15% at three days a week to +50.1% at five or more. Junior engineers and long-tenured staff saw the largest lifts. Senior individual contributors (IC5–IC6) adopted at higher rates but their retention was near zero — the population most like METR's subjects tried it and stopped.

A finding worth reporting honestly

In the same Microsoft study, Copilot CLI users showed roughly 2.2× the PR lift of Claude Code adopters (+24.9% vs +11.4%). Caveats are substantial — Microsoft owns Copilot, the Claude Code rollout there was newer and smaller, and selection effects are hard to rule out. But it's in the data and shouldn't be quietly dropped.

What follows for a greenfield codebase

With no legacy mental models to compete with, we sit firmly in the population where gains appear. Two caveats travel with that:

  • Our most senior people may genuinely be slower on the work they know best. Don't mandate uniform usage — it costs credibility with exactly the people whose buy-in matters.
  • Microsoft measured merged PRs; GitClear measures what's inside them. Optimising the first without watching the second is how you buy throughput with maintainability.

Error-masking constructs+47%Empty catches and swallowed errors — the "make it pass" instinct
Two-week code churn+15%
+39% in AI-heavy projects		The single best signal we have. Code merged then rewritten because it was never right
Cross-file function calls−35%Reuse declining
Refactoring line moves−70%Nobody is tidying up

Security

Two independent studies, two different numbers, and the gap between them is methodological rather than contradictory. Quote whichever matches the question being asked, and know why they differ.

  • AppSec Santa (2026) — ~1 in 4. 534 samples across six frontier models tested against the OWASP Top 10; roughly 25% contained a confirmed vulnerability. Best performer 19.1%, worst 29.2%. SSRF was the single most common class, because models routinely generate HTTP calls that accept a caller-supplied URL without validating it.
  • Veracode, via the Cloud Security Alliance — 45%. A larger share of samples failed a security test, with log injection (88% failure) and cross-site scripting (86%) worst. Java fared worst by language at 72%.
  • Why they differ: "confirmed vulnerability" is a higher bar than "failed a test". The honest reading is a floor of one in four and a plausible ceiling near one in two — either way, too high to leave to human review alone.
  • A 2025 Snyk survey found 56% of organisations had experienced a security incident at least partly attributable to AI-generated code.
  • Time to detect an AI-introduced vulnerability runs 42 days longer than for human-written code, because existing tooling wasn't designed for the patterns AI produces.
Why this shapes the security-reviewer agent

The prevalence data is why that agent is told to weight injection and SSRF specifically, and why it must demand a concrete exploitation path before recording a finding. Generic "this could be unsafe" output wastes fix-loop tokens without reducing risk.

If challenged on the number, lead with the lower one. A claim of 25% that survives scrutiny is worth more than a claim of 45% that invites an argument about methodology — and the conclusion is identical at either figure.

Cost

The programme-killer

The most instructive failure in the public record isn't a team that used agents badly. It's a team that used them well and lost the budget argument anyway.

Microsoft introduced Claude Code to engineers in December 2025. Adoption reached 84–95% of the cohort by April. Its own researchers measured a 24% increase in merged pull requests that did not decay over four months. In June 2026 it was cancelled for roughly 5,000 engineers in the Experiences & Devices division, who were moved to GitHub Copilot CLI.

The mechanism is easy to repeat. The pilot ran under flat seat licensing, making token consumption invisible during the months when usage habits were forming. When billing moved to consumption, the true figure — $500 to $2,000 per engineer per month — arrived all at once against a budget set when nobody knew what the number would be. Uber's CTO separately confirmed the company burned its full 2026 AI coding budget in four months.

Spend, for calibration
MeasureFigureNote
Typical per developer$150–250 / monthAcross observed enterprise deployments
Typical per active day~$13Under $30/day for 90% of users
Microsoft heavy usage$500–2,000 / monthThe figure that ended the programme
Prompt caching saving41–80%Measured on agentic workloads; up to 90% on input tokens

The four controls this implies

  • Usage-based billing from day one. Never pilot on flat rate and convert later — you want the cost signal present while habits form.
  • Per-user caps, not per-team. Team caps punish your highest-value users by averaging them with light ones.
  • Spend visible to the engineer generating it. The cheapest cost control is a developer who can see their own number.
  • Track cost per merged PR, not per seat. This is the question a CFO actually asks — and the one Microsoft could not answer in its own defence.
Bottleneck

Where the constraint actually sits

  • Agentic AI PRs have a pickup time 5.3× longer than unassisted PRs; AI-assisted PRs wait 2.47× longer (LinearB 2026 benchmarks).
  • Feature-branch throughput rose 59% year over year while throughput on main actually fell (CircleCI 2026 data). Main-branch success rates dropped to 70.8%.
  • Telemetry from 10,000+ developers: 98% more pull requests opened, 91% longer review times, and flat delivery metrics. Twice the work in, the same value out — the clearest single statement of where the constraint sits.
  • Output per engineer rose roughly 60% from 2025 to 2026, while a reviewer still handles the same number of PRs they always did.
  • Anthropic's own research describes a delegation gap: AI is used in ~60% of work but only 0–20% of tasks can be fully delegated. Verification is named as the new bottleneck.
The design consequence

Generation capacity is effectively free and about to be abundant. Review capacity is the real constraint. Risk lanes, reviewer subagents, PR size caps, automated baseline checks and bounded pipeline loops all exist for one reason — to make review cheaper. Anything that raises generation without raising review throughput makes the organisation slower, not faster.

Agent design

Why role-based agent teams underperform

The MAST failure taxonomy

Berkeley researchers analysed 1,600+ annotated execution traces across 7 popular multi-agent frameworks, producing 14 failure modes in three categories. Inter-annotator agreement κ = 0.88.

Failure modes by share of observed failures
ModeCategoryShare
Step repetitionSystem design15.7%
Reasoning–action mismatchInter-agent13.2%
Unaware of termination conditionsSystem design12.4%
Disobey task specificationSystem design11.0%
Incorrect verificationVerification9.1%
No or incomplete verificationVerification8.2%
Task derailmentInter-agent7.4%
Fail to ask for clarificationInter-agent6.8%
Premature terminationVerification6.2%
Conversation resetInter-agent2.2%
Ignored other agent's inputInter-agent1.9%
Disobey role specificationSystem design0.5%

The three category totals, which are the figures to quote: specification and system design 41.8%, inter-agent misalignment 36.9%, task verification 21.3%. Agents violating their assigned roles is the rarest mode in the whole taxonomy, at 0.5% — and that is the failure a role-based agent team is built to prevent.

The authors tested interventions: adding high-level task verification improved success by +15.6%; workflow and role adjustment gained +9.4%. They caution that neither resolves all modes.

The persona evidence

A systematic study across 4 model families, 162 roles spanning 6 relationship types and 8 expertise domains, and 2,410 factual questions found that adding personas to system prompts produced no improvement, and often a small negative effect, versus a control with no persona. A 2026 follow-up across 1,140 questions and 38 expert roles refined rather than overturned this: small aggregate differences, with role prompting systematically increasing expertise depth while reducing clarity.

Anthropic on when multi-agent works

  • Multi-agent architectures excel at breadth-first problems that split into independent parallel strands, and are less effective for tightly interdependent tasks — coding is named specifically.
  • Multi-agent systems consume roughly 15× the tokens of standard interactions.
  • Token usage alone explains ~80% of performance variance in their BrowseComp evaluation — more than prompt engineering or architecture.

Reading these together

Role-based decomposition optimises a failure mode representing 0.5% of observed failures, at roughly 15× token cost, on a task type the model vendor's own research says multi-agent handles poorly — while the categories that actually dominate (verification at 21.3%, specification at 41.8%) go unaddressed.

That's the whole case for boundary-based agents over role-based teams, and for spending the effort on verification instead.

Topology

Repository structure in the agent era

  • Cross-project and cross-cutting changes are as much as 20% of commits and disproportionately expensive. A trivial library change propagating to consumers takes weeks in a polyrepo versus about a day in a well-tooled monorepo.
  • Agents working across polyrepos suffer contextual fragmentation — each repository interaction is effectively a conversation with no memory of the rest of the system.
  • Airbnb compressed an 18-month migration into 6 weeks using agents in a monorepo, on the strength of whole-system visibility.
  • A 2025 CNCF survey found 42% of organisations that adopted microservices are consolidating back into larger deployable units, driven by operational and economic overhead rather than technical limits.
  • 2026 guidance converges on a modular monolith at 10–100 engineers, with service extraction justified selectively. Communication overhead makes microservices genuinely necessary somewhere past 50.
Discount this section slightly — and say so before someone else does

The 20%-of-commits and Airbnb figures come from Nx's own blog, and Nx sells monorepo tooling. The same source also reports 4× faster agent completion and a third less total effort, which this document deliberately does not quote — a vendor's own benchmark of its own product is not evidence.

What survives the discount: the mechanism is independently checkable. An agent cannot read a repository it has not been given, so cross-repo changes genuinely do cost more context. And the CNCF consolidation finding points the same way from a source with no monorepo tooling to sell. Lead with the mechanism, not the vendor's numbers.

Conway's Law, stated plainly

Service boundaries end up mirroring team boundaries whether you intend it or not. Draw team boundaries around the domains you want as services, not the other way round.

Autonomy

Bounded agent loops and deployment safety

The four bounds, and what happens without them

Every goal-driven agent loop needs a maximum iteration cap, a budget cap, a success function the agent can actually evaluate, and an escalation path for when the goal can't be met. Skip any one and you have a runaway.

The documented incident

The widely-retold case: two agents fell into a recursive clarification loop, ran for eleven days and produced a $47,000 API bill — no stop conditions, no budget limits, no cost monitoring. We could not trace it to a primary source, so use it to illustrate, never to prove.

The peer-reviewed version makes the point better. Formal resource contracts on agent loops produced a 90% reduction in token consumption and 525× lower variance across iterative workflows. Variance is the real finding: an unbounded loop's cost is not merely high, it is unpredictable, which is what makes it impossible to budget for.

Practice adds a fifth control: no-progress detection — hash each tool call with its arguments and abort when the same call repeats inside a recent window. Iteration caps catch loops that run long; this catches loops stuck immediately.

Progressive delivery

  • Canary at ~5% of traffic with SLO queries against Prometheus, expanding only when metrics hold. Argo Rollouts and Flagger are the standard implementations.
  • SLO-based rollback reverts the traffic split within seconds, not minutes.
  • Automated rollback is widely reported to resolve deployment incidents around 70% faster than manual intervention. Directional — primary source not confirmed. The mechanism is the stronger argument: seconds versus a paged engineer.
  • Feature flags decouple deploy from release entirely — code ships dark and exposure becomes a separate, instant decision.

Why this is the load-bearing evidence for autonomous deploy

The 70% figure is an argument about operational maturity, not about AI, which makes it much easier to win with a sceptical audience. It's also what makes the reversibility principle actionable: you don't argue about trusting the agent, you invest in the undo until the gate is no longer justified.

Governance context

  • 82% of enterprises reportedly have AI agents or workflows their security teams did not know existed. An autonomous pipeline with commit and deploy rights is a non-human identity with production access — register, scope, log and rotate it like any service account.
  • Agentic coding agents that execute shell commands and call APIs require the same credential lifecycle management and least-privilege controls applied to other non-human identities.
Context

DORA's amplifier model

DORA's 2025 framing is that AI is an amplifier — it magnifies an organisation's existing strengths and weaknesses rather than adding capability independently. Adoption reached 90% of respondents, with a median of two hours daily spent working with it.

The seven capabilities identified as amplifying AI's benefit are notable for how few are about AI at all:

DORA AI Capabilities Model
CapabilityOur equivalent
Clear and communicated AI stanceThe nine invariants, published
Healthy data ecosystemsSynthetic dataset, contracts repo
AI-accessible internal dataService catalogue, generated clients
Strong version control practicesCODEOWNERS, protected branches, small PRs
Working in small batchesPR size caps in CI
User-centric focusGherkin as the product contract
Quality internal platformsplatform-standards, the golden path

Nearly 40% of surveyed teams fall into the top two of seven maturity profiles, which is worth knowing when someone claims this is only achievable by elite organisations.

How to use this table

Everything marked Verified was checked against the primary source and is exact. Lead with those. They're enough to carry the whole argument on their own.

The rest are directionally sound but shouldn't be quoted to the decimal in a room where someone might check. A claim you can defend beats a bigger claim you can't — and the recommendations in this document don't change if you drop every unverified row.

All pricing figures are separately marked as indicative throughout, and need confirming in writing before they enter a budget.

Implementation

Setup, in dependency order

The toggles are set to our actual situation — Azure, GitHub, one repository, under ten engineers, Team plan. Steps are ordered so each one's prerequisites are already done. Tick steps off as you go — progress is saved in this browser.

Five stages, started by events rather than dates. A company with no pipeline has no basis for a date, and a date invites people to hold you to a number nobody could have known. Triggers are checkable — does a second team exist? has there been an incident? — so nobody has to litigate whether we're behind. The one genuine deadline is stage 1, because every item in it becomes materially more expensive the moment code exists without it.

Why the alternatives are still in here

Azure and GitHub are decided, so leave those toggles alone. They remain switchable for two reasons: it lets you read what a step's requirement actually is as opposed to how Azure happens to satisfy it, and it is the exit route if a platform decision is ever revisited. A step that reads the same on all three options is a step that never depended on the platform — which is most of them.

The two toggles genuinely worth moving are topology and team size. Repository topology is the one hard decision left open in this document, and team size changes the review rules materially — several controls that work at eight engineers are stated differently at thirty.

The place the platform matters more than syntax is reversible release. On Azure that is Container Apps revision traffic splitting, or Argo Rollouts if we end up on AKS. Both meet the requirement, and neither supplies the watcher that moves traffic back on an SLO breach — that job is ours to build either way, and the step says so rather than assuming it comes free.

The short versionFull tab: ~44 min · this box: 1 min

  • Five stages, each started by an event rather than a date, then the automated pipeline. Stage 1 is entirely conversation and contracts — no code until every one of its items is a default.
  • One team pilots the whole thing before anyone else joins. Rollout is a behaviour-change exercise, not a deployment: adoption spreads socially, and mandates don't appear anywhere in the evidence.
  • The pipeline comes last, deliberately. Automating a process you haven't run by hand just automates your misunderstanding of it.
  • If only three things get done: the strictness settings on both languages before any code exists, the shared CI setup before anyone copies and pastes it, and cost controls before the first licence is bought. All three are unrecoverable in practice — nobody turns warnings-as-errors on against a year of existing code.
  • Some steps carry a ready-to-paste Claude prompt; most deliberately don't. Mechanical work gets one. Decisions, contracts and judgments about people don't — and that split is the whole argument in miniature.
Some steps carry a prompt. Most deliberately don't.

Where a step is mechanical and verifiable, it carries a ready-to-paste Claude prompt naming the actual files and constraints — plus a verify line, because a prompt without a way to check the output is the exact failure this document warns about everywhere else.

Where a step is a decision, a contract or a judgment about people, it says so and gives you nothing to paste. That split is the point. It's this whole document's argument, applied to its own instructions: AI touches most of the work, and the decisions stay yours.

Assess an existing repo against all of this

Point Claude at a repository and have it tell you which steps are actually done. Useful for the offshore codebase, and for checking your own work later.

Audit prompt
Audit this repository against the setup below. For each item report
exactly one of: DONE / PARTIAL / MISSING / NOT-APPLICABLE, with the
file or config that proves it. Say MISSING rather than guessing.

  1. One owning team per module (CODEOWNERS present and complete)
  2. C#: <Nullable>enable</Nullable> AND
     <TreatWarningsAsErrors>true</TreatWarningsAsErrors>
     set solution-wide in Directory.Build.props, not per project
  3. TypeScript: strict, noUncheckedIndexedAccess,
     exactOptionalPropertyTypes; `any` banned as an error
  4. Architecture boundaries enforced as tests/lint, BOTH languages
     (NetArchTest or equivalent on C#, Nx tags on TS)
  5. Shared config that others extend rather than redefine, BOTH
     languages — and something that checks the two have not diverged
  6. OpenAPI generated from the API, TS client generated from that,
     CI failing on a stale client. Report PARTIAL if hand-written.
  7. Design tokens generated, not hand-written alongside
  8. Shared component library; raw interactive elements banned outside it
  9. Machine-readable catalogue of what exists, covering BOTH languages
 10. Reusable CI workflow called by every project, pinned to a tag
 11. Gherkin/.feature files executed as tests, not just written
 12. Accessibility enforced in CI (jsx-a11y, axe-core, token contrast)
 13. Secret and PHI-shaped-data scanning on every PR
 14. Migrations gated: no DropColumn/RenameColumn without a
     contract-phase label
 15. Provenance: Assisted-by trailer present on what reaches main
     (check the merge strategy, not just the branch commits)

Then rank what's missing by cost-of-delay, not by effort — what gets
more expensive the longer it's left. Give me the top three and why.
Verify: spot-check two DONE answers by opening the file it cites. An audit that reports DONE without a file reference is a model being agreeable — that's the specific failure mode to watch for here, and it's why the prompt demands proof rather than a verdict.

Configure for our situation
