Autonomous pipeline

Showing all steps.

Stage 1 · Before the first commit

Decide and fund

Nothing else starts until these land. All of it is conversation and contracts, not code.

Move on when: the first pipeline exists. Done when a repository created today inherits every one of these by default, with nobody remembering to apply them.

01

Assess what survives from the existing codebase

Now the largest open question3–5 days

C# on Azure with a React front end exists, and anything can be thrown out. Nobody has looked properly yet, so the rebuild assumption running through this document is an assumption rather than a finding. This is the step that turns it into one.

This step got more consequential, not less, when the stack was settled. While the language was open, salvage competed against a rewrite in a different stack, and "it is the wrong platform" was an easy reason to discard it. That reason is gone — the existing code is already in the target language on the target cloud, so the only question left is whether it is good C#. That is a much closer call, and it is worth doing properly rather than assuming the answer everyone already expects.

Assess these four separately — they have different answers

The four are not one question
LayerWhat to look forTypical answer
Domain knowledgeBusiness rules, eligibility logic, edge cases, integration quirks, the things learned painfully from real provider data.Almost always worth keeping, and usually the genuinely expensive part. It survives a rewrite because it moves as knowledge, not as code.
IntegrationsWorking connections to provider systems, feed formats, authentication that someone already fought through.Often worth keeping even when the code around it is poor. A working integration represents access and agreements, not just software.
Application codeTest coverage, whether the build is reproducible, how much is dead, whether a change can be made safely.The layer early signals say is weak. Judge it by "can I change this safely?", not by whether it currently runs.
InfrastructureIs it reproducible from code, or was it clicked together? Are environments separated? Is anything load-bearing undocumented?Independent of the code answer. Azure can stay even if every line above it goes.
What decides it, in one question: can a competent engineer make a small change safely, today, without archaeology? If yes, it is maintainable and worth extending regardless of how it reads. If no, it is a liability whose cost grows with every feature added to it — and no amount of AI assistance fixes a codebase nobody can reason about.
The trap to avoid in both directions. Sunk cost argues for keeping code that should go. Greenfield enthusiasm argues for discarding knowledge that took two years and real customers to acquire. Separating the four layers above is what stops one answer contaminating the others — the common good outcome is "rebuild the application, keep the domain model and the integrations".
Timebox it. Three to five days, written up, with a recommendation per layer. This assessment can absorb a month if you let it, and a month of assessment is a month of not building — while every day-one item in this stage sits waiting and getting more expensive.
Do this with Claude
Assess this codebase for salvage. Report on four layers separately
and do not blend them:

1. DOMAIN KNOWLEDGE  business rules, eligibility logic, edge cases,
   integration quirks. What has been learned here that a rewrite
   would have to rediscover? Quote the code that encodes it.
2. INTEGRATIONS      external systems it talks to, formats, auth.
   Which are working, and what would re-establishing one cost?
3. APPLICATION CODE  test coverage and whether the tests can fail;
   dead code; build reproducibility; the three files most changed
   and whether they are safe to change again.
4. INFRASTRUCTURE    reproducible from code or clicked together?
   Environments separated? Anything load-bearing and undocumented?

For each layer give KEEP / EXTEND / REPLACE with the evidence.
Where you are uncertain, say so and name what you would need to
look at. Do not recommend a rewrite because the code is unfamiliar
or unfashionable  only because it cannot be changed safely.
Verify: take the two files it calls safest and the two it calls worst, and read them yourself. An assessment you have not spot-checked is a model being agreeable — and this is the one decision where being agreeable costs the most, because it sets the whole stack direction.
02

Settle the repository topology

Blocks everythingHalf a day

Ask explicitly: is polyrepo a decision someone made, or an assumption nobody examined? Make sure everyone in the room understands that monorepo does not mean monolith.

Why now: the cost is asymmetric. Polyrepo too early is expensive and hard to reverse; monorepo too long is annoying and easy to fix. It also determines whether you need a platform person from day one.
With the platform and language settled, this is the one genuinely hard decision still yours to make — so it is the one that deserves the argument. Note it has an extra wrinkle here that a single-language shop does not have: one repository holding a .NET solution and a TypeScript workspace needs path-filtered CI from the first commit, or every pull request pays for both toolchains. That is a morning's work if done at the start and a persistent tax if not.

No prompt for this one. It's a decision, not a task — and now the only one on this page that is genuinely hard to reverse. Claude can argue both sides; it can't own the answer.

03

Name one owning team per repo or module

Free

No shared ownership, no exceptions. If two teams both need to own something, the boundary is wrong — fix the boundary.

Why now: costs nothing on day one and is near-impossible to retrofit. Shared ownership means nobody reviews carefully and nobody is accountable when standards drift.
04

Choose the work tracker and design the label taxonomy

Blocks the pipelineHalf a day

GitHub Issues unless a product function already exists. Then design the labels, because they are the pipeline's state machine, not decoration: ask:new to start it, lane:1–lane:4 for risk, size:S|M|L, svc:* for the affected area, stage:* for where a run has got to.

Why now: every automated stage triggers on a label and reports by writing one. Choosing a tracker the agent can't read and write natively means building and maintaining a sync layer forever — and a label scheme invented later means migrating every open issue.
05

Sign the plan, wire SSO, set per-user cost caps

Before any seat is issued

Usage-based billing from the start. Per-user caps with alerts at 75% and 90%. Spend visible to the engineer generating it. Confirm in writing whether Claude Code is on all seats or Premium only.

Why now: Microsoft cancelled 5,000 seats over unforecast spend despite measuring a 24% throughput gain — because they piloted on flat rate and the real number arrived all at once. Retrofitting cost controls after habits form is the documented failure.
05

Provision API keys with hard spend limits

Before any use

One key per person or workload, never a shared key. Organisation-level and per-key spend ceilings with alerting.

Why now: without seat-level governance, keys are your only attribution mechanism. A shared key means you cannot answer "who spent this" — which is the question that ends programmes.
06

If a design system is being commissioned, brief it now

Only if using an external firmLong lead time

Commissioning brand and components externally is a good trade — brand quality is hard and it isn't what an engineering team should be discovering. But four conditions belong in the brief, not in a conversation afterwards:

  • A standalone, versioned library plus an example app that consumes it as a published dependency — not a demo importing the source
  • Built on headless primitives — Radix, React Aria or Ark UI
  • Tokens as DTCG JSON, icons as SVG sources, guidelines as Markdown, in a repository we control
  • WCAG 2.1 AA contrast proven across the token set before sign-off
Settle the access model before the brief is signed. We run GitHub with Enterprise Managed Users, which does not support outside collaborators — an external firm cannot simply be added to one of our repositories. Two workable routes: they build in their own repository and deliver a versioned package plus a source drop, or they are provisioned as managed users through our identity provider, which means licences, an access review and probably a contract clause. Pick one now; it has a procurement cycle attached and the brief is being written.
Why the example-app test specifically: it's the one condition that can't be fudged. If they can build an app consuming the library as a dependency, we have a real library we can version and change. If they can't, we have a website with nice buttons in it, and extracting a library from it lands on us at exactly the moment we least want the work.
Why in Stage 1: vendor lead times are measured in months, and every one of these is far cheaper to ask for than to retrofit. Contrast in particular — a palette that fails AA discovered after the logo is on the door means the brand changes, not the code.
Standards: the token pipeline and the delivery conditions →
07

Get legal confirmation on UK staff processing US health data

One email

UK GDPR's territorial reach follows where processing happens, not where the data subject is. Pair it with the written rule: synthetic data only in development, always.

Why now: it's a cheap email today and an awkward discovery once a UK team is hired and working.
08

Sign the Anthropic BAA and turn Zero Data Retention on

Before any real workDays, not hours

Two separate actions, both required, neither automatic. Then write down what the BAA actually covers, because the coverage is narrower than people assume: Claude Code on CLI and Desktop-local only, and only with ZDR enabled. Not covered — Claude Code Web, Desktop remote, Workbench, the Batch/Files/Skills APIs, and any third-party MCP data flow.

Why now: ZDR and the Covered Models programme are mutually exclusive, so this is a real choice made once. And the exclusion list is the part that bites — a team that believes "we have a BAA" will eventually route member data through an MCP server that sits outside it. Publish the list next to the invariants so the boundary is visible rather than assumed.

No prompt for this one. Legal and commercial. Never take a model's word for what a contract covers — confirm the exclusion list in writing with Anthropic.

09

Publish the nine invariants

An hour

One owning team per repo · per-user cost caps · AGENTS.md with one-line CLAUDE.md · every rule in CI mirrored in hooks · reviewer subagent before any human · Gherkin as the product contract · telemetry on from day one · synthetic data only · one hand-edited source per fact, everything else generated.

Why now: writing them down stops them being re-litigated in every subsequent meeting, and shrinks the decision surface to the four things that genuinely fork.
10

Decide where it runs — or defer it deliberately

Currently openHalf a day to scope

Azure, decided. What remains is to name which Azure services satisfy the four requirements, and — the part that gets skipped — who builds the piece Azure does not give you.

The one that matters: Container Apps supplies a weighted traffic split between revisions; it does not supply anything that watches an SLO and moves the split back. That watcher is a job somebody writes. Name them in this step, because the whole stage-11 automation argument rests on it and it is the easiest thing in the plan to assume is included.

What it blocks: almost nothing. Twenty-one of the twenty-five standards are indifferent to it, and every stage-one item is a setting or a schema that needs no server. Do not let this conversation delay them.
What it genuinely decides: four requirements — reversible release, an environment per change, test-result storage and queryable metrics. Each has a valid answer either way; only reversible release is load-bearing enough to change the plan if it cannot be met.
The confidence table and the four requirements →
11

Work through the Enterprise Managed Users constraints

Blocks three things in this planHalf a day, plus lead time

EMU is a stricter environment than standard GitHub, and three of its restrictions land directly on decisions already made here. Find out now, not when someone is blocked.

What EMU changes, and what to do about it
ConstraintWhat it collides withThe move
No outside collaborators The design firm delivering the component library. Under EMU you cannot simply add an external contributor to a repository. Decide the handover model before the brief is signed: they build in their own repository and deliver a versioned package plus a source drop, or they are provisioned as managed users through our identity provider — which means licences, an access review, and probably a contract clause. Neither is hard; discovering it late is.
Apps need enterprise approval The Claude Code GitHub app, and any other integration the pipeline leans on. Get the app allow-listed at enterprise level early. Whoever administers the enterprise may not be in this team, and their queue is not our queue. Confirm before stage 5 depends on it.
Managed accounts are walled in Engineer habits — no personal account, restricted forking, restricted public contribution. Say it in onboarding rather than letting people find out. Also affects any workflow that assumed a public fork or a personal sandbox.
Enterprise Cloud licensing The cost model, which was written against the Team tier. Roughly a fivefold difference per seat. Corrected on the cost tab — but confirm the actual negotiated rate rather than list price.
The upside, and it is real. Everything this plan wants from identity comes free with EMU: accounts provisioned and de-provisioned from the identity provider, no orphaned access when someone leaves, enterprise-level audit, and app installation that cannot happen by accident. The governance layer other organisations bolt on later is already here.
The one to action today: the design firm question. Everything else can be resolved in the week it is discovered; that one has a contract and a procurement cycle attached, and the brief is being written now.
Where this sits in what we know →
12

Set the day-one platform controls — GitHub

Before the first commitAn hour

Branch protection requiring a review from someone other than the author. CODEOWNERS mapping paths to owning teams. Environments for dev, staging and production, with a required reviewer on production. Issue forms applying ask:new on submission.

Settle the Lane 1 bypass while you are in the settings. A ruleset cannot be conditional on file path, so "Lane 1 auto-merges" requires the pipeline identity to be a named bypass actor. Either add it now, deliberately, with the Lane 1 path patterns owned as a sensitive file — or decide Lane 1 also needs a human and drop the auto-merge. Both are fine. Leaving it unresolved means someone adds the bypass under deadline pressure in month three and nobody reviews the decision.

Note the plan floor: branch protection, CODEOWNERS-required reviewers and environment protection on private repositories all need Team or above. All three are load-bearing here, so the free tier is not an option.
12

Set the day-one platform controls — GitLab

Before the first commitAn hour

Protected branches with required approvals and prevent approval by author set. CODEOWNERS mapping paths to owning teams. Protected environments for dev, staging and production, with an approval rule on production. A description template plus a label rule applying ask:new.

Where GitLab is genuinely stronger: self-hosting — which matters more than usual if the hosting decision lands on-premise. Running source control and CI in the same estate as the product removes a whole class of network and residency argument.
Where it costs you: no first-party Claude Code action, so the agent stages are wired by hand against the headless CLI. Budget a few extra days at stage 5.
12

Set the day-one platform controls — Azure DevOps

Before the first commitAn hour

Branch policies requiring a reviewer, with allow requestors to approve their own changes off. Required reviewers by path. Environments with approval checks for dev, staging and production. Work item templates carrying the intake fields.

When this is the right answer: the parent mandates Entra ID and Azure. Then take the whole estate on ADO rather than splitting — a coherent ADO setup beats a GitHub/ADO hybrid, which makes the agent cross a vendor boundary at nearly every stage transition.
The trade: as with GitLab, agent integration is DIY. Note the plan's own evidence caveat though — the study measuring ~24% more merged PRs ran on Azure DevOps, so the platform is demonstrably fine for this class of work.
13

Fund the platform function

Polyrepo only

One dedicated person from day one. Under a monorepo this is part-time until roughly 15 engineers.

Why now: polyrepo forces you to build distribution, contracts and drift detection before they hurt. Discovering that in month four turns into an unbudgeted headcount request.
Stage 2 · As the pipeline is built

Paved road v0, and instrument as you go

The minimum that makes every later step cheaper — and the point at which telemetry is emitted rather than excavated later.

Move on when: a second team appears, the first production incident happens, or the first high-risk surface lands. Done when every metric can be answered without asking a person.

14

Scaffold both halves, strict, with boundaries enforced in each

Everything sits on this2–3 days

One repository, two toolchains, and the strictness settings on both are the single most valuable hour in this entire plan — they are the reason the C# decision pays for itself, and they are effectively impossible to introduce later. Turning warnings-as-errors on against an existing codebase means fixing thousands of them at the worst possible moment, so in practice nobody does.

Directory.Build.propssolution-wide, so no project can opt out 
<Project>
  <PropertyGroup>
    <Nullable>enable</Nullable>
    <TreatWarningsAsErrors>true</TreatWarningsAsErrors>
    <AnalysisLevel>latest-recommended</AnalysisLevel>
    <EnforceCodeStyleInBuild>true</EnforceCodeStyleInBuild>
    <LangVersion>latest</LangVersion>
  </PropertyGroup>
</Project>
tsconfig.base.jsonthe TypeScript equivalent, equally non-negotiable 
"strict": true,
"noUncheckedIndexedAccess": true,       // array access returns T | undefined
"exactOptionalPropertyTypes": true,     // optional !== "can be undefined"
"noImplicitOverride": true,
"verbatimModuleSyntax": true

Then close the escape hatches on both sides. TypeScript: @typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any as an error, and ban-ts-comment requiring a written justification of at least ten characters. C#: a build check rejecting any #pragma warning disable without a justification comment on the same line. Same rule, same intent, two mechanisms — and this step is where the habit of doing both starts.

Why this is step 14 and not step 40: Directory.Build.props and @acme/config are the two places every standard in this document is ultimately expressed. Nothing else can be enforced until they exist. And build the parity check in the same sitting — a script that lists the rules configured on each side and fails when one gains a rule the other lacks. It is an hour of work and it is the only thing standing between a two-language codebase and two codebases.
Do this with Claude
Scaffold a single repository with a .NET solution and an Nx TypeScript
workspace side by side.

.NET (src/): Acme.Domain, Acme.Application, Acme.Infrastructure,
Acme.Api (ASP.NET Core), Acme.Domain.Tests (xUnit).
TypeScript: apps/web-member and apps/web-admin (React + Vite);
packages/tokens, ui, api-client, utils, config, testing.

Then:
1. Directory.Build.props at the solution root with Nullable=enable,
   TreatWarningsAsErrors=true, AnalysisLevel=latest-recommended,
   EnforceCodeStyleInBuild=true. Solution-wide, not per project.
2. tsconfig.base.json with strict, noUncheckedIndexedAccess,
   exactOptionalPropertyTypes, noImplicitOverride,
   verbatimModuleSyntax.
3. @acme/config holding shared ESLint, tsconfig and Prettier presets.
   Every package extends them; none redefines.
4. Architecture tests in Acme.Domain.Tests using NetArchTest:
   Acme.Domain depends on nothing in this solution; Acme.Api does not
   reference EF Core types; Acme.Application does not reference
   Acme.Infrastructure.
5. Nx tags + @nx/enforce-module-boundaries: ui cannot import from
   apps; nothing imports a feature folder; utils imports nothing local.
6. no-explicit-any as error; ban-ts-comment needing 10+ chars.

Do not add application code. Show me the two boundary rule sets side
by side before applying them.
Verify by breaking it, four times. dotnet build and nx run-many -t lint typecheck must pass on the empty scaffold. Then: add an unused variable to a C# file and confirm the build fails, not just warns. Add a reference from Acme.Domain to Acme.Infrastructure and confirm the architecture test fails. Import apps/web-member from packages/ui and confirm lint fails. A boundary rule you have never seen fail is a boundary rule you do not have.
15

Shared reusable CI workflow, plus one repo calling it

Highest leverageHalf a day

The capability: one CI definition, held centrally, that every project calls in a few lines — so improving the pipeline improves every project by moving a tag, rather than by editing N copies.

Every major platform has this and they are near-equivalent: GitHub reusable workflows (workflow_call) · GitLab include with extends · Azure DevOps template references. Pick per the platform decision; the shape below is the same on all three. Pin callers to a tag, never a branch.

service-x/.github/workflows/ci.ymlGitHub shown — the other two are the same shape 
name: ci
on: [push, pull_request]
jobs:
  ci:
    uses: acme/platform-standards/.github/workflows/service-ci.yml@v1
    secrets: inherit
Why now: this must exist before the first workflow gets copy-pasted. After that you're doing archaeology across N repos to work out what each one diverged into. Pin callers to a tag, never a branch.
16

AGENTS.md in every repo, CLAUDE.md as a one-line import

An hour

CLAUDE.md contains exactly one line: @AGENTS.md. The repo's AGENTS.md carries only what's true of that repo — what it does, its domain vocabulary, its local quirks. Organisation-wide conventions go in the plugin.

Why now: cross-vendor portability costs one line. Microsoft moved 5,000 engineers between agents in six months; whatever we standardise on today, we may not be on in three years. And keep it short — a bloated instruction file causes rules inside it to be ignored.
17

Three subagents: explore, code-reviewer, security-reviewer

Half a day

All read-only with explicit tools: lines. The reviewers need the calibration paragraph telling them that finding nothing is a valid outcome.

security-reviewer is a separate agent rather than an instruction to the code reviewer, and is weighted towards the classes that actually show up in generated code: authorisation gaps, injection, and SSRF — the single most common vulnerability class in AI-written samples. It also owns the check that no route touching member data is missing an authorisation test.

agents/code-reviewer.mdexcerpt 
---
name: code-reviewer
description: Review a diff for correctness against its stated requirements
tools: Read, Grep, Glob, Bash(git diff *)
---

You review a diff you did not write. You have deliberately not been
given the reasoning that produced it.

Check: does it satisfy every requirement it claims to (check R-numbers
in specs/*/spec.md); correctness bugs; scope creep; whether the tests
can actually fail.

Do NOT report style, naming, or architectural preference.

CALIBRATION — read carefully. You will feel pressure to find something
because that is what you were asked to do. Resist it. Reporting a
non-issue costs more than missing a minor one, because chasing phantom
findings produces defensive code and tests for impossible cases.
If the diff is sound, say "no correctness issues found" and stop.
That is a valid and common outcome.
Why these three: they carry the strongest evidence of the candidates — context isolation and independence from the work. Splitting security out is deliberate: a single reviewer asked to weigh correctness and security together reliably under-weights security. Without the calibration paragraph either reviewer invents findings, which costs tokens in the fix loop and produces over-engineering.
18

Turn telemetry on

Free

Set CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TELEMETRY=1 and an OTLP endpoint in committed settings. Point it at a local Docker Grafana stack or a free-tier account. Leave all content logging off — OTEL_LOG_USER_PROMPTS, OTEL_LOG_TOOL_DETAILS and OTEL_LOG_RAW_API_BODIES stay unset.

Why now: costs nothing and you cannot generate history retroactively. When you need to answer "has our rejection rate worsened since we changed the reviewer agent," you'll want six months of data. Content is redacted by default and should stay that way in a healthcare context.
19

Define the environments and where secrets live

Blocks deploymentA day

The capability: three named environments — dev, staging, production — each able to carry its own scoped secrets and its own approval rule. Production carries a required-reviewer rule from day one, even while everything is still manual; that is the control stage 11 later relies on for Lane 4.

GitHub Environments, GitLab protected environments, or Azure DevOps environments with approval checks. All three do this natively.

Secrets live in the cloud provider's secret manager, referenced by GitHub Environment secrets. Never in .env files, never in the repo, and no production secret is ever readable from a developer machine.

Why now: environments are almost impossible to retrofit once things are deployed, because everything grows implicit assumptions about a single environment. The required-reviewer rule specifically wants setting while it costs nothing — adding a gate later reads as distrust; having one from the start is just how it works.
20

Define risk lanes and wire them to CODEOWNERS

An hour

Four lanes by blast radius, routed by path pattern so the lane follows what you touched rather than what the author claims.

Why now: review capacity is the binding constraint, so spending it uniformly spends it badly. Doing this before the first feature means the pattern is established rather than retrofitted.
21

Create platform-standards, build the plugin and marketplace

Polyrepo only2–3 days

Package skills, agents, hooks and commands as a plugin; distribute through Organisation settings → Plugins. Add the version-drift CI check immediately.

.github/workflows/version-drift.ymlDo not skip this 
- run: |
    P=$(jq -r '.version' .claude-plugin/plugin.json)
    M=$(jq -r '.plugins[]|select(.name=="acme-platform")|.version' \
          .claude-plugin/marketplace.json)
    if [ "$P" != "$M" ]; then
      echo "::error::Version mismatch ($P vs $M). Claude Code will"
      echo "silently install the stale version. Bump both."
      exit 1
    fi
Why the check: both manifests carry a version. Bump one and forget the other and Claude Code installs the stale plugin with no error message. You'll ship a fix, watch it not take effect, and lose an afternoon.
21

Commit the shared .claude/ directory

Monorepo — much simpler

Agents, skills, hooks and commands live in .claude/ at the repo root. Inheritance is automatic on clone; changes go through normal code review.

Why this is enough: a plugin's only real advantage is distribution across repositories, which is worth nothing here. Structure the directory as if it were a plugin and promoting it later is packaging, not a rewrite.
22

Create the contracts repo with generated clients

Before the second service2–3 days

OpenAPI specs emitted from each service, event schemas, FHIR profiles. Publishes typed clients — TypeScript and C# — as versioned packages. Add breaking-change detection with oasdiff on every PR.

Why now: in a monorepo the compiler catches cross-service breakage; in a polyrepo nothing does. Retrofitting contracts onto services already calling each other by hand-rolled HTTP means changing both sides of every integration you have. Also the single best cross-repo context mechanism — an agent can't read another repo, but it can read types that are true by construction.
22

Wire the contract: OpenAPI out of C#, TypeScript client in

Before the first feature, not the second serviceA day

This is mitigation 3, and it is the step that replaces something a single language would have given us free. The API surface emits contracts/openapi.json at build time; NSwag or Kiota generates packages/api-client from that spec; CI regenerates and fails if the committed client differs. oasdiff fails the build on a breaking change. Event schemas and FHIR profiles live alongside, hand-authored, with their own owners.

Why before the first feature rather than the second service: with two languages the boundary exists on day one — it is not a cross-service concern that arrives later, it is the line between the API and the browser. Every hour the front end spends calling the API through hand-written types is an hour of work to undo, and a hand-written client is very hard to remove once screens depend on its particular shape.
The failure mode to design against: nobody hand-edits a generated client on purpose. They do it when the generator emits something awkward and the deadline is Friday — and then the staleness check is disabled "temporarily". So make the generated client physically unpleasant to edit: a header comment saying it is generated, a .gitattributes entry marking it linguist-generated so it collapses in diffs, and the CI check failing loudly rather than warning. If the generator output genuinely needs adjusting, the fix goes in the generator config or the C# annotations, never the output.
23

Stand up @acme/tokens and the generation chain

Before any componentA day

DTCG JSON as the only hand-edited source. A build step — Style Dictionary or equivalent — generates the Tailwind theme, CSS custom properties and, if native ever happens, a native theme. @acme/config's Tailwind preset consumes the generated file and nothing else.

Two CI checks from the first commit: the generated output must be current, and every foreground/background pair the token set permits must clear WCAG AA contrast — 4.5:1 for body text, 3:1 for large text and interface components.

Do this even if the design firm is still being briefed — especially then. The chain is what you hand them, not what you build after they deliver. Standing it up now with placeholder colours means the brief can say "tokens as DTCG JSON, here is the repository and the build that consumes them" rather than describing a format in prose and hoping. And it makes the contrast condition testable on delivery day rather than a promise nobody checked, which is the single most expensive thing to discover late — because the fix is that the brand changes, not the code.
Why before components: a component built against a hand-written colour is a component to redo. Get the chain running with placeholder brand values if the real palette isn't ready — the pipeline matters more than the values in it on day one.
Why generated rather than written alongside: a hand-maintained Tailwind theme next to a token file is two definitions of one colour. They agree the day they're written and drift from then on, and the token file — which design tooling still trusts — becomes the one that's wrong.
Do this with Claude
Build packages/tokens.

Source: DTCG-format JSON in tokens/ (color.json, space.json,
type.json). These are the only hand-edited files.

Generate into generated/ with Style Dictionary:
  - tailwind.theme.ts  (consumed by @acme/config's Tailwind preset)
  - tokens.css         (CSS custom properties)
  - tokens.native.ts   (flat constants, for a future native app)

Add two CI checks:
1. Regenerate and fail if generated/ differs from the committed
   output — the "stale generated file" guard.
2. Contrast: for every foreground/background pair the token set
   permits, assert WCAG AA — 4.5:1 for body text, 3:1 for large
   text and UI components. Fail with the offending pair named.

Seed with placeholder brand values. The pipeline matters more than
the values on day one.
Verify: hand-edit a hex directly in generated/tailwind.theme.ts and push — CI must fail on the staleness check. Then set two token colours to a failing pair and confirm CI names them. A contrast check that passes on a deliberately bad palette isn't running.
24

Create the remaining shared homes, empty

Do this before the first featureHalf a day

@acme/ui (components) · @acme/utils (pure front-end helpers) · @acme/api-client (generated, so create the folder and the generation script but no content) · @acme/testing and Acme.TestKit (fixtures, one per language) — joining @acme/config and Directory.Build.props from step 14 and @acme/tokens from step 23. Each with an owner, a README stating what belongs in it, and a tag or project reference so boundary rules can name it.

Why empty, and why now: the destination has to exist before the first shared thing is written, or it lands in a feature folder and is invisible to everyone else forever. Half a day of empty folders now versus an extraction project later. It is also what makes the reuse catalogue meaningful — a catalogue of things scattered across feature folders is a list, not an architecture.
The README is not filler, it is the reuse gate's documentation. Two languages means "where does this go?" now has a wrong answer available in the other language, and the most common cross-boundary duplication is a formatter or validator written twice. Each README says explicitly what belongs there and what belongs on the other side — @acme/utils's says "check the catalogue for a C# counterpart before adding a formatter here", and that sentence will earn its keep within a month.
25

Ship the issue forms and wire the label automation

Half a day

The capability: a structured intake form whose submission applies a starting label automatically. Required fields: the outcome wanted, who it affects, how we'd know it worked, and what should explicitly not happen.

GitHub issue forms (.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/*.yml), GitLab description templates plus a label rule, or Azure DevOps work item templates. All three work; what matters is that the fields are required and the label is automatic.

Why the form matters more than it looks: free-text asks produce triage guesses. Structured fields give the classifier something to classify, and the "what should not happen" field is the one that most reliably surfaces the negative requirement nobody thought to state — the cheapest defect you will ever catch.
26

Stand up the Azure estate, declaratively

If cloud2–3 days

Accounts or subscriptions separated per environment, described as code so environments are reproducible and every change is reviewed like any other. A managed database, somewhere to run containers, and object storage for test results.

Use OpenTofu rather than Terraform — drop-in, MPL-2.0, and the licence cannot change under you.

Why declarative rather than clicking: it puts infrastructure under the same review and audit trail as application code, which is most of what an auditor actually wants to see. It also makes the second environment nearly free, and the second environment is what stops one shared box becoming a queue.
26

Stand up the host estate and the deployment path

If on-premiseThe long pole

Machine images or container hosts built from a versioned definition, not configured by hand. A load balancer that can be flipped between two pools. Backup and restore that has actually been rehearsed. Somewhere durable for test results — a database table is fine.

The deployment path is the part that matters: blue/green behind the load balancer, with an automated health check flipping the pool back on breach. That satisfies the reversibility requirement as completely as any cloud-native controller does.

Be honest about the cost shape. On-premise moves hosting from a monthly bill to capital plus a fraction of a person — and that person is a real, ongoing commitment, not a one-off. It is frequently the cheaper answer for predictable load; it is almost never the cheaper answer if you forget to count them.
The item most often skipped: an environment per change. It is harder here than on cloud, and if it genuinely is not feasible, say so and shorten the feedback loop somewhere else — because every control in this plan assumes small batches, and small batches assume you can test one safely.
26

Build everything that doesn't depend on where it runs

Unblocked today

While the hosting question is open, the following are all buildable and none of them care about the answer: the workspace and its boundary rules, the shared config and token packages, the reuse catalogue, the test layering contract, provenance, the telemetry schema, review controls, and the whole standards register.

Why this step exists at all: to make it explicit that the hosting conversation is not a blocker. The most expensive mistake available here is treating an open infrastructure question as a reason to defer the settings and schemas — those are the items that become unrecoverable once code exists.
27

Start the synthetic dataset

Long lead time

Members, discharge summaries, ADT messages, care plans, benefit configs, and the awkward edge cases. Synthea gets you a starting corpus; the domain-specific layers you build.

Why now: without a convincing synthetic dataset, engineers will reach for real data to test with, and no hook catches every path. It's also your eval substrate later, so it pays for itself twice.
Stage 3 · Once one team can run it end to end

One team pilots the whole flow

Resist expanding here. You want the setup refined against reality before other teams inherit its rough edges.

Move on when: the pilot team has run the full lifecycle on real work and the rough edges are logged. Done when they'd choose the paved road over going round it.

28

Pick the pilot team by PR throughput, not enthusiasm

Evidence-led

Engineers with a baseline of two or more PRs per week were 31% more likely to retain the tooling. Seniority and stated enthusiasm predicted adoption but not retention.

Why this matters: adoption spreads socially rather than by instruction. Reported figures put the effect of a skip-level peer using it at around 216% on trial odds, a manager at 82%, a reviewer at 54% — directional rather than confirmed, but the ranking is the useful part, and nothing about mandates appears in the top predictors at all. Pick a team whose success will be visible to others.

No prompt for this one. People, politics and who owes whom a favour. The data narrows the field; you pick.

29

Ship the spec commands and run the full lifecycle on real work

Half a day to build

/spec, /plan, /tasks as skills with disable-model-invocation: true. EARS requirements, Gherkin acceptance. Product approves the Gherkin; the tech lead approves the requirements.

Why the format: EARS grammar makes vagueness ungrammatical — you can't write "handles errors gracefully" because the template demands a named trigger, system and response. Specification and design originate 41.8% of multi-agent failures, so this is the highest-leverage half hour anyone spends.
30

Make the Gherkin executable — wire playwright-bdd

The load-bearing stepHalf a day

Point playwright-bdd at specs/**/*.feature so the scenarios product approved run directly as end-to-end tests. Step definitions live in @acme/testing and are shared across features. CI fails on any .feature file with no matching step definition.

Set the sharding and scope rules now, in the same sitting. Every acceptance scenario needs an end-to-end test and none can move down the pyramid — so the suite grows linearly with features while the ten-minute budget does not move. Decide it while the suite is small: shard across CI workers, run only the scenarios for services the PR touched, run everything on merge and nightly. The ten minutes is a promise about the pull-request loop, not a cap on how many scenarios may exist. Left until it hurts, this conversation becomes an argument for writing fewer acceptance tests, which is the one outcome to avoid.
Note this step is untouched by the language decision, which is worth saying because it is the load-bearing link in the whole spec argument. playwright-bdd drives a browser, so it does not care that the back end is C#. The chain from a scenario product approved to the test gating production stays single, unsplit, and language-independent.
Why this is the step that makes the rest true: without it, Gherkin is documentation and will drift from behaviour within a month — at which point the spec is worse than useless, because people still trust it. With it, the scenario product signed off in stage 3 is literally the test gating production in stage 10. Every claim in this document about specs being the contract depends on this one wiring job.
31

Generate the reuse catalogue and wire the discovery stage

Stops the codebase rotting1–2 days

Two walkers, one file. A Roslyn analyser walks the .NET solution and a ts-morph script walks the TypeScript workspace; the merge step emits a single catalogue.json with a lang field on every entry. Generated in CI per commit and published as an artifact — not committed, because a committed catalogue goes stale on every merge and turns one merge into a queue-wide red build. Then the reuse-analysis skill reads it and maps each ask onto reuse / extend / new-because.

Why generated, and why a blocking stage: a hand-maintained index is stale in a fortnight and then actively misleads the agent into building duplicates. And the check has to block — duplicated code rose 81% and cross-file reuse fell 35% across 623 million AI-assisted changes, because new code is always the path of least resistance. An advisory reminder does not change that; a stage that fails on an unjustified "new" does.
Do this with Claude
Write tools/catalogue — TWO walkers and a merge step, emitting one
catalogue.json that covers the whole repository.

1. tools/catalogue/cs — a Roslyn-based console app walking the .NET
   solution. One entry per public type, service and public static
   helper: { name, lang: "cs", assembly, file, kind, signature,
   xmldoc, usageCount }
2. tools/catalogue/ts — a ts-morph script walking the Nx workspace.
   One entry per exported component, hook, schema and helper:
   { name, lang: "ts", package, file, kind, signature, docblock,
     usageCount }
3. tools/catalogue/merge — combines both into catalogue.json.

Rules:
  - Generated in CI per commit, published as a build artifact.
    Do NOT commit it — a committed catalogue is stale after every
    merge and reds the whole PR queue.
  - The merge step FAILS if either walker returned zero entries. A
    half-catalogue is more dangerous than none, because the half it
    misses looks checked.
  - Exclude anything not public / not exported from an entry point.
  - Mark generated files (packages/api-client) so the reuse skill
    does not offer generated types as reusable building blocks.

Then write the reuse-analysis skill. Given an ask, it reads
catalogue.json and produces specs/<slug>/reuse.md mapping every
capability the ask needs to exactly one of:
  reuse <name>  |  extend <name>  |  new — because <reason>

For every "new", it must ALSO state whether a same-purpose thing
exists in the OTHER language, and why duplicating it is correct.
Cross-boundary duplication is the one kind no tool here catches.

The stage FAILS if any "new" has no reason, or if a "new" names a
capability already in the catalogue.
Verify, three ways. Run it against an ask you know duplicates something — "add a notification banner" when a banner already exists — and it must return reuse, not new. Feed it an unjustified "new" and confirm the stage fails rather than warns. Then break one walker on purpose — point the Roslyn one at an empty solution — and confirm the merge step fails instead of quietly emitting a TypeScript-only catalogue. That third check is the one that matters most and the one nobody thinks to run.
32

Install the comment-hygiene and boundary lint rules

Half a day

Two custom ESLint rules. The first fails any comment narrating history — "removed the old handler", "no longer uses the cache", "previously did X" — because those describe code that isn't there. The second is Nx's module-boundary rule, configured so UI cannot import from data access and no package may import from a feature folder.

Why a lint rule rather than a review convention: historical comments are the single most confusing thing an agent can read, because it treats them as current fact and writes code around behaviour that no longer exists. Reviewers miss them consistently — they read as harmless context. A rule catches every one, on every change, forever.
Do this with Claude
Write a custom ESLint rule "no-historical-comments" in @acme/config.

Flag any comment matching, case-insensitively:
  removed | deleted | used to | no longer | previously | formerly |
  changed from | was (using|doing) | instead of the old |
  legacy approach

Message: "Comment narrates a past change. Describe what the code
does now, or delete the comment."

Also add rules for: no commented-out code blocks, and TODO comments
requiring an issue link.

Include a test file with passing and failing cases for each pattern,
and wire the rule into the shared preset so every package inherits it.
Verify: add // removed the old retry handler to any file and confirm lint fails. Then check the false-positive rate on real prose — a comment saying "the API previously returned null, so we guard" is a legitimate explanation, and if the rule blocks that class of comment it's too aggressive and people will disable it.
33

Build the golden path command

1–2 days

One command scaffolds a new service that is conformant on creation: AGENTS.md, CODEOWNERS, the six-line CI caller, a contract stub registered in the contracts repo, and the deploy pipeline.

Why measure this: time from "we need a new service" to "deployed and conformant" is the best single measure of whether the paved road exists. If teams copy-paste from an existing service instead of using the command, the golden path has stopped being the easy path.
34

Add PR size caps and Playwright conventions

Half a day

Cap diffs in CI. Encode test rules as a skill plus lint: getByRole first and data-testid second with CSS and XPath banned; no sleeps; one scenario per test named for its requirement ID; suite under ten minutes.

Why: agents will happily produce a 2,000-line diff nobody can meaningfully review, and small batches is one of DORA's seven amplifying capabilities. Role-based locators also only work on accessible markup, so the suite doubles as an accessibility forcing function.
35

Adopt WCAG 2.1 AA and enforce it in CI

Likely a legal requirementA day

Name the standard explicitly, then enforce it mechanically in four places: eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y as errors, axe-core assertions in the Playwright suite failing on any serious or critical violation, the existing getByRole-first locator rule, and the token contrast gate from step 23.

The half that headless primitives don't cover: Radix and React Aria give you focus, keyboard and screen-reader behaviour — genuinely the expensive part. They cannot give you colour contrast, because that comes from the brand palette. A palette failing AA is a common audit finding and the worst to receive late, since the fix is that the brand changes. If a design firm is producing the tokens, make proven AA contrast a condition of sign-off rather than something discovered after integration.
Why this is not optional for this product specifically: a Medicare aftercare service serves a population with materially higher rates of visual, motor and cognitive impairment — and Section 1557 of the ACA and Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act attach to health programmes receiving federal financial assistance, with HHS rulemaking naming WCAG 2.1 AA as the standard. That's the level to adopt, because it's the level that's cited. Retrofitting accessibility is also one of the most expensive kinds of rework there is, because it changes markup structure rather than styling.
And it's nearly free here: Radix primitives arrive accessible, and the getByRole convention already forces accessible markup because the tests can't find elements otherwise. This step mostly makes an accident into a commitment — and gives an agent a checkable rule instead of a preference.
36

Wire error tracking, separately from metrics

Free tier is enoughAn hour

Sentry or equivalent, with releases tagged so an exception maps to the commit and the feature flag state that produced it. Source maps uploaded from CI.

Why it's a separate tool from Prometheus: they answer different questions. Prometheus tells the canary that the error rate crossed a threshold — enough to trigger a rollback, useless for finding the cause. Error tracking gives the stack trace and the affected users. Auto-rollback without error tracking means you reliably survive incidents while never learning anything from them, which is a slow way to accumulate the same bug repeatedly.
37

Ship /explain-diff and require a written approval

The comprehension mechanismAn hour

A command that walks a reviewer through a diff and asks them to state, in their own words, what changed and why. Approving a PR requires a one-sentence summary in the review body — a bare approve is rejected by CI. Watch time-to-approve alongside PR size.

Why it exists: this is the single point where "developers always fully understand the code" either happens or quietly stops happening. Thirty seconds, impossible to fake without reading the diff, and it leaves a plain-English trail on every change. Approvals landing two minutes after a PR opens mean nobody is reading — and you want to know that now, not during an incident.
38

Make a new engineer productive on day one

Do it while the pilot team remembersA day

One command from a fresh clone to a running system with seeded synthetic data — a devcontainer, or a version manager plus a single bootstrap script. Then a short CONTRIBUTING.md covering the lanes, how to run /spec, and what the hooks will stop them doing and why.

Why now and not later: the pilot team has just discovered every undocumented step, and that knowledge evaporates within weeks. Write it while it's still irritating.
Why it matters more than usual here: this setup has a lot of machinery — hooks that block things, lanes that route reviews, a spec stage before code. An engineer who hits three unexplained blocks in their first week concludes the tooling is hostile, and that impression is very hard to reverse. Onboarding is where adoption is won or lost, and the evidence is that adoption spreads socially rather than by mandate.
39

Write the first skills — from observed friction only

Ongoing

Domain conventions, FHIR modelling, whatever the pilot team actually kept re-explaining. Not what you predicted they'd need.

Why wait: skills written from imagination are the ones nobody uses. A month of observation gives you the real list, and skills load on demand so they cost nothing when unused.
Stage 4 · As risk arrives

Expand and enforce

Triggered by a second team, not by a date. Controls tighten because the risk profile changed, not because a quarter ended.

Move on when: roughly six months of incident history exists. Done when controls match this system's actual risk profile rather than a generic checklist.

40

Onboard teams two and three

Social, not mandated

Let the pilot team do the persuading. Make usage visible in PR comments — reviewers are a strong transmission vector.

Why phased: big-bang rollouts produce inconsistent configuration and erode trust early. Rollout is a behaviour-change exercise, not a deployment.
41

Ship the five hooks

Half a day

Written in Node, not bash, so they work identically on Windows and macOS. Each one named, so there is no ambiguity about what exists:

  • no-prod-data — blocks non-synthetic data sources
  • no-real-records — blocks anything resembling a real member record entering the repo
  • protect-main — blocks force-push and direct pushes to main
  • spec-required — blocks edits to Lane 3+ paths without an approved spec
  • require-reuse — blocks Lane 2+ edits when reuse.md is missing
Why only five, and why now: the failure mode isn't a security incident — it's a cage tight enough that people route around the tooling and you lose both productivity and visibility. Ship a small named set informed by what actually went wrong in the pilot, then tighten with evidence. Each must mirror a CI check, never replace one.
Why require-reuse belongs here rather than later: it's the hook that makes the reuse stage binding rather than advisory, and the reuse stage is the thing standing between you and the fifteenth button. Without it, stage 2 produces a document nobody is obliged to act on.
42

Add the conformance check and weekly drift sweep

Polyrepo only

Does this repo have an AGENTS.md, call the shared workflow, extend the shared lint config, declare an owning team, have CODEOWNERS? A scheduled job clones every repo, checks, and opens PRs fixing what it can.

Why: distribution gets standards out; detection tells you where they didn't land. A conformance dashboard does most of the work on its own — nobody wants to be the red row.
43

Report throughput and stability together

Never one without the other

Merged PRs and cycle time, alongside change failure rate, revert rate and two-week code churn. Plus review pickup time, cost per merged PR, and rejection rate per agent from telemetry.

Why paired: AI adoption reliably raises throughput while degrading stability — that's the documented default trajectory, not a hypothetical. Reporting throughput alone hides the cost. Two-week churn is the highest-value single signal and computable from git alone.
44

Name a spend owner and run the first monthly review

One named personRecurring

Usage data, cost per feature, quality signals, developer friction, and what to change in the configuration. Book it as a standing meeting.

One named person owns the total monthly bill — not just Claude, but CI minutes, cloud, and every subscription. They present one slide: total, per engineer, per merged feature, and what moved since last month.

Why a named owner and not a dashboard: Microsoft's failure was not overspending — they had the budget and their own study showing a 24% gain. It was that nobody was watching until the number was already indefensible. Per-user caps and per-stage budgets stop runaway incidents; only a person looking monthly catches a gradual drift, and drift is the shape this actually fails in.
Why it's also a rollout mechanism: a configuration that's right at ten people is wrong at thirty, and the only way to notice is to look on a schedule.
Stage 5 · Once there is history to learn from

The autonomous pipeline, and compounding

Only start once the review flow is working manually. Automating a process you haven't run by hand automates your misunderstanding of it.

Done when: headcount doubles and change failure rate doesn't move. That is the whole thesis, evidenced.

45

Declare the four bounds before writing any stage

Non-negotiableAn hour

Iteration cap, budget cap, a success function the agent can run and read itself, and an escalation path. Plus no-progress detection — hash each tool call with its arguments and abort when the same call repeats.

The budget cap is two numbers, and this is the hour to decide that. A per-stage cap does not bound a feature: implementation is capped per task, and three tasks puts the ceiling above the $150 the cost tab quotes. So the harness accumulates spend against a run identifier as well as per stage, and refuses to start a stage that would breach the run cap. Agreeing it now costs a sentence; retrofitting it means every stage already assumes the wrong thing.
Why before, not after: bounded agent loops have been measured using 90% fewer tokens with 525× less run-to-run variance. Agreeing the four bounds first is what lets the next step build them in once, structurally, instead of each stage remembering. When a cap is hit the run must stop or escalate, never keep trying.
46

Build agent-stage.yml — the shared stage harness

Build this before any stage2–3 days

One reusable CI job — a GitHub reusable workflow, a GitLab included template, or an Azure DevOps template — taking skill, model, effort, max-iterations, budget-usd and run-id as inputs. It enforces both caps, runs no-progress detection, emits cost telemetry tagged with the stage name, and escalates by commenting on the issue and applying stage:blocked. Every stage becomes a short block calling it.

effort is an input for the same reason model is. It is the per-request depth and cost dial, and a stage that names a model but inherits a default effort has left the largest lever untuned. Make it required rather than optional so nobody forgets — low for classification, medium for mechanical transformation, high for spec, design and review.
Why first: if stages are written individually, the bounds get implemented eleven times and eleven ways — and the one that gets skipped is the one that runs away. Building the harness first makes the caps structural rather than a thing each stage remembers. It's also the only way improving every stage stays a one-line change.
Do this with Claude
Write .github/workflows/agent-stage.yml — a reusable workflow
(workflow_call) that every pipeline stage calls.

Inputs: skill, model, effort (required), max-iterations, budget-usd,
run-id, tools (optional).

It must:
  1. Run anthropics/claude-code-action@v1 with the named skill,
     passing effort through.
  2. Stop at max-iterations. Stop at budget-usd. Whichever first.
  3. ALSO enforce a run-level budget: accumulate spend for this
     run-id across every stage that has already run, and refuse to
     start if this stage would breach the run cap. Per-stage caps
     alone do not bound a feature — implementation is capped per
     task, so three tasks exceeds the quoted per-feature ceiling.
  4. No-progress detection: hash each tool call with its arguments;
     abort if the same call repeats 3 times in a 10-call window.
  5. On any cap being hit: do NOT retry. Comment on the issue with
     the reason and the last output, apply label stage:blocked, exit
     non-zero.
  6. Emit OpenTelemetry with stage name, run-id and effort as
     attributes, so cost is attributable per stage AND per run.
  7. Declare permissions explicitly at job level. Take secrets by
     NAME (ANTHROPIC_API_KEY only) — never `secrets: inherit`, which
     hands every repository secret to a job running generated code.
  8. Never grant contents:write on main. Stages write to a feature
     branch and open a PR.

Then convert my existing stage 1 triage job to call it.
Verify — actually test the failure paths, they're the whole point. Set budget-usd: 0.01 and confirm the run stops and comments rather than continuing. Set max-iterations: 1 on a task needing more and confirm it escalates rather than retrying. Then test the run cap specifically: run three stages against one run-id with a run cap below their sum, and confirm the third refuses to start. And grep the workflow for inherit — it should not appear. A harness whose caps have never fired is a harness you are hoping works.
47

Wire stage 1: triage on new issues

Cheapest stage

Triggered by the ask:new label. Reads the issue, searches for prior art, proposes a lane, posts one comment. Cheap model, low effort, one iteration, read-only tools, $2 cap.

Why start here: fully reversible — the output is a comment. It's the safest place to learn how headless agents behave in CI before anything can write code.
And it is the right place to get the injection boundary right, because the blast radius is nil. This stage reads text a human wrote into an issue and hands it to a model. Wrap the issue body in a delimited block stating that its contents are data to analyse, never instructions to follow — and make sure the lane the agent proposes is not the lane that gets applied. Path-based routing sets the lane after the plan exists. If an issue body can talk the pipeline into Lane 1, every control downstream is optional.
48

Then wire the rest, in reversibility order

One at a timeWeeks, not days

Each stage runs shadowed first — producing its output for humans to compare against what they'd have done — and is only promoted once it's been right for a fortnight.

Order to automate, and what each one earns
NextStageWhy this order
1st2 · Reuse analysisWrites a file, blocks nothing yet. Cheap, and it starts paying immediately.
2nd4 · Design and 6 · DecompositionBoth produce documents a human reads next. Wrong output costs a re-run.
3rd8 · VerificationOnly adds findings — it can't merge anything. Strictly additive safety.
4th7 · ImplementationFirst stage that writes code. Gate 3 still catches everything, so the blast radius is a wasted review.
5th10 · StagingNobody real is using staging. Automate freely.
Last11 · ProductionOnly after the reversibility stack below is built and proven. Not before.
Why not all at once: the order is the reversibility rule applied to the build itself. Each stage is automated only once the one before it has been trustworthy for a fortnight, so if quality drops you know exactly which change caused it. Automating six stages in a sprint gives you six suspects and no signal.
49

Guard the test-modification failure

Specific and important

A PreToolUse hook denying edits to test files during implementation unless the task is tagged as a test task, plus a CI check that fails when a PR modifies both a test assertion and the code under test without a spec change justifying it.

Why: when an agent can't make a test pass, it will eventually consider changing the test. Rational given "make the tests pass"; catastrophic given what you meant.
50

Build the reversibility stack before automating deploy

The real work

The capability — the most important infrastructure requirement in this plan: a release can be reversed in seconds, automatically, on a metric the process can read. Plus feature flags decoupling deploy from release, and expand/contract changes so no deploy needs coordinating.

On Kubernetes: a progressive delivery controller running a canary against a metrics backend. On virtual machines or bare metal: blue/green behind the load balancer with an automated health check flipping the pool back — same guarantee, older technique, entirely adequate. On a managed platform: whatever its native traffic-shifting primitive is.

If this requirement cannot be met — no automated reversal, whatever the reason — then stage 11 stays a human gate and part of the automation argument goes with it. That is a legitimate outcome. It just needs deciding rather than discovering.

Why this order: autonomous deployment isn't about trusting the agent more — it's about making being wrong cheap enough that trust stops being the question. Reversal in seconds versus a paged engineer is an operational-maturity argument rather than an AI one, which is why it's the easier one to get approved.
51

Set up the autonomy ratchet

Governance

Publish each lane's autonomy level with a named owner, and set the promotion criteria before you need them. The five levels and their evidence thresholds →

Where to start and stop: Lane 1 reaches Levels 1–2 within weeks and Level 3 in about a quarter — the 90-day dwell at Level 2 is a deliberate gate, not friction to route around. Lane 4 — member data or clinical logic — should sit at Level 1 indefinitely, and that's correct rather than a failure. Never demote a lane silently after an incident; it's a published number, so a drop is a visible decision with a stated reason and a route back.
52

Build the golden set and test the pipeline nightly

Most skipped step

15–20 recorded past asks with known-good outcomes across all four lanes, including an ambiguous one, one needing a contract change, one that should be rejected as a duplicate, and one where escalating is the correct behaviour. Track spec fidelity, escape rate, false-finding rate, escalation rate and cost drift.

Why it matters: once agents run delivery, a prompt change is a production change. Editing the reviewer's instructions alters the quality gate on every PR in the company. Without a regression suite you find out from an incident. Agent definitions go through PR review and run against the golden set in CI, like any other code.
53

Register the pipeline as a non-human identity

Day one of the pipeline

Least-privilege scoping, credential rotation, full audit logging, and an entry in whatever identity register you keep.

Why: 82% of enterprises reportedly have AI agents their security teams didn't know existed. A pipeline with commit and deploy rights is a service account with production access — treat it like one before an auditor asks, not after.
Risk register

How this fails, and what the early warning looks like

Every one of these has happened to someone. The value is in the leading indicator — by the time the failure is obvious it's expensive.

Failure modes, signals, responses
FailureEarly warningResponse
Cost blows the budget Spend per active day trending up while merged PRs stay flat. Cache-read share of tokens falling. Check what's churning at the top of the context — a skill or instruction file changing mid-session invalidates the cache and you pay full price every turn. Then per-user caps, then conversations with the specific heavy users.
Review queue collapses PR pickup time rising; PR size distribution widening; work-in-progress climbing while merges stay flat. Tighten the PR size cap before adding reviewers. Move more baseline checks off human eyes. Do not add parallelism — it multiplies input to the constraint.
Quality erodes invisibly Two-week churn rising. Duplication ratio rising. Revert rate up while throughput looks great. This is the documented default trajectory, not bad luck. Raise the lane of whatever's churning, and check whether reviewers are approving without reading — a pre-reviewed diff makes rubber-stamping easy.
Developers route around the tooling Hook override comments appearing frequently. People disabling hooks locally. Usage dropping among strong engineers specifically. You've built a cage. Remove hooks rather than adding them, and find out what was blocked that shouldn't have been. Losing visibility is worse than the risk you were guarding.
Senior engineers reject it Adoption strong among juniors, near-zero retention at senior level. Expected, and matches the evidence — experts on familiar code were measured slower. Don't mandate. Point them at unfamiliar surfaces and review load instead, where the gains actually are.
Standards drift across repos Conformance dashboard reddening. The same paragraph appearing in three AGENTS.md files. Anything true of more than one repo belongs in the plugin. Run the drift sweep as a campaign and fix it in one pass.
The spec gate becomes a rubber stamp Gherkin scenarios written in technical language. Product approving within minutes, never asking for changes. The gate is already dead — fix the language, not the process. If Product can't evaluate it, they aren't reviewing it, and you've lost the single cheapest quality intervention you have.
Agent weakens tests to pass PRs modifying both a test assertion and the code under test. Coverage flat while features ship. The guard hook and CI check should catch this. If it's getting through, the success function is wrong — the agent is optimising what you measured rather than what you meant.
Pipeline runs away Escalation rate climbing. Same stage repeatedly hitting its cap. Cost per run rising for unchanged inputs. Almost always a spec-quality problem rather than a code problem — three failed fix cycles usually means the requirement was ambiguous. Check the golden set for regression before touching the caps.
An irreversible data change ships A migration PR without a contract-phase label. DROP or RENAME appearing outside a contract release. Rollback drills failing at the database step. The most dangerous failure here, because it silently voids the whole reversibility argument. The canary reports healthy — nothing was reading the dropped column yet — and rollback then can't start the previous version. Rehearse a rollback against a restored snapshot quarterly; a rollback path nobody has run is a hypothesis.
Accessibility fails late axe-core violations suppressed rather than fixed. data-testid locators rising as a share of the suite. Any member-facing flow shipping without keyboard navigation tested. Fix the suppressions before adding features — accessibility rework changes markup structure, so cost grows with every component built on top. A rising data-testid share is the leading indicator, because it means elements can no longer be found by role.
The two languages drift apart A rule appears in the ESLint preset with no counterpart in Directory.Build.props. The parity check is disabled or has been failing so long nobody reads it. Duplication metrics look fine on each side separately. The specific failure this stack is exposed to, and it is silent. Fix the parity check before anything else — a standard wired on one side only is worse than no standard, because it reports as adopted. Then look for the duplicated helpers: same purpose, one in each language, neither aware of the other. No tool catches those, so it is a standing agenda item at the retro rather than a check.
Nobody can explain what's automated Disagreement about whether a lane is gated. Autonomy levels not written down anywhere current. Publish the levels with a named owner per lane. A silent demotion after an incident is how this erodes — make every change visible with a stated reason and a route back.
The one that ends programmes

Look again at the first row. Microsoft had excellent productivity data — their own study measured +24% merged PRs, sustained over four months, and adoption hit 84–95%. They cancelled anyway, for 5,000 engineers, because nobody could defend $500–2,000 per engineer per month against a budget set before anyone knew the number.

Instrument the value side with the same rigour as the spend side, from week one. Cost per merged PR is the number that answers the question you'll actually be asked.

Deliberately excluded

What not to build yet

Defer until the pain is real
ThingWait until
Managed settings and MDMYou have an endpoint estate, or a compliance requirement forces it. Below that it's overhead that reads as distrust.
Worktree parallelismReview throughput demonstrably has headroom. Running 4–8 agent sessions multiplies input to the bottleneck.
Internal MCP serverPast roughly eight services. Before that the service catalogue file does the job.
Paid observabilityFree tiers cover you well past current size.
GitHub Secret Protection & Code SecurityA customer security questionnaire names them. At ~$50/committer/month combined this is the largest optional line — gitleaks plus the security-reviewer agent covers the same ground until someone contractually requires the branded product. Selling to health plans, someone eventually will.
LaunchDarkly or a paid flag serviceFlag governance becomes someone's actual job. Because we adopt the OpenFeature standard rather than a vendor, switching later is a config change.
PactFlowPast roughly five teams. Its value is cross-team governance dashboards; below that the OSS Pact Broker is the same thing without the bill.
Nx Cloud paid tierCI wait time becomes a complaint. This is the one most likely to be worth buying first — it converts directly into CI minutes and engineer waiting time.
A second FHIR implementationNever. Health Data Services plus the Firely SDK is the answer; hand-rolling FHIR resource handling because a library felt heavy is the most expensive shortcut available on this stack.
Claude Fable 5Not now, and possibly never. Priced above the Opus tier for marginal gain on well-specified work — the spec chain does the job a larger model would be paid to guess at. And it requires 30-day retention, so it cannot run under the Zero Data Retention this plan needs for BAA coverage. Revisit only if both the pricing and the retention position change.
Claude through Azure FoundryNever, despite being the tidy-looking choice on an Azure estate. Costs the Batch API's 50% saving and task budgets, and buys back nothing — inference routes to Anthropic's servers regardless of region.
Kubernetes / AKSUntil Container Apps demonstrably cannot do something we need. AKS is a platform team we do not have, and the one capability people reach for it for — traffic-split canary — Container Apps already has.
Spec ceremony on Lanes 1–2Never. That's the point of lanes.
Role-based agent teamsNever, for coding. Optimises 0.5% of failures at 15× the token cost.
A separate review lane for AI codeNever. Unenforceable, and within a year everything is agent-assisted.

If only three things get done

  • One owning team per repo. Free now, brutal later.
  • Shared CI workflow before the first copy-paste. The moment one gets pasted, drift begins and never stops.
  • Cost controls before the first seat. The best-documented failure in this space is an organisation that measured a real productivity gain and cancelled anyway.
